II.4.1
Implications for Cosmology

We’d like to note here that the dSChas particularly strong implications for cosmology, while the theoretical motivation for the dSCis comparatively weak, as we discuss in
sections

II.4.2
,
II.4.2
and
II.4.2
. Most importantly, there are no proven no-go theorems against fully stable dSsolutions.
Furthermore, the dSCis supposed to hold in the asymptotic region of scalar field space, yet cosmology happens in the bulk. We will see that a plethora of models are in tension with the
\order{1}
values of
\mathfrak{s}_{1,2}
, yet would be compatible with
\mathfrak{s}_{1,2}\lesssim\order{0.1}
.
Following our spirit
it is easier to loosen an overly tight bound than to tighten a loose model
, we will take the dSCat face value and apply the scalar field boundary constraints in the bulk. Therefore, we are overly cautious.

Axions

with a potential of the form

V\sim 1-\cos\left(\phi/f\right)

(16)

face constraints, in particular around the maximum of the potential.
Smaller values of
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
increase the range of compatibility for the values of
\phi/f
.
Furthermore, the field displacement is restricted to be sub-Planckian by the DC,
while the axion decay constant
f
is restricted to be sub-Planckian by the WGC.
To satisfy all three conjectures simultaneously might require fine-tuning
Ibe et al. 2019
or a dynamical mechanism
Reig 2021
. For example,
Gasparotto and Obata 2022
use the birefringence
52
52

52

Cosmic birefringence, i.e. the rotation of linearly polarised light caused by a phase velocity difference between left- and right-handed photons, is difficult to measure, as the calibration uncertainty of Planck’s polarimeter (
\delta\beta\approx$0.28\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}$
) is of the same order as the measured effect (
$0.3\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}$\sim$0.45\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}$
), which does not yet take into account systematics such as foreground dust
Gasparotto and Obata 2022
;
Eskilt and Komatsu 2022
;
Diego-Palazuelos et al. 2022
;
Sullivan et al. 2025
;
Mohammadi et al. 2023
;
Sherwin and Namikawa 2023
;
Namikawa 2021
;
Clark et al. 2021
;
Nagata and Namikawa 2021
;
Minami and Komatsu 2020
;
Minami et al. 2019
;
Komatsu 2022
.

angle in the Planck 2018 data set to constrain the axion decay constant to
f\lesssim${10}^{16}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
, if the axion plays the role of DEand has a monodromy potential.
53
53

53

This constraint rules out the axi-Higgs proposal
Fung et al. 2021
, which requires a higher decay constant, yet leaves a window of compatibility for an Early Dark Energy (EDE)proposal where the axion couples to the photon
Fujita et al. 2021
.

While this bound might satisfy the WGC, the dSCis violated for their model, as they derive an upper bound of
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\lesssim$4\text{\times}{10}^{-8}$
, which is far below the expected
\order{1}
value.

Cicoli et al. 2019
apply the dSCto an axion model, and we summarise their findings in the following:
Axions as DEin the form of quintessence come with the benefit that the shift symmetry naturally prevents large quantum corrections to the potential. Moreover, ultra-light axions in the form of pseudo-scalars evade fifth-force constraints. Axions become ultra-light if the corresponding saxions (
\varphi
) receive large mass contributions from perturbative effects, as
m_{a}\sim\exp(-\varphi)
. A rather general form of an axion potential is

V=\Lambda_{\text{cc}}^{4}-\sum_{i=1}^{N_{a}}\Lambda_{i}^{4}\cos\left(\frac{a_{i}}{f_{i}}\right)+\dots,

(17)

with
\Lambda_{\text{cc}}
the cosmological constant,

N_{a}
the number of ultra-light axions,

\Lambda_{i}
the scale of the non-perturbative effects that gives rise to the axion,

a_{i}
the field value of the
i
-th axion,

f_{i}
the axion decay constant,
and the ellipsis indication that this is only the leading order of the non-perturbative potential.
The potential has a minimum at
\expectationvalue{V}=\Lambda_{\text{cc}}^{4}-\sum_{i}\Lambda_{i}^{4}
, an inflection point at
\Lambda_{\text{cc}}^{4}
, a maximum at
\Lambda_{\text{cc}}^{4}+\sum_{i}\Lambda_{i}^{4}
, and
2^{N_{a}-1}
saddle points.
The large Hubble friction freezes every axion into its field value after inflation, until the Hubble scale drops below the mass scale and the axion starts to roll and oscillate around its minimum.
Even though all axions in the theory are ultra-light, one will be the lightest and all others can be integrated out, which yields a slow-roll condition that depends only on the lightest axion,
a_{\textnormal{l}}
:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{V}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{(\nabla V)^{2}}{V^{2}}

(18)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\frac{\Lambda_{\textnormal{l}}}{\Lambda_{\text{cc}}}\right)^{4}\frac{1}{f_{\textnormal{l}}}\right]^{2}\frac{\sin^{2}\left(\frac{a_{\textnormal{l}}}{f_{\textnormal{l}}}\right)}{\left(1-\left(\frac{\Lambda_{\textnormal{l}}}{\Lambda_{\text{cc}}}\right)^{4}\cos(\frac{a_\textnormal{l}}{f_\textnormal{l}})\right)^{2}}

(19)

\displaystyle<1.

(20)

Choosing different values for the model parameters leads to different verdicts about the axion models:
54
54

54

See also
section

II.19.1
on axions and their WGC-compatibility.

Alignment

occurs when the minimum of the potential vanishes, i.e.
\Lambda_{\text{cc}}=\Lambda_{\textnormal{l}}
, which requires a super-Planckian axion decay constant, which violates the DC, unless it is achieved as an effective decay constant in assisted quintessence or through other multi-field mechanisms
Cicoli et al. 2019
;
Kim et al. 2005
;
Shiu et al. 2015a
;
Dimopoulos et al. 2005
;
Cicoli et al. 2014
;
Reig 2021
.

Hilltop quintessence

occurs if the region of the potential around the maximum is at a positive energy level and the field starts close to the maximum. The expectation is that, given a high enough number of axion fields, one will be around the maximum after inflation, as it is easy to displace the ultra-light fields from their minimum. However, even if that happens, it needs fine-tuning to get enough
e
-foldings while keeping the axion decay constant sub-Planckian. Hilltop quintessence violates the WGCand
eq.

12
, but not the refined dSC.
Similar results with an axion close to the hilltop were derived in a KKLTscenario: in order to be compatible with the dSC, fine-tuning, especially about the initial conditions, is necessary. Interestingly, if the fine-tuning takes place and
eq.

13
is satisfied,
eq.

12
is satisfied away from the saddle
Emelin and Tatar 2019
.

Also
Cicoli et al. 2022a
find that axionic quintessence hilltop inflation requires very finely tuned initial conditions, as well as a very low inflationary scale of
H_{\textnormal{I}}\lesssim$1\text{\,}\mathrm{MeV}$
.

Quasi-natural quintessence

occurs if the minimum of the potential corresponds to
\Lambda_{\text{cc}}
, which yields a slow-roll condition of
f_{\textnormal{l}}\gtrsim\left(\Lambda_{\textnormal{l}}/\Lambda_{\text{cc}}\right)^{4}
that is satisfied as long as
\Lambda_{\text{cc}}\gg\Lambda_{\textnormal{l}}
. Quasi-natural quintessence violates the dSC.

Oscillating scalars

occur when
m_{a}\simeq H
. The field oscillates around its minimum, which violates the dSC.

Boson stars

are found to be compatible with the dSC
Choi et al. 2019
as long as they are stable
Herdeiro et al. 2019
. Unstable boson star solutions belong to the swampland.

For a representation of DEas a fluid with pressure
p
and energy density
\rho
, parametrised by
\omega
, the EoSreads
p=\omega\rho
.
Kopeikin and Petrov 2014
stress that the representation of a scalar field as a fluid is a rather formal one — not all thermodynamic properties of an ideal fluid are fulfilled by a scalar field, e.g. the sound speed is capped at the speed of light, irrespective of the formal prediction using the EoS.

The
\Lambda
Cold Dark Matter (
\Lambda
CDM)model satisfies those constraints, but it is incompatible with the dSC, as
\nabla V/V=0\ngtr 0
for a cosmological constant
Agrawal et al. 2018a
;
Colgáin 2019
.
To explain the observed accelerated expansion of our Universe with
V>0
and
\nabla V\neq 0
, DEmust be dynamical, i.e. the EoSis time-dependent
Palti 2019
;
Agrawal et al. 2018a
.
Current cosmological observations give us some boundaries for the EoSparameter
\omega

\omega

1+\omega\gtrsim 0.15\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}
should hold to be compatible with the dSCwhile avoiding a suppression of LSSgrowth
Agrawal et al. 2018a

directly through

Palti 2019
.

The compatibility between the dSCand observations is discussed using model-independent reconstructions:

•

Arjona and Nesseris 2021
find the dSCcompatible with low redshift data.

•

A parametrisation-dependent Bayesian machine learning algorithm that reconstructs
H(z)=H_{0}+H_{1}z^{2}/\left(1+z\right)
finds that
eq.

12
is respected while
eq.

13
is violated
Elizalde and Khurshudyan 2021
.
57

The DCis satisfied. However, the model shows a sign switch in the DEEoSin the redshift range
z\in\left[0,\,5\right]
, i.e. DEis in the phantom regime for some time.

•

The model-independent Gaussian process used by
Khurshudyan 2023
shows a tension between reconstructed DEand the dSCin the low redshift range
z\in\left[0,1\right]
.
58
58

58

Furthermore, the relative DMenergy density is strongly dependent on the used kernel, ranging from
\Omega_{\textnormal{DM}}\simeq$0.262(0.011)$
to
\Omega_{\textnormal{DM}}\simeq$0.293(0.013)$
, while the relative energy density of radiation ranges from
\Omega_{\gamma}\simeq$0.00013(0.00002)$
to
\Omega_{\gamma}\simeq$0.00023(0.00002)$
.

•

The claim by
Akrami et al. 2019
that DEmodels with
\mathfrak{s}_{1}>1
are ruled out with
3\sigma
by observational constraints is not supported by a model-independent analysis of DESIdata
Arjona and Nesseris 2024
.
59
59

59

Concretely,
Akrami et al. 2019
test models of the form
V(\phi)\sim\exp(-\mathfrak{s}_1\phi)
, for which they find
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\leq 1.02
is needed to be compatible with observational constraints. They don’t find cosmologically viable solutions for models with double exponential potentials in M-theory compactifications, for the
O(16)\crossproduct O(16)
heterotic string, and for type II string theory. Observed issues with those models stem from unknown quantum corrections, decompactification of extra-dimensions in cosmologically relevant regions of the parameter space, and fifth forces that are incompatible with the WGC.

That contradicting verdicts are derived is predicted by
Schöneberg et al. 2023
. They identify two major pitfalls of model-independent analyses: On the one hand, the smoothness of the expansion history of the Universe has to be modelled, and the appropriate number of derivatives has to be taken into account. This emulation can differ. On the other hand, it is not trivial to enforce positivity of kinetic terms and field energy density. The results depend on the used method to achieve these. The two pitfalls together can lead to different conclusions.

Concrete models are for example assessed by
Schöneberg et al. 2023
, who find several explicit DEpotentials that are in at least slight tension with the dSC, or
Freigang et al. 2023
, who find it challenging to fulfil the dSC, even in multi–scalar field settings. However, they
Freigang et al. 2023
conclude that having a large
\eta_{V}
parameter makes it easier to satisfy the dSC.
In the following, we make some remarks about various models that are constrained by the dSC:

•

In analogy to warm inflation (see
section

II.4.1
),
Dall’Agata et al. 2020
present warm DE, where an axion field is slow-rolling down a steep potential while experiencing friction induced by a coupling to a U(1) gauge field. Due to the additional friction term in the EoM, the potential can satisfy the dSC, while the observations can be matched by a suitable choice for the axion decay constant
f

Dall’Agata et al. 2020
:

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\int\!\sqrt{-g}\left(-\frac{\left(\partial\phi\right)^{2}}{2}-V-\frac{F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}}{4}\right.

\displaystyle\phantom{\ =\int\!\sqrt{-g}\left(\right.}\left.-\frac{\phi}{4f}F_{\mu\nu}\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\right)\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x

(21)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tau}^{2}\phi+\frac{2}{a}\frac{\partial a}{\partial\tau}\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial\tau}+a^{2}\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi}-\frac{a^{2}}{f}\vec{E}\cdot\vec{B}

(22)

\displaystyle\vec{E}\cdot\vec{B}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}a^{4}}\int\!k^{3}\partial_{\tau}\absolutevalue{A}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}k.

(23)

The quantities have their usual meaning:
F_{\mu\nu}
is the gauge field strength tensor,
\tilde{F}_{\mu\nu}
its dual,
a
is the scale factor,
\tau
the conformal time, and
k
is the comoving momentum of the gauge field
A
.
The model is also studied by
Papageorgiou 2020
and found to satisfy the dSCand observational constraints, including Gravitational Wave (GW)production with a frequency of
{10}^{-16}\text{\,}\mathrm{Hz}
.

•

Warm DEcoupled to a non-Abelian SU(2) field is studied by
Papageorgiou 2020
and found to satisfy the dSCin a range compatible with observations. While in warm U(1) DEthe additional friction is caused by tachyon production and backreactions of the gauge field, in warm SU(2) DE, the friction is classical. This model shares similarities with chromonatural inflation (see
sections

II.17.1
and
II.19.1
).

•

Chameleon models studied by
Casas et al. 2024b
enable short phases of up to one
e
-fold of accelerated expansion while keeping the field excursion sub-Planckian if heavy states are present that stabilise the scalar potential during that time. The density of the heavy states is diluted by the expansion, which limits the lifetime of this phase. They highlight that there are significant obstacles to implement such models within string theory, as these requires a flat region in the otherwise steep potential.

In chameleon models studied by
Brax et al. 2020
, where DEis coupled to matter such that

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle=A(\phi)m_{0}

(24)

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle=V(\phi)+\omega\phi\rho_{\textnormal{eff}}

(25)

\displaystyle V(\phi)

\displaystyle=\left(1-\omega_{\phi}\right)\frac{\phi_{\textnormal{eff}}}{2}-\left(A-1\right)\frac{\rho}{2}

(26)

\displaystyle V_{\textnormal{eff}}

\displaystyle=V(\phi)+\left(A(\phi)-1\right)\rho_{\textnormal{m}},

(27)

the coupling to matter

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=M_{\textnormal{P}}\frac{\partial\log A}{\partial\phi}

(28)

is bounded by the dSCto

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathfrak{s}_{1}\left(1-\omega_{\phi}\right)}{2A_{\textnormal{max}}}\frac{\rho_{\textnormal{eff}}}{\rho}-\mathfrak{s}_{1}\frac{\Delta A}{2A_{\textnormal{max}}},

(29)

where
A_{\textnormal{max}}
is the maximal value
A
can take, either because the function has a maximum or because the field displacement is capped by the DC.
For a DE-dominated universe with
\rho_{\textnormal{eff}}/\rho=\Omega_{\Lambda}/\Omega_{\textnormal{m}}
,
very small field displacement,
and
\omega_{\phi}\approx-1
,
they find that

\beta\gtrsim\order{1}
, i.e. another
\order{1}
bound related to the dSCand the DC. This puts such chameleon models with below-unity DE–matter coupling and sub-Hubbleian field mass in the swampland
Brax et al. 2020
.
The observed absence of fifth-forces puts an upper bound on the coupling constant of
\beta\lesssim${10}^{4}$

Brax et al. 2020
.
Some concrete models with screened scalar-interactions in scalar–tensor theories are the following
Brax et al. 2020
:

–

Chameleon models track the minimum of an effective potential for most of cosmic history. To be compatible with fifth-force and swampland constraints, chameleon fields must vanish in the future, as either large couplings
\beta
are required, which would be in contradictions with the observed smallness/absence of fifth forces, or super-Planckian field excursions, which would be in contradiction with the DC.

–

Symmetron models have Higgs-like potentials of the form
V(\phi)=V_{0}-\mu^{2}\phi^{2}/2+\mathfrak{c}\phi^{4}/4
and a coupling to matter of the form
A(\phi)=1+\phi^{2}/m^{2}
respectively
\beta\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=M_{\textnormal{P}}\frac{\partial\log A}{\partial\phi}=\phi M_{\textnormal{P}}/m
with the vacuum value constraint by the dSCto be
\beta_{0}\geq\mathfrak{s}_{1}\Omega_{\Lambda,0}/\Omega_{\textnormal{m},0}
. To be compatible with fifth-force and swampland constraints, scalar fields must vanish in the future to satisfy
\phi(\rho)\rho/m^{2}\geq\mathfrak{s}_{1}V(\phi)/M_{\textnormal{P}}
.

–

Dilaton models, where a dilaton tracks the minimum of the effective potential, have potentials of the form
V_{0}\exp(-\phi)+\order{\exp(-2\phi)}
, and couplings
\beta\gtrsim\mathfrak{s}_{1}\rho_{\Lambda}/\left(\rho+4\rho_{\Lambda}\right)
respectively coupling functions
A=l_{\textnormal{s}}\exp\left(\Psi(\phi)\right)/\sqrt{8\pi G_{\textnormal{N}}}
with
l_{\textnormal{s}}
the string scale and
\Psi(\phi)
the matter field. Such models have a patch in parameter space that is compatible with observational constraints, the dSC, and the DC.
60
60

60

DEdescribed by strongly coupled dilatons can be eternal
Brax et al. 2010
.

•

A
f(R)=R-\mathfrak{p}R^{k}
DEmodel with

is found to yield
V^{\prime}/V=0
around the minimum, which renders it incompatible with the dSC

.

•

A holographic model of emergent DE, where DEis a holographic fluid on the 4d Friedmann–Lemaître–Robertson–Walker (FLRW)hypersurface in a 5d Minkowski bulk, is found to be compatible with Supernova (SN)Ia and
H(z)
data as well as the dSCup to the present epoch, but will violate the dSCin the future
Cai et al. 2019
.

the Hubble parameter,

\phi
the quintessence field,

V(\phi)
its potential,
prime denoting a derivative with respect to the scalar field and
an overdot a derivative with respect to time,
and the Christoffel symbol in the covariant derivative is with respect to the field space metric
G_{ab}
.

To assess the viability of quintessence models in the light of the dSC, several aspects have to be considered:

Field value

The model could satisfy
eq.

12
for some part of the field trajectory and
eq.

13
for another part, but the parts could be disconnected, restricting the allowed field ranges and values.

dSCparameters

Even for field values that are theoretically allowed, observational data might put bounds on the parameters
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
.

Other swampland conjectures

The field range is restricted to remain sub-Planckian by the DC. The dSCparameter is restricted by the TCC.
62
62

62

See
section

II.4.2
for the TCCbound
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\geq 2/\sqrt{\left(d-1\right)\left(d-2\right)}
and other bounds.

The second Friedmann equation for a FLRWuniverse

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{a}}{a}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}\left(2\rho_{\textnormal{r}}+\rho_{\textnormal{m}}+\rho_{\phi}+3p_{\phi}\right)

(30)

\displaystyle p_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\dot{\phi}^{2}-V

(31)

indicates that the potential energy of quintessence has to dominate over the other energy components of the Universe to yield accelerated expansion
Barrau et al. 2021
.
Models that assure a late-time dominance relatively independent of initial conditions are
freezing models,
63
63

63

An example of a freezing model is the Ratra–Peebles potential
V=\Lambda^{4+\mathfrak{p}}\phi^{-\mathfrak{p}}
, with
\Lambda
some energy scale and
\mathfrak{p}>0

Barrau et al. 2021
;
Ratra and Peebles 1988
.
However, such models are disfavoured by the dSC
Tosone et al. 2019
and violate the dSCin the infinite future when
V^{\prime}/V\rightarrow 0

Barrau et al. 2021
.
Another example of a freezing model is an exponential potential of the form
V\sim\exp\left(-\mathfrak{c}\phi\right)
, with
\mathfrak{c}
a constant
Barrau et al. 2021
;
Ferreira and Joyce 1998
.

where the motion of the field slows down at late times when the potential becomes flat, and
thawing models,
64
64

64

Thawing models, where a field is initially damped by Hubble friction to
\dot{\phi}_{\textnormal{i}}=0
and
\omega
is increasing over time with
\omega_{\textnormal{i}}\approx-1

Storm and Scherrer 2020
, typically have potentials
V=V_{0}\cos\left(\phi/\mathfrak{c}\right)
or
V=V_{0}\left(1+\cos\left(\phi/\mathfrak{c}\right)\right)

Barrau et al. 2021
;
Frieman 1995
, and satisfy either
eq.

12
or
eq.

13

Barrau et al. 2021
for the different values that
\phi
can take, i.e. thawing models are compatible with the dSCin general.

where the field is initially damped by Hubble friction
Barrau et al. 2021
.
In a model-agnostic reconstruction of quintessence from observational data, no preference for thawing or freezing models was derived; the data seems to indicate an oscillation of the EoSparameter around
w=-1

Park et al. 2021
.

Tada and Terada 2024
derive a thawing quintessence model respectively an axion-like quintessence by a reconstruction of DESI-data for
\omega_{0}\omega_{a}
CDM. Both potentials satisfy the dSC(as well as the DCand the WGC).

Simple quintessence models are often disfavoured by theoretical considerations
Hebecker et al. 2019a
or by observational constraints, especially if the DCis taken into account
Raveri et al. 2019
;
Akrami et al. 2019
;
Trivedi 2021
;
Ben-Dayan 2019
;
Barrau et al. 2021
;
Brinkmann et al. 2022

or
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\sim\sqrt{2}
from the asymptotic limit of string theories is demanded
Colgain and Yavartanoo 2019
;
Akrami et al. 2019
;
Raveri et al. 2019
;
Bhattacharya et al. 2024
;
Alestas et al. 2024
;
Park et al. 2021
;
Schöneberg et al. 2023
.
Many simple, exponential single-field quintessence models favour a small value of
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\sim${10}^{\order{\num{e-1}}}$\text{\ \textendash\ }${10}^{\order{\num{e-2}}}$
to satisfy the observational constraints, which is in tension with the dSCassumption of
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\sim\order{1}
.
For example,
Tosone et al. 2019
find
\nabla V/V\lesssim 0.31
for thawing quintessence with exponential potentials and
\nabla V/V\lesssim 0.54
for models with double exponential potentials, yet these bounds can be eased by using different observational data sets.
It should be noted that
eq.

13
is often not considered in the literature cited above.
The refined dSC
eq.

13
is, for example, satisfied by runaway quintessence, where a runaway scalar potential is frozen by Hubble friction near its hilltop maximum, constituting rolling quintessence
Olguin-Trejo et al. 2019
. Fifth forces are avoided if the modulus is localised in the extra dimensions, away from the SM
Olguin-Trejo et al. 2019
. The model can be tuned to have an EoSof
\omega=-1
in the past, from which it slowly deviates, i.e. it is currently increasing (meaning becoming less negative).

Emelin and Tatar 2019
find axion-like quintessence with a hilltop potential to satisfy the dSC: either the potential satisfies
eq.

12
at all times, yet violates the DC, or the potential has a saddle point, respectively, a local minimum in the axionic direction. In the latter case,
eq.

12
is satisfied except for the saddle point, but there
eq.

13
is satisfied. The DCis satisfied as well.

Focusing on
eq.

12
could be somewhat excused by the finding that the dSCin the form of
eq.

12
favours higher values of
H_{0}
, whereas the refined conjecture in the form of
eq.

13
favours models that lead to a lower value of the Hubble parameter (compared to the
\Lambda
CDMmodel), at least for the generic quintessence models
Banerjee et al. 2021a
studied. To solve the Hubble crisis, the former would be required.
However, it might be the case that single-field quintessence is not able to resolve the Hubble crisis, unless there is a non-minimal coupling
Banerjee et al. 2021a
. This seems to be a more general finding: A coupling is actually expected, unless it is broken by a symmetry
Carroll 1998
.
Although
Yang 2020
warn that the available observational data might be insufficient to make a statement about the refined dSC
eq.

13
, coupled quintessence is a viable Ansatz to satisfy both dSCcriteria (
eqs.

12
and
13
)
Mishra et al. 2023
:

Bruck and Thomas 2019
;
Baldes et al. 2019
find that introducing a coupling helps to alleviate tensions regarding the dSCand the DCfor DEmodels, and
a model-independent reconstruction by
Yang 2020
finds coupled quintessence with DEcoupled to DMcompatible with
eq.

12
, and presents an upper bound of
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\lesssim 4.44
at
z=0
with 95% significance (the uncoupled case of quintessence yields
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\lesssim 1.23
in the same study).

Besides introducing a coupling, also more complicated fields or multi-field settings can satisfy the dSCconstraints
Payeur et al. 2024
;
Akrami et al. 2021
;
Garg and Krishnan 2019
;
Eskilt et al. 2022
;
Cicoli et al. 2020a
;
Gallego et al. 2024
.
65
65

65

However, as
Brinkmann et al. 2022
discuss, multi-field quintessence cannot start in a matter-dominated epoch and match the current DEcontribution. If
\Omega_{\textnormal{DE}}\simeq 0.7
and
\omega_{\textnormal{de}}\simeq-1
are to be matched, the quintessence fields need to start in an epoch of kinetic domination.

Multi-field models can circumvent the constraints for instance by keeping one field near an extremum while the other field rolls down a steep potential (such that the gradient is of
\order{1}
in field units)
Ben-Dayan 2019
.

In multi-field quintessence, similar considerations regarding the turning rate of the fields apply as presented and referred to around
eq.

87
: a non-geodesic motion through a curved field space allows the individual fields to not be slow-rolling, i.e. to have a steep potential that satisfies the dSC, while at the same time showing a flat effective potential, which satisfies the observational preference for slow-rolling.
Cicoli et al. 2020b
study multi-field quintessence regarding this aspect.
Payeur et al. 2024
study rapid-turn DE, a multi-field quintessence model inspired by hyperinflation, and claim that there is a small patch in the parameter space that satisfies observational constraints as well as the dSC, the DC, and the TCC.
Turning allows a two-field model studied by
Eskilt et al. 2022
;
Akrami et al. 2021
with a scalar field space metric of the form
G_{ab}=\text{diag}\left(1,f(r)\right)
, with
f(r)
a function depending on the radial field
r
,
66
66

66

The model assumes a field space metric of the form
\mathrm{d}s^{2}=\mathrm{d}r^{2}+f(r,\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}
and the scalars
\phi^{a}=(r,\theta)
. The polar coordinates
r
and
\theta
are coordinates in the field space, not in the physical space.

to realise cosmic acceleration that effectively resembles a cosmological constant while satisfying the dSC:
The action, Friedmann equation, and EoMare given by
sections

II.4.1
,
II.4.1
and
II.4.1
.
In this model, field space has strongly curved trajectories and the fields spin rapidly in field space. Even though the DEbackground resembles
\Lambda
CDM, there are differences that are potentially observable: spinning solutions enhance clustering on sub-Hubble scales.
67
67

67

A heavy mode present during inflation is usually suppressed
Akrami et al. 2021
;
Achúcarro et al. 2012
. Yet in the late Universe, the heavy mode could enhance clustering and affect the DMdistribution due to a tachyonic instability
Akrami et al. 2021
.
In this case though, the main mechanism for clustering comes from the light mode and is as follows
Akrami et al. 2021
: The spinning of the fields reduces the sound speed, as the turning rate
\mathfrak{T}
alters the dispersion relation such that
c_{\textnormal{s}}=1+4a^{2}\mathfrak{T}^{2}/m_{\phi}^{2}
, where
m_{\phi}^{2}=a^{2}n^{a}n^{b}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}V-a^{2}\mathfrak{T}^{2}+R\left(\partial_{\tau}\phi\right)^{2}/2
is an effective mass describing the DEperturbations depending on the covariant derivative (
\nabla_{a}
) in the normal direction (
n^{a}
) of the potential
V
, the scale factor
a
, the Ricci scalar
R
, and the derivative with respect to conformal time of the scalar field
\partial_{\tau}\phi
.
With a lower speed of sound, the Jeans scale becomes sub-horizon and the clustering becomes observable
Akrami et al. 2021
;
Creminelli et al. 2010a
;
Creminelli et al. 2009
;
Takada 2006
;
Hu and Scranton 2004
.

Such a model can simultaneously satisfy the dSCand observational constraints on slow-rolling, as the potential can be arbitrarily steep while the effective slow-roll parameter remains small (see also our later discussion in
section

II.4.1
):

\displaystyle\frac{\absolutevalue{\nabla V}}{V}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{G^{ab}V_{a}V_{b}}}{V}

\displaystyle\geq\mathfrak{s}_{1}

(32)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\epsilon_{V}}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\sqrt{G^{ab}V_{a}V_{b}}}{V}

(33)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{V}\Omega_{\phi}\left(1+\frac{\mathfrak{T}^{2}}{9H^{2}}\right)^{-1}

(34)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\nabla_t\vec{T}}

(35)

\displaystyle T^{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{\dot{\phi}^{a}}{\dot{\phi}}

(36)

where we introduced the effective DEslow-roll parameter
\epsilon_{\phi}=\frac{3}{2}\left(w_{\phi}+1\right)=\frac{3}{2}\dot{\phi}^{2}/\left(\frac{1}{2}\dot{\phi}^{2}+V\right)
with
\dot{\phi}^{2}=G_{ab}\dot{\phi}^{a}\dot{\phi}^{b}

and the turning rate
\mathfrak{T}
that depends on the normalised tangent vector to the field-space trajectory
\vec{T}
,
68
68

68

More details on the turning rate are presented in
section

II.4.1
.

and used the relative energy density of DE
\Omega_{\phi}=\rho_{\phi}/\rho_{\textnormal{crit}}\approx V/\left(3M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}H^{2}\right)
.
69
69

69

In
section

II.4.1
, we omit
\Omega_{\textnormal{Inflaton}}=1
, since no other energy component is present during inflation.
For DEwe see that additional energy components further suppress the rolling-rate of the scalar field, which explains the observed value of
\epsilon_{\phi}\ll 1

Akrami et al. 2021
.

We see that the field can be effectively slow-rolling (
\epsilon_{\phi}\ll 1
), in accordance with observations, while the potential is steep (
\sqrt{\epsilon_{V}}\sim\order{1}
), in accordance with the dSC.

Besides couplings and multi-field settings, also modified gravity or higher-dimensional theories allow for dSC-satisfying quintessence models:

Li et al. 2020a
state that quintessence can be realised in a dSC-compatible way if an AdSvacua is uplifted by frozen, large-scale Lorentz-violation achieved through a non-trivial Brans–Dicke coupling between the quintessence field and gravity.
Quintessence in a Horndeski setting with a cubic Galileon term
\left(\nabla\phi\right)^{2}\mathop{{}\Box}\nolimits\phi
in the Lagrangian is found to be compatible with observational constraints and the dSC
Brahma and Hossain 2019a
.
And quintessence in a supergravity setting is assessed by
Chiang and Murayama 2018
:
They propose a model with two separated sectors, a quintessence sector
\phi_{q}
and a hidden sector
\phi_{h}
. The Kähler and superpotential show the separation:

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=\phi_{h}^{*}\phi_{h}+\phi_{q}^{*}\phi_{q}

(37)

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=W_{0}(\phi_{h})+W_{1}(\phi_{q}).

(38)

The F-term scalar potential is given by

\displaystyle V_{F}

\displaystyle=e^{K/M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}\left(D_{i}WK^{i\bar{j}}D_{\bar{j}}W^{*}-\frac{3}{M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}\absolutevalue{W}^{2}\right)

(39)

\displaystyle D_{i}W

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial W}{\partial\phi_{i}}+\frac{W}{M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}\frac{\partial K}{\partial\phi_{i}},

(40)

and yields
V\supset m_{\textnormal{3/2}}^{2}\absolutevalue{\phi_q}^{2}
, since the gravitino
m_{\textnormal{3/2}}
is related to the superpotential
\expectationvalue{\abs{W_0}^2}\sim m_{\textnormal{3/2}}^{2}M_{\textnormal{P}}^{4}
.
There is a large scale separation between the Hubble scale and the gravitino, which causes the quintessence field to roll down its potential very early, which makes it indistinguishable from a cosmological constant. Since DESIdata suggests that DEis actually dynamical, this has to be broken, which can either be achieved by a shift symmetry in the quintessence sector or by sequestering the hidden sector. As
Chiang and Murayama 2018
show, the former leads to trans-Planckian field ranges (yet satisfies the dSC) and the latter violates the dSC(yet satisfies the DC), as fifth-force constraints require
\left(\absolutevalue{\nabla V}/V\right)^{2}\lesssim${10}^{-5}$
.

If a model — single-field or multi-field — satisfies all the constraints, it might do so only for finely chosen values of the free parameters of the model. The fine-tuning problems might be severe, as
Hertzberg et al. 2019
argue: Quintessence could receive quantum corrections from loop effects that are larger than the observed DEdensity, i.e. the field potential must be finely tuned to be
V\sim${10}^{-120}$M_{\textnormal{P}}^{4}

Kolda and Lyth 1999
;
Garny 2006
;
Choi 2000
. Furthermore, the field is supposed to be slow-rolling, which means that also
\absolutevalue{\nabla V}\sim${10}^{-120}$M_{\textnormal{P}}^{3}
has to be achieved. However, the double fine-tuning is only a
double
fine-tuning if
V
and
\nabla V
are not correlated in any way.
70
70

70

One example are conformally coupled scalars, where only the quantity
V/\absolutevalue{\nabla V}
matters, such that it is a single fine-tuning problem like in the case of the cosmological constant
Hertzberg et al. 2019
.
Admittedly, the conformal couplings might not be the best example to make this point, as such couplings invoke fifth forces which are severely constraint by observations and must be either screened, or the coupling must be only active in the dark sector
Hertzberg et al. 2019
. If it is the latter, there are still quantum corrections that could spoil the fine-tuning of the quintessence potential by coupling to the SM
Hertzberg et al. 2019
.

Another fine-tuning problem is mentioned by
Cicoli et al. 2019
: not only does it require fine-tuning to achieve
V(\phi_{0})=\Lambda_{\text{cc}}^{4}
with
\phi_{0}
today’s quintessence field value, moreover the quintessence field has to be extremely light,
m\simeq${10}^{-32}\text{\,}\mathrm{eV}$
.

A dSC-consistent proposal without fine-tuning for the cosmological constant, but instead for the Electro Weak (EW)scale, has been found in a model of Starobinsky inflation with non-minimal coupling to the Higgs field that thaws later into quintessence
Wang 2019
.
A quintessence–Higgs coupling has also been investigated by
Han et al. 2019
. For an exponential quintessence potential
V\sim\exp\left(-\mathfrak{c}\phi\right)
, they derive a lower bound of
\mathfrak{c}\gtrsim$0.35(0.05)$
, which supports
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\sim\order{1}
. They derive their bound by requiring that the EWvacuum is stable during inflation, taking into account observational data related to DEand matter energy density, the Higgs mass and vacuum expectation value, and the mass of the top quark.

As a final note on quintessence, we note that quintessence can play an important role in cyclic cosmology, where the universe undergoes repeated phases of expansion and contraction. If this is the case, the dSCputs strong constraints on the number of
e
-foldings per cycle in cyclic models:
Corianò and Frampton 2020
derive that the number of
e
-folds until the next contraction phase starts is
3\Delta\phi/2M_{\textnormal{P}}\Omega_{\phi,0}\mathfrak{s}_{1}\sim\order{1}
, while models of cyclic cosmology predict this number to be of
\order{100}
. The tension vanishes if the dSCallowed for
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\sim${10}^{\order{\num{e-2}}}$
or the DCallowed for
\Delta\phi/M_{\textnormal{P}}\sim\order{100}
. If the swampland conjectures remain strong in their current forms, this puts cyclic cosmology in this form in the swampland.

Slotheon DE
71
71

71

From
slow Galilean
DE, as the slotheon scalar field experiences friction, which makes it moving slower than its canonical counterpart
Adak et al. 2013
.

has an action that slightly differs from quintessence
Mukhopadhyay and Majumdar 2019
;
Mukhopadhyay et al. 2020
;
Germani et al. 2012
;
Adak et al. 2013
:

\displaystyle S=

\displaystyle\int\!\sqrt{-g}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left.\biggl(M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}R-V(\phi)\right.\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.\left.-\left(g^{\mu\nu}-\frac{G^{\mu\nu}}{\Lambda^{2}}\right)\nabla_{\mu}\phi\nabla_{\nu}\phi\right)\right]\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x,

(41)

where
G^{\mu\nu}
is the Einstein tensor,
\Lambda
is an energy scale, and the other terms have their usual meaning;
the coupling
\frac{G^{\mu\nu}}{\Lambda^{2}}\nabla_{\mu}\phi\nabla_{\nu}\phi
acts as an additional friction that slows the scalar field down.

Evidence supporting the slotheon model could present itself in the following form:

•

the small-scale structure growth is suppressed around the matter domination–DEdomination transition due to the additional friction term which modifies the EoM
Mukhopadhyay et al. 2020
;
Adak et al. 2013
,

•

the matter powerspectrum is suppressed at small wavenumbers, but the strength of the suppression declines for increasing wavenumbers
Adak et al. 2013
,

•

the growth rate
f\sigma_{8}
is lowered, and the redshift-space distortion could be observable, as the coupling modifies the Poisson equation
Mukhopadhyay et al. 2020
;
Adak et al. 2013
.

Considering a thawing field,
Mukhopadhyay and Majumdar 2019
found that the slotheon satisfies the dSCand is favoured over quintessence models with a single exponential potential by observational constraints. The slotheon model favours
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\approx 0.8
, while quintessence with a simple single exponential potential favours lower values, which causes tension with the dSC. As an aside, it is mentioned that the DCis satisfied and that
eq.

13
is violated.

Thermal DE

is characterised by the presence of temperature terms in the scalar field potential. The model is presented by
Hardy and Parameswaran 2020
and further discussed by
Bento et al. 2020
: Conceptually, such a potential is of the form
V\sim V_{0}+\phi^{2}T^{2}
. The temperature dependence can keep the scalar field near a false vacuum and does not require the potential to be flat. When the universe cools down, this mechanism ceases to be effective and the scalar field rolls to its true vacuum. The model makes several potentially observable predictions: The thermal support comes from a hidden sector, which changes
\Delta N_{\textnormal{eff}}
, which in turn affects BBNrespectively the CMB. The temperature dependence introduces a coupling between the hidden sector and the SM, which induces a fifth-force. Thermal phase transitions in the hidden sector might produce a stochastic GWbackground. Since the universe cools down over time, this is rather an EDEmodel. See our comments on EDEelsewhere in this review article regarding EDEconstraints and the feasibility of reducing the Hubble tension.

The compatibility between thermal DEand the dSCdepends on the chosen potential: While
Bento et al. 2020
claim that this model is compatible with swampland conjectures, and they even mention the dSCin a footnote, the potential they use might be in tension with the dSC:
For large scalar field values,
\absolutevalue{\nabla V}/V\sim 1/\phi
, and for small field values
\absolutevalue{\nabla V}/V\sim\phi
, i.e. in either case the ratio will be small. There is only a window of compatibility for intermediate field values, such that
\absolutevalue{\nabla V}/V\sim\order{1}
. However,
V/\rho_{\textnormal{DE}}\rightarrow 0
for intermediate field values (see figure 3 in the paper by
Bento et al. 2020
respectively figure 1 in the work by
Hardy and Parameswaran 2020
).
Yet, an alternative modulus-like zero temperature potential
V(\phi)=m_{\phi}^{2}\left(\phi-\phi_{1}\right)^{2}/2
suggested by
Hardy and Parameswaran 2020
is actually compatible with the dSC.

Pole DE

Linder 2020
presents an Ansatz for DEwith a pole in the kinetic term:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{2}\frac{\varsigma}{\sigma^{n}}\left(\partial\sigma\right)^{2}-V(\sigma)

(42)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle=\frac{2\sqrt{\varsigma}}{\absolutevalue{2-n}}\sigma^{\left(2-n\right)/2}

(43)

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\absolutevalue{2-n}}{2\sqrt{\varsigma}}\right)^{2/\left(2-n\right)}\phi^{2/\left(2-n\right)}

(44)

with
\varsigma
the residue,

\sigma\in\left[0,\infty\right]
the pole position, and

n
the order.
He finds that poles can be stilts over the swampland, at least sometimes: The tested models all satisfy the DC. For the dSC, he expresses
eq.

12
in terms of the pole
\sigma
:

\frac{\absolutevalue{\nabla V}}{V}=\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{n}}{\varsigma}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\mathrm{d}V/\mathrm{d}\sigma}}{V},

(45)

and uses this to assess the following models where
n>2
.
72
72

72

For
n>2
, monomial potentials get transformed into inverse power-laws, and inverse power-law becomes monomial, which is the more interesting case than
n<2
where monomials stay monomials and inverse power-law potentials stay inverse power-law potentials.

V\sim\sigma^{k}

\absolutevalue{\nabla V}/V=2\absolutevalue{k}/\phi\absolutevalue{2-n}
, which can be satisfied, since the field excursion is sub-Planckian.

V\sim\exp\left(-\beta\sigma\right),\,n=2

\absolutevalue{\nabla V}/V=\beta\sqrt{\sigma^{n}/\varsigma}\rightarrow\beta/\left[\exp\left(-\phi/\sqrt{\varsigma}\right)\sqrt{\varsigma}\right]
, which can satisfy the dSCin an observationally viable range where
\phi
is not too small but
\beta
is small.

V\sim\exp\left(-\beta\sigma\right),\,n=4

\absolutevalue{\nabla V}/V=\beta\sqrt{\sigma^{n}/\varsigma}\rightarrow\beta\sqrt{\varsigma}/\phi^{2}
, which only satisfies the dSCfor the observationally not viable case with
\beta\sqrt{\varsigma}
large, as this case drives
\omega
away from -1.

Fine-Structure Constant

Studying dS

\left(p-1\right)\absolutevalue{\frac{V^\prime}{V}}\geq\absolutevalue{\frac{f^\prime}{f}}

for compatible EFTs, with
f

\left(p-1\right)

\alpha=1/f

\absolutevalue{\frac{\dot{\alpha}}{\alpha}}\lesssim\mathfrak{s}_{1}H\approx\frac{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}{${10}^{10}$\text{years}},

(47)

which is consistent with the experimental bound of
\absolutevalue{\dot{\alpha}/\alpha}<${10}^{-15}$\text{year}^{-1}
respectively

Higgs Field

The Higgs field violates

as the Higgs potential

V_{\textnormal{H}}=\lambda_{\textnormal{H}}\left(\absolutevalue{h}^{2}-v_{\textnormal{H}}^{2}\right)^{2}

has a local maximum at

\frac{\text{min}\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}V\right)}{V}\sim-\frac{10^{35}}{M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}

it is compatible with

Fukuda et al. 2019
;
Denef et al. 2018a
;

Inflation

is often described by a slow-rolling inflaton field. There are various models of inflation, which we discuss in the following. Important parameters to assess inflationary models are the slow-roll parameters

\displaystyle\epsilon_{V}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{(\nabla V)^{2}}{V^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\mathfrak{s}_{1}^{2}

(50)

\displaystyle\eta_{V}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\nabla^{2}V}{V}

\displaystyle\lesssim-\mathfrak{s}_{2}.

(51)

Slow-roll inflation takes places if
\epsilon_{V}<1
and
\eta_{V}<1

Cremonini et al. 2023a
.
The dSCinforms us that in QGa single scalar field must not be slow-rolling
Garg and Krishnan 2019
;
Dias et al. 2019a
and
Agrawal et al. 2018a
point out that most inflationary models violate
eq.

12
(cf.
Kinney et al. 2019
).
To rule out inflation based on these preliminary findings would be short-sighted.
Focussing on
\epsilon_{V}
and
\eta_{V}
might be straightforward, as only the potential has to be evaluated, but the relevant parameters for slow-roll inflation are actually the Hubble slow-roll parameters

\displaystyle\epsilon_{H}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\dot{H}}{H^{2}}

(52)

\displaystyle\eta_{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{\dot{\epsilon}_{H}}{H\epsilon_{H}},

(53)

since they depend on the full solutions of the scalar field’s EoM
Anchordoqui et al. 2021
;
Seo 2019a
;
Brahma and Hossain 2019b
;
Liddle et al. 1994
;
Guleryuz 2023
.
This is for example relevant
in the presence of multiple but non-aligned inflaton fields,
non-BDterms
Bunch et al. 1997
,
73
73

73

Describing a BDvacuum as a finely tuned superposition of spontaneous particle creation that is precisely matched by anti-particles coming from infinity shows the unnaturalness of such a setting, which makes it plausible that non-BDterms should be expected
Danielsson 2019
.

Dirac–Born–Infeld (DBI)inflation,
74
74

74

In DBIinflation, a D-brane moves through a warped throat in an extra-dimension, which leads to non-canonical kinetic terms in the EFT, and a subluminal speed of sound

;

.
This leads to large tensor components in the CMB, which are in reach of assessment
Alishahiha et al. 2004

or

In such settings, the tensor-to-scalar ratio can be shifted, such that

\displaystyle r_{\textnormal{ts}}

\displaystyle=16\epsilon_{H}\lambda_{\textnormal{c}},

(54)

where the parameter
\lambda_{\textnormal{c}}
could measure the deviation from the BDsolution, in which case it is constraint by backreaction terms and non-Gaussianity constraints

The take-home message here is that the dSC
\epsilon_{V}\sim\order{1}
can be satisfied without violating the Hubble slow-roll condition
\epsilon_{H}\ll 1

Lin and Solomon 2021
.

In the case of single-field inflation, the Hubble slow-roll parameters are related to the potential slow-roll parameters as follows

For analytical studies it can be beneficial to write
\epsilon_{H}=\frac{3}{2}\left(x^{2}-y^{2}+1\right)+\frac{1}{2}z
with

,

, and

Alestas et al. 2024
.

\displaystyle\epsilon_{V}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial_{\phi}V}{V}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2H^{2}}=\epsilon_{H}

(55)

\displaystyle\eta_{V}

\displaystyle=2\epsilon_{H}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{H}.

(56)

Even if an inflationary model appears to be incompatible with the dSC, it might be possible to rescue the model by putting it in a brane-world scenario.
76
76

76

The term
brane-world
loosely refers to any theory where a matter field is localised on a space-filling hypersurface (a brane) — the matter field could be exclusively living in the worldvolume of the brane, even though
Fichet 2020
presents some swampland arguments that favour spacetime-filling matter fields, where only some operators are localised on the brane. Matter fields exclusively localised on a brane lead to global symmetries (violating the no global symmetries conjecture (
section

II.10
)), and an energy gap of
\absolutevalue{p}\sim\left[\Lambda_{\textnormal{S}}/\sqrt{N_{\textnormal{S}}},\Lambda_{\textnormal{S}}\right]
(see
section

II.14
for the notation), where the EFTis not valid between the 4d and 5d regime, which highlights an inconsistency
Fichet 2020
. However, the work of
Fichet 2020
is criticised by
Nortier 2022
: the global symmetries can be avoided by including 5d bilocal operators arising from 5d local operators involving brane form factors. Furthermore, non-local operators that are localised on the brane and couple to the 4d bulk fields avoid global symmetries as well.

A main driver of this observation is that potentials in a brane-world with brane tension
\mathcal{T}
roll slower than potentials in the standard 4d case, as

H^{2}\simeq\frac{8\pi}{3M_{\textnormal{P;}4}^{2}}V\left(1+\frac{V}{2\mathcal{T}}\right)

(57)

is proportional to the energy density
\rho
instead of
\sqrt{\rho}

Mohammadi et al. 2022
;
Maartens et al. 2000
;
Golanbari et al. 2014
;
Mohammadi et al. 2020a
;
Elizalde et al. 2019
;
Banerjee and Paul 2017
, which induces an increased Hubble friction compared to the SM
Osses et al. 2021
. The Friedmann equation contains terms quadratic in the energy density, which dominate in the high-energy regime, i.e. where the energy density is larger than the brane tension
Mohammadi et al. 2022
.
Considering the tension of the brane yields modified slow-roll parameters for the brane:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\textnormal{B}}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{V}\frac{1+V/\mathcal{T}}{\left(1+V/2\mathcal{T}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{M_{\textnormal{P};4}^{2}}{16\pi}\left(\frac{V^{\prime}}{V}\right)^{2}

(58)

\displaystyle\eta_{\textnormal{B}}

\displaystyle=\eta_{V}\frac{1}{1+V/2\mathcal{T}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{M_{\textnormal{P};4}^{2}}{8\pi}\frac{V^{\prime\prime}}{V},

(59)

where
\epsilon_{V}
and
\eta_{V}
are the standard 4D slow-roll parameters with their usual definition regarding the inflaton potential
Osses et al. 2021
;
Mohammadi et al. 2022
.
Furthermore, the number of
e
-folds is modified as well:

N_{e}\simeq-\frac{8\pi}{M_{\textnormal{P};4}}\int\!\frac{V}{V^{\prime}}\left(1+\frac{V}{2\mathcal{T}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\phi,

(60)

i.e. it takes more
e
-foldings to get from an initial field value
\phi_{\textnormal{i}}
to a final field value
\phi_{\textnormal{f}}

Osses et al. 2021
.
The tensor-to-scalar ratio at low energies is not affected, and
r_{\textnormal{ts}}\simeq 16\epsilon_{V}
holds;
at high energies, where the massless mode to extend into the 5d bulk,
r_{\textnormal{ts}}\simeq 24\epsilon_{\textnormal{B}}
holds
Osses et al. 2021
.
Models of brane inflation to be found compatible with the dSCare for example
power-law inflation of the form
V\sim\phi^{n}
,
natural inflation of the form
V\sim\left(1-\cos{\phi/f}\right)
, or
T-model inflation (
eq.

114
)
Mohammadi et al. 2022
.

If inflation is not driven by the scalar field but by fluctuations, e.g. induced through non-perturbative effects such as a spinodal instability, the dSClooses its constraining power
Holman and Richard 2019
.

Even before the dSCwas formalised as a swampland conjecture — actually even before the swampland programme itself started —
Hertzberg et al. 2007

\epsilon_{V}>27/13
, i.e. they already presented a bound very similar to the dSCthat ruled out slow-roll single-field inflation. Also
Flauger et al. 2009

\epsilon_{V}\sim\order{1}
in type IIA settings that rule out slow-roll inflation.

The culprits are the scalar spectral index

and the tensor-to-scalar ratio
r_{\textnormal{ts}}
that are constrained by observational data to
r_{\textnormal{ts}}<0.1
and
n_{\textnormal{s}}=$0.9649(0.0042)$

Planck Collaboration et al. 2020

and

by the dSC
Kinney et al. 2019
;
Kehagias and Riotto 2018
;
Matsui and Takahashi 2019
;
Ben-Dayan 2019
;
Agrawal and Obied 2018
;
Fukuda et al. 2019
;
Chiang et al. 2019
.

This finding seems to hold even in non-minimally coupled matter–non-metricity theory of gravity (

\mathcal{F}

DBIModels

The inflaton field is kept near its maximum by an effective sound speed limit, such that
r_{\textnormal{ts}}\simeq 16c_{\textnormal{s}}\epsilon_{V}<0.1
, which still requires
\mathfrak{s}_{1}<\sqrt{2\epsilon_{V}}<0.4

Rasouli et al. 2019

A multi-field DBImodel with non-canonical terms satisfies the dSCif the speed of sound is sufficiently small
Solomon and Trodden 2020
.

Some authors argue that the dSCis actually not violated at all:

•

Kehagias and Riotto 2018
raise the argument that we do not know how scalar perturbations are created. If curvature perturbations are only created at super-Hubble scales, at the very end of inflation, or after inflation ends, e.g. when a curvaton field decays, the restrictions from the low tensor-to-scalar ratio do not apply and the dSCis not violated.

•

Akrami et al. 2019
argue that the dSCdoes not even apply to slow-roll inflation, since slow-roll does take place far away from the dSregime, as the inflaton perturbations are
\propto 1/\absolutevalue{\nabla V}
and observations predict
\absolutevalue{\nabla V}\gtrsim${10}^{5}$V^{3/2}
, i.e. there is no dSphase during slow-roll inflation.

Eternal inflation

is not possible if the dSCis true
Matsui and Terada 2020
;
Brahma and Shandera 2019
;
Kinney 2019
;
Dimopoulos 2018
;
Matsui and Takahashi 2019
;
Rudelius 2019
;
Dvali and Gomez 2013a
;
Dvali et al. 2019
;

;
Alestas et al. 2024
.
In eternal inflation, quantum fluctuations dominate over classical fluctuations,
78
78

78

This allows the inflaton field to roll upwards in the potential
Kinney 2019
.

\delta_{q}\phi\lesssim\delta_{c}\phi
still allows for eternal inflation to happen in some edge cases, as regions where the scalar field moves up the potential expand faster than regions where the scalar field moves down the potential
Wang et al. 2020
.

which translates to the following constraints
Matsui and Terada 2020
;
Rudelius 2019
;
Barenboim et al. 2016
;
Rudelius 2021a
;
Matsui and Takahashi 2019
;
Kinney 2019
:

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\delta\phi}_{\textnormal{q}}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{H}{2\pi}

(61)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\delta\phi}_{\textnormal{c}}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{\absolutevalue{\dot{\phi}}}{H}

(62)

\displaystyle\frac{\expectationvalue{\delta\phi}_{\textnormal{q}}}{\expectationvalue{\delta\phi}_{\textnormal{c}}}=\frac{H^{2}}{2\pi\absolutevalue{\dot{\phi}}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim 1

(63)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\frac{H}{M_{\textnormal{P}}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim 2\pi\sqrt{2\epsilon_{V}}.

(64)

For eternal inflation to happen,
eq.

63
has to hold, which is expressed in terms of the slow-roll parameter in
eq.

64

Matsui and Takahashi 2019
;
Kinney 2019
.
Eternal chaotic inflation is incompatible with the dSC, since
eq.

64
yields
H/M_{\textnormal{P}}\gtrsim 2\pi\mathfrak{s}_{1}
, i.e. a trans-Planckian Hubble scale
Matsui and Takahashi 2019
;
Kinney 2019
;
Wang et al. 2020
.

A second issue is that the volume expansion must dominate over the decay rate of the field, which translates into the following condition
Matsui and Terada 2020
;
Rudelius 2019
;
Matsui and Terada 2020
;
Barenboim et al. 2016
;

\displaystyle\frac{V^{\prime\prime}}{V}

\displaystyle>-\frac{2\left(d-1\right)}{d-2}\frac{1}{M_{\textnormal{P};d}^{d-2}}.

(65)

This clashes directly with the
\frac{V^{\prime\prime}}{V}<-\mathfrak{s}_{2}
constraint from the refined dSC
Rudelius 2021a
.
79
79

79

It was argued by
Kinney 2019
that eternal inflation can be made consistent with the refined dSC, but this argumentation was debunked by
Brahma and Shandera 2019
.

The lifetime of the current phase of accelerated expansion of the Universe can be derived by combing the dSCwith the DC
Agrawal et al. 2018a
:

\tau\lesssim\frac{3\Delta\phi/M_{\textnormal{P}}}{2\mathfrak{s}_{1}\Omega_{\phi,0}H_{0}},

(66)

with
\Delta\phi/M_{\textnormal{P}}\sim\order{1}
the field range limit from the DC,

\mathfrak{s}_{1}
the dSCparameter,

\Omega_{\phi,0}
today’s relative DEdensity,
and
H_{0}
the Hubble constant. Since this yields a finite number, eternal inflation is ruled out.

A particularly illuminating paper is presented by
Rudelius 2019
: He studies different models of inflation that can lead to eternal inflation and shows that eternal inflation is incompatible with the dSC. He even goes one step further and proposes that it is not the case that there cannot be eternal inflation
because
the dSCholds, but that there is a QGrule that forbids eternal inflation from taking place, and the dSCis a mere consequence of this fundamental no-go theorem. We’d like to highlight a few key takeaways from his paper

•

eternal inflation takes place, unless one of the following conditions holds:

\displaystyle\frac{\absolutevalue{\nabla V}}{V}

\displaystyle>\frac{\sqrt{2V}}{2\pi M_{\textnormal{P}}^{3}}

(67)

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{i}V}{V}

\displaystyle<-\frac{3}{M_{\textnormal{P}}}

(68)

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi^{2}M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}\absolutevalue{\nabla V}^{2}}{V^{2}}-\frac{V}{3M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}\frac{\sum_{i}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{i}V}{V}

\displaystyle>\frac{V}{M_{\textnormal{P}}^{4}}

(69)

\displaystyle\left[-\sign\left(\nabla^{p}V\right)\right]^{p+1}\frac{\absolutevalue{\nabla^pV}}{V^{\left(4-p\right)/2}}

\displaystyle>\mathfrak{c}_{p}M_{\textnormal{P}}^{p-4}

(70)

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma}{H^{4}}

\displaystyle>\frac{9}{4\pi},

(71)

where
\mathfrak{c}_{p}\gg 1
is a numerically derived constant and
\Gamma
the decay rate per unit volume.

Equations

67
,
68
and
69
are implied by the dSC:

–

Since
V<M_{\textnormal{P}}^{4}
,
eq.

12
implies
eq.

67
for
\mathfrak{s}_{1}>\sqrt{2}M_{\textnormal{P}}/2\pi
.

–

Since
\absolutevalue{\sum_i\nabla_i\nabla_iV}\geq\absolutevalue{\min\nabla_i\nabla_jV}
,
eq.

13
implies
eq.

68
for
\mathfrak{s}_{2}>3/M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}
.

–

The same two observations can also be used to show that
eq.

69
is implied by the combined dSC(
eq.

14
) for
2\pi^{2}\mathfrak{p}_{2}>1/3
.

•

Ruling out eternal inflation or dSsolutions could well be just a lamppost effect. This new insight combined with the above findings would then indicate that eternal inflation takes place in a strong-coupling string regime and is truly a QGphenomenon.
80
80

80

Banks 2019
argues that eternal inflation is incompatible with QG, as eternal inflation would violate holographic principles and would require an infinite dimensional Hilbert space while the entropy of the Universe appears to be finite.

•

The motivation to promote a
‘no eternal inflation’
no-go theorem is that a universe with eternal inflation is qualitatively, fundamentally different from a universe without eternal inflation.
81
81

81

See e.g. the work by
Banks 2019
.
Checking if a model complies with the dSCis often parameter-dependent, i.e. there is no fundamental difference between a model that violates the dSCand a model that satisfies the dSC.

•

Models of inflation that can but do not necessarily lead to eternal inflation are:

–

Power-law inflation with
V\sim\phi^{\mathfrak{p}}
can lead to eternal inflation, but parameter-choices that are likely compatible with observations do not lead to eternal inflation.
82
82

82

Even worse,
Oikonomou et al. 2023
;
Oikonomou et al. 2022
find models of power-law inflation to be generally incompatible with Planck data, as the observed value of the spectral index of scalar perturbations
n_{\textnormal{s}}
requires
1.7\lesssim\mathfrak{p}\lesssim 2.8
while compatibility with the observed tensor-to-scalar ratio
r_{\textnormal{ts}}
requires
0\leq\mathfrak{p}\lesssim 0.9
, which is mutually exclusive.

–

Starobinsky inflation can lead to eternal inflation for super-Planckian field ranges.

–

Inflection point inflation with a potential of the form
V=V_{0}+\mathfrak{l}\phi+\frac{1}{6}\mathfrak{c}\phi^{3}
is not eternal if compatible with observations.

•

Models of inflation that necessarily lead to eternal inflation are:

–

Quadratic hilltop inflation of the form
V=V_{0}-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\phi^{2}+\frac{1}{6}\mathfrak{c}\phi^{3}
inflate eternally if compatible with observations.

–

General hilltop inflation with potentials of the form
V=V_{0}-\frac{1}{p}\mathfrak{c}\phi^{\mathfrak{p}}
with
\mathfrak{p}\geq 4
inflate eternally.

Similar findings, resulting in a no eternal inflation conjecture are also presented by
Russo and Townsend 2022
: having two dilatons and one axion coupled to gravity, they derive bounds on the scalar field space gradient
83
83

83

The scalar field gradient is equal to the dilaton self-coupling in their model.

and curvature that are incompatible with eternal inflation.
84
84

84

The characterising feature of their model is that it reproduces itself under dimensional reduction or truncation (which only happens under the presence of
two
dilatons).

They stress that both criteria are necessary to rule out eternal inflation — a bound only on the gradient alone is insufficient.
Eternal inflation is ruled out by showing that the spacetime metric cannot have a future cosmological event horizon.

Despite these conceptual constraints, there are concrete models of eternal inflation. In the following, we present some, focussing on their interplay with the dSC.

•

Hilltop models of eternal inflation either violate entropy bounds or the dSC, and suffer from a graceful exit problem
Wang et al. 2020
.
Hilltop potentials
Lin 2020
as well as models with tachyonic scalar fields
Trivedi 2022a
show better consistency in brane-world scenarios.

Trivedi 2022a
achieved better compatibility between the Gibbons–Hawking entropy bounds and the dSC
Wang et al. 2020
by applying a generalised uncertainty principle to derive a lower bound on the potential in such a setting.

•

Eternal inflation with a steep potential of the form
V(\phi)=V_{0}\exp\left(\mathfrak{s}_{1}\phi/M_{\textnormal{P}}\right)
is found to be problematic in the light of the dSC
Dimopoulos 2018
;
Alestas et al. 2024
;
Agrawal et al. 2018a
;
Akrami et al. 2019
;
Raveri et al. 2019
;
Akrami et al. 2021
. For
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\geq 1
, there was less than one upwards quantum fluctuation per Hubble time, which essentially means that inflation stops
Dimopoulos 2018
.

•

Matsui and Terada 2020
study a model in the context of the Hartle–Hawking no-boundary proposal:
An infinitely extended plateau potential violates the DCas well as the dSCright away, even though it is favoured by observations
Planck Collaboration et al. 2020
. To get a similar observational behaviour without the theoretical complications, an approximate plateau potential is studied:

V=V_{0}\left[\left(\tanh{\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{6\varsigma}}}\right)+\varepsilon\cosh{\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{6\varsigma}}}\right],

(72)

with
\varepsilon\ll 1
, which breaks the shift symmetry of
\phi
, and
\varsigma
a dimensionless parameter, which controls whether the model is a small field inflation model or a large field inflation model (since the typical field range is given by
\Delta\phi\sim\sqrt{6\varsigma}
). When
\phi/\sqrt{\varsigma}
is large, the number of
e
-folds during inflation is high and the slow-roll parameters are small. The value of
V_{0}
is bounded by CMBobservations.
85
85

85

We would expect that also BBNobservations could provide us with bounds, but this was not taken into consideration by
Matsui and Terada 2020
.

Numerical analysis shows that
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
have to be smaller than
\order{10^{-5}}
, a clear contradiction with the dSC, which demands
\order{1}
-parameters.

•

Guleryuz 2024
presents eternal inflation with a twist that is compatible with observations and the dSC: In his model of
non-perpetual
eternal inflation, where a tachyonic instability ends eternal inflation after more than 400
e
-folds, such that it is locally perceived as eternal inflation, the speed of sound is subluminal and
\mathfrak{s}_{2}\simeq\left(0.6\sim 8\right)
is found.

in a GRbased cosmology is ruled out if

and

We discuss various single-field models in more detail in the following.

Herrera 2020
uses Planck data to reconstruct an effective potential with a
k
-essence
86
86

86

In
k
-essence, kinetic terms are present in the Lagrangian of the scalar field, such that
L=-\left(g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi/2\right)\left(1+2g(\phi)\right)-V(\phi)
, with
g(\phi)
a coupling function.

Ansatz for the inflaton, and shows that this model has a narrow range of viability, where it satisfies observational constraints as well as the dSC:
He expresses the potential in terms of the number of
e
-folds
N_{e}
, such that

\displaystyle V(N_{e})

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{e}}{\lambda+\beta N_{e}}

(73)

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{e}^{2}}{12\pi^{2}P_{\textnormal{s}}},

(74)

where the powerspectrum

requires

for

.
For
\beta>0
, the reconstructed effective potential corresponds to natural inflation.
For
\beta<0
, the reconstructed effective potential corresponds to hyperbolic inflation, which yields an exponential respectively power-law function for the effective scalar field potential in different limits.
Taking into account observational constraints as well as the dSCyields
\pm$1.5\text{\times}{10}^{8}$<\beta<\pm$3.2\text{\times}{10}^{8}$
, i.e. there is a narrow window for this model to work.

In modified gravity theories, single-field inflation can be accommodated while retaining compatibility with the dSCand the DC
Trivedi 2022b
;
Trivedi 2021
;
Mohammadi et al. 2020b
;
Sadeghi et al. 2021
.
87
87

87

It is not a general finding that modified gravity makes inflation compatible with the dSCwhile inflation taking place in a GRsetting is at odds with the dSC.
In a theory with a non-minimal coupling between matter and curvature,
Bertolami et al. 2023
;
Das 2020
find that cold single-field slow-roll inflation is incompatible with the dSC. The studied model has the action
S=\int\!\sqrt{-g}\left[f_{1}(R)M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}/2+f_{2}(R)\mathcal{L}\right]\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x
, with two functions that depend on the curvature —
f_{1}(R)
corresponds to the function known from
f(R)
gravity that introduces a Yukawa-type 5
th
force, and
f_{2}(R)
introduces an additional force that depends on the spatial gradient of the Ricci scalar
Bertolami et al. 2023
.
Also found to be incompatible with the dSCis mimetic
f(G)
gravity
Gashti et al. 2023
.
Nevertheless, a general statement can be made
Trivedi 2022b
: If a theory of modified gravity yields Friedmann equations of the form
F(H)=\rho/3\sim V/3
during inflation, the slow-roll parameter can be expressed as
\epsilon=F(H)H^{\prime}\mathfrak{s_{1}}/H^{3}
, and the dSCis not violated as long as
H^{3}/F(H)H^{\prime}\gg\order{1}
can be obtained during inflation.
This generalisation relies on two key assumptions
Trivedi 2022b
:
First, the Klein–Gordon equation for the inflaton holds, i.e.
\ddot{\phi}+3H\dot{\phi}+V^{\prime}=0
.
Second, the background metric and its perturbations are not modified.

Some examples are

Inverse Monomial Inflation

Gauss–Bonnet inflation

has the action

S=\frac{1}{2}\int\!\sqrt{-g}\left[R-\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi-2V(\phi)-\mathcal{F}(\phi)\mathfrak{G}\right],

(77)

with
\mathfrak{G}=R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}-4R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}+R^{2}
the topological GBterm,
and
\mathcal{F}(\phi)
the GBcoupling function
Yi and Gong 2019
;
Odintsov and Oikonomou 2018
;
Chakraborty et al. 2018

;
Kanti et al. 1999
;

For the choice
\mathcal{F}(\phi)=3\mathfrak{p}/4V(\phi)

0<\mathfrak{p}<1

\displaystyle r_{\textnormal{ts}}

\displaystyle=16\left(1-\mathfrak{p}\right)\epsilon_{V}

(78)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{V}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\mathfrak{p}}{2}\left(\frac{V^{\prime}}{V}\right)^{2}

(79)

\displaystyle\eta_{V}

\displaystyle=-2\left(1-\mathfrak{p}\right)\left[\frac{V^{\prime\prime}}{V}-\left(\frac{V^{\prime}}{V}\right)^{2}\right],

(80)

which can be compatible with observational constraints regarding

Lorentz violation can remedy the compatibility between models of single-field inflation and the dSC. The relevant equations with
\mathfrak{c_{\textnormal{L}}}
parametrising the Lorentz violation are the following

\displaystyle\ddot{\phi}+3H\dot{\phi}+\frac{V^{\prime}}{1+\mathfrak{c_{\textnormal{L}}}}

\displaystyle=0

(81)

\displaystyle H^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3M_{\textnormal{P}}}\left(\frac{1+\mathfrak{c_{\textnormal{L}}}}{2}\dot{\phi}^{2}+V\right)

(82)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}{2\left(1+\mathfrak{c_{\textnormal{L}}}\right)}\left(\frac{V^{\prime}}{V}\right)^{2}

(83)

\displaystyle\eta_{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}{2\left(1+\mathfrak{c_{\textnormal{L}}}\right)}\frac{V^{\prime\prime}}{V}

(84)

\displaystyle N_{e}

\displaystyle=\int\!\frac{1+\mathfrak{c_{\textnormal{L}}}}{M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}\frac{V}{V^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\phi.

(85)

For
\mathfrak{c_{\textnormal{L}}}\gg 1
the field can be slow-rolling while satisfying the dSC

. Models that use the breaking of Lorentz invariance include the following:

Inflation with Spontaneous Symmetry Breaking

Broken Lorentz invariance
and a potential of the form

Multi-field inflation

cannot be distinguished from single-field inflation with current observations, as the expected perturbations or non-Gaussian effects are smaller than the current observational thresholds. This does not necessarily change in the near future, as
Tokeshi and Vennin 2024
point out. In their study, they found that multi-field models that have a single-field attractor solution typically inflate the most, i.e. volume-selection effects favour scenarios that look like single-field inflation.
However, it is interesting to study multi-field models from a theoretical perspective, as the incompatibility with the dSCcan be avoided:
\epsilon_{V}\neq\epsilon_{H}
can be obtained
Guleryuz 2023
, such that the slow-roll condition potentially can be satisfied without spoiling the dSC
Achúcarro and Palma 2019
;
Ben-Dayan 2019
.
92
92

92

Not all multi-field models satisfy the dSC, e.g.
two-field quintessential Higgs inflation, with a quintessence field coupled to a non-minimally coupled Higgs field
Eshaghi et al. 2022
;
Eshaghi et al. 2025
doesn’t do the trick, as
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\lesssim$8\text{\times}{10}^{-3}$
is found to be in agreement with observations.

Examples are
axion inflation
Damian and Loaiza-Brito 2019
;
Jin et al. 2021
;
Rudelius 2019
;
fat inflation
Chakraborty et al. 2020
;
Aragam et al. 2022
;
multi-field DBIinflation with non-canonical kinetic terms
Solomon and Trodden 2020
,
models of inflation with a flat or negatively curved field space metric
Aragam et al. 2020
;
rapid turn inflation
93
93

93

Rapid turn inflation models are multi-field inflation models where angular momentum prevents the inflaton field from rolling down a steep potential, with the angular momentum either satisfying
\partial_{t}\left(a^{3}J\right)=0
, such that the angular momentum redshifts exponentially fast during inflation, or satisfying
\partial_{t}\left(a^{3}J\right)=a^{3}\partial_{\theta}V(\phi,\theta)
, such that the angular momentum is nearly constant during inflation
Kolb et al. 2023
. Models of the former type are also referred to as
hyperbolic inflation
, whereas the latter are models of
monodromy inflation
. Both types satisfy the dSC. Moreover, models of monodromy inflation are not only compatible with the dSC, they can even produce sufficient DMabundance through gravitational particle production
Kolb et al. 2023
.

such as angular inflation
Christodoulidis et al. 2019
, hyperinflation,
94
94

94

Bjorkmo and Marsh 2019
found that hyperinflation generalised to an arbitrary number of fields can satisfy either the dSC
or
the DC, but not both simultaneously while also reheating the universe.
Small-field hyperinflation compatible with the dSCneeds to be severely fine-tuned if it is to agree with cosmological observations, such as the powerspectrum, or solve the horizon and flatness problem
Bjorkmo and Marsh 2019
.
The potential in hyperinflation agrees with the dSCbecause the steepness of the potential is bound by the curvature of the hyperbolic plane
R
:

3R<\absolutevalue{V^\prime}/V<1/R.

and monodromy inflation;

It is reasoned that axion monodromy inflation is a test of UVeffects, as non-perturbative effects leave their imprints on IRobservables such as the powerspectrum and the bispectrum
