g^{2}\gtrsim\frac{3}{2}\left(\frac{H}{M_{\textnormal{P}}}\right)^{2}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=g_{\text{min}}^{2},

(224)

and using
V=3M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}H^{2}
, such that the bound of applicability becomes

\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}\absolutevalue{V^{\prime\prime}}}{V}\lesssim\left(\frac{g}{g_{\text{min}}}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{1/4},

(225)

which is consistent with the dSC
Mishra 2023
.

This makes it even less likely to find a coupling small enough to satisfy
eq.

222
.
We conclude that it is not trivial to circumvent the FLBduring inflation. The FLBis likely a meaningful constraint for inflation.

Thermal effects could limit the energy scale of inflation to
\Lambda_{\text{infl}}\ll${10}^{5}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
, as we show around
section

II.7.2
.

For singe-field slow-roll inflation, the bounds

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\geq h\left(\frac{16\pi^{3}g_{\text{re}}g_{\text{em}}}{60m_{e}^{4}}\right)^{1/4}T_{\text{rh}}

\displaystyle>${10}^{4}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$

(226)

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2m_{e}^{2}}{\sqrt{96g_{\text{em}}}M_{\textnormal{P}}}

\displaystyle<${10}^{7}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$

(227)

\displaystyle r_{\textnormal{ts}}

\displaystyle\lesssim$3\text{\times}{10}^{-15}$,

(228)

with
h
the value of the Higgs field,

g_{\text{rh}}
the effective degrees of freedom during reheating,

g_{\text{em}}\simeq$9.5\text{\times}{10}^{-3}$
the electromagnetic coupling,

m_{e}
the electron mass,

T_{\text{rh}}\gtrsim T_{\text{BBN}}\sim$10\text{\,}\mathrm{MeV}$
the temperature during reheating,
and
r_{\textnormal{ts}}
the tensor-to-scalar ratio,
can be obtained by noting that the Higgs vacuum expectation value does not correspond to the EWscale during inflation
Lee et al. 2022c
.

If the model of inflation solely consists of the SMparticles plus an inflaton field, the charged SMparticles violate the FLBfor high enough inflaton field energies
Montero et al. 2022
.
The FLBcan be satisfied in Higgs inflation, if the vacuum expectation value of the Higgs field is high enough to make gluons, quarks, and leptons sufficiently massive
Montero et al. 2022
;
Montero et al. 2020b
.
Another option to satisfy the FLBis by coupling the inflaton to the kinetic terms of the gauge fields, as this reduces the gauge coupling — one has to make sure though that compatibility with the WGCis retained: this means that
m^{2}\geq\sqrt{6}gqM_{\textnormal{P}}H\geq 3H^{2}

Montero et al. 2022
. Since the expected mass of particles during inflation is of
\order{H}
this is likely satisfied
Montero et al. 2022
.

Cribiori and Farakos 2023
show that single-field D-term inflation is in tension with the FLB, but also with the gravitino DC.

Particle Physics

Since the FLBfor the U(1) gauge field in our Universe is satisfied, the photon is massless.

Charged states obtain mass bounds:
There can be no massive, charged state with mass below
\Lambda^{2}M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}
, which is satisfied today but can be an important constraint during inflation
Montero et al. 2021
.
Massless charged particles are only compatible with the FLBif
the DEpotential vanishes entirely
Lee et al. 2022c
,
or the particles arise from confined or Higgsed non-Abelian gauge fields, i.e.

\displaystyle m_{\text{gauge field}}\gtrsim H

(229)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\text{c}}\gtrsim H,

(230)

with
\Lambda_{\text{c}}
the confinement scale
Mishra 2023
;
Montero et al. 2021
;
Montero et al. 2022
.
This bound relates a pure QFTquantity (
\Lambda_{\text{c}}
), with a quantity (
H
) closely related to the dSspacetime that acts as a background
Mishra 2023
.
208
208

208

Venken 2023
argues that the confinement scale should be higher than a scale proportional to the vacuum energy and that the Higgs mass should be higher than a scale proportional to the Higgs vacuum scale when thermal effects are taken into account. We discuss this in more detail in our paragraph on thermal effects.

Using the FLB, a non-zero Higgs vacuum expectation value is considered by
Gonzalo et al. 2022b
;
Montero et al. 2021
.

Lee et al. 2022c
study the applicability of the FLBfor various forms of the Higgs potential: The UVbehaviour is yet undetermined, but allows for three different scenarios.
First, the potential could be monotonically increasing, in which case the FLBis not applicable.
Second, the potential could have degenerate Higgs vacua
209
209

209

For degenerate Higgs vacua, the FLBis trivially satisfied, as the effective potential vanishes.

or inflection points.
210
210

210

In the latter case, the vacuum energy at the deflection points becomes too large to satisfy the FLB, unless quantum effects allow a tunnelling faster than BHdecay, which renders the FLBinapplicable.

The FLBis applicable and sets an upper limit on the quartic coupling.
And third, the potential could have a local UVminimum that is negative, i.e. the space would be AdSand the FLBnot applicable (unless another source of vacuum energy lifts the combined effective potential into dS).
An argument against a cowboy hat potential with a local minimum at the origin is that in this setting, the electron, and other hypercharged states, remain classically massless
Montero et al. 2021
.
To be compatible with the FLB, any long-lived minimum of the Higgs potential must break EWsymmetry
Montero et al. 2021
.

but the constraints can be improved by taking into account the FLB:
Montero et al. 2022

q\geq\left(m/$1.6\text{\,}\mathrm{meV}$\right)^{2}
, or even

As
Kobayashi and Afshordi 2014
show, particle production through Schwinger pair production during magnetogenesis is constrained by a strong suppression of the magnetic field by IRhyperconductivity: as shown by
Fröb et al. 2014
, light charged particles with
m^{2}\ll H^{2}
can induce strong currents,
J\sim H^{3}/E
for
m/H\ll qE/H^{2}\ll 1
, which end magnetogenesis. However, the regime of IRhyperconductivity cannot be reached if the FLBholds
Grewal and Parmentier 2022
. This weakens the constraints on magnetogenesis coming from hyperconductivity, yet not those coming from other considerations, e.g. that the magnetic field must not dominate the energy budget of the Universe.

II.7.2
General Remarks

The FLBdoes not apply if the symmetry is broken, but if there is a phase where the symmetry is intact, the bound applies, e.g. a phase of unbroken EWsymmetry with vanishing masses is incompatible with a positive cosmological constant
Agmon et al. 2023
.

What can we learn from taking the limits of the FLB?

•

In the limit
H\rightarrow 0
the FLBbecomes trivial — as expected, for a conjecture about dSspace
Montero et al. 2020b
.

•

The limit
M_{\textnormal{P}}\rightarrow\infty
forbids all charged particles. This indicates that gravity cannot be decoupled in a dStheory
Montero et al. 2020b
.

•

In the limit
m^{2}\ll gqM_{\textnormal{P}}H
, the Schwinger pair production is exponentially suppressed, and the BHlooses its charge slowly, eventually vaporising completely, without ever leaving the shark fin region from
fig.

4

Montero et al. 2020b
. This corresponds to reaching equilibrium of the thermal bath and leaving empty dSspace
Montero et al. 2020b
.

•

In the limit
m^{2}\gg gqM_{\textnormal{P}}H
, the Schwinger pairs screen the electric field of the BH, i.e. the electric field is replaced by photons, which do not support the solution, and the BHcollapses into a big crunch
Montero et al. 2020b
.

What features are distinct for dS?

Other than in flat or AdSspace, where a particle that tunnels out of a BHwill eventually fall back into it (unless gravity is the weakest force, as demanded by the WGC), in dSspace, a particle can tunnel into a region where the cosmological expansion carries it away from the BH, eventually
213
213

213

The lifetime of a BHin dSspace is exponentially long in the dSlength scale
l

Montero et al. 2020b
.

evaporating the BHcompletely — in dSthe decay of BHsis not a QGprediction, but already part of the theory in a semi-classical picture
Montero et al. 2020b
.

An important observation in
section

II.1
was that AdSspaces are likely not scale separated. For dS, the FLBimplies the opposite
Montero et al. 2021
: dSspaces are scale separated with

M_{\text{KK}}\gtrsim\sqrt{\Lambda}.

(231)

This positions Minkowski space as a
great divider
, where the behaviour on each side is qualitatively distinct
Montero et al. 2021
.

What are the implications of thermal effects?

An upper bound on the energy scale of inflation of
\Lambda_{\text{I}}\ll${10}^{5}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
can be obtained by studying thermal effects in dSspace, as
Venken 2023
shows:
First of all, when studying thermal effects in dSspace, one has to consider cooling and dilution due to cosmic expansion.
However, unless the coupling is miniscule (
g\lesssim H/M_{\textnormal{P}}
), charged Nariai BHsdecay within 1
e
-fold,
214
214

214

This can be obtained by combining the magnetic FLB(
eq.

251
) with
eq.

222
.

such that the temperature of the thermal radiation can be considered constant.
Then, one has to express the energy density of thermal radiation
\rho_{r}
as

\rho_{r}=\sigma T^{d},

(232)

with
d
the number of spacetime dimensions, and
\sigma
a dimensionless coefficient that depends on
d
and on the thermally excited degrees of freedom.
As a next step, one has to consider that the FLBis only applicable when the vacuum energy is dominating, which allows Nariai BHsto exist. The exact cutoff of applicability is yet unknown. The best we can do is require

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{r}}{V}

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{l}

(233)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow T

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\mathfrak{l}V}{\sigma}\right)^{1/d}

(234)

where the factor
\mathfrak{l}\sim\order{1}
depends on the specific model in question.
It is expected that
\sigma\sim N_{T}
, with
N_{T}
the number of species that are part of the thermal radiation, i.e. species that are lighter than
T
.
N_{T}
is certainly smaller than
N_{\textnormal{S}}
, the total number of species part of the EFTas set by the species scale (
section

II.14
), but since the species scale is certainly higher than the Hubble scale (since cosmic expansion is definitely part of the EFT), the bound that takes thermal effects into account is always stronger than the bound that ignores thermal effects.
This is of particular importance during inflation, where the bound could be recast as

\Lambda_{\text{QCD}}<\left(\mathfrak{l}V_{\text{I}}/\sigma\right)^{1/d},

(235)

which would either mean that
V_{\text{I}}<\Lambda_{\text{QCD}}
or that there is a large number of extra species present during inflation (a recurring theme in the swampland programme):

N_{T}\gtrsim\mathfrak{l}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{\text{I}}}{\Lambda_{\text{QCD}}}\right)^{d},

(236)

with
\Lambda_{\text{I}}=V_{\text{I}}^{1/d}
.
We can combine the bounds to make a statement about the energy scale of inflation:

{AmSalign}

N_T≳
l(

Λ

I

Λ

QCD

)
^d

Λ_S=

M

P;d

N

S

1

d-2

⇒N_S=(

M

P;d

Λ

S

)^d-2

N_T<N_S

⇒
l(

Λ

I

Λ

QCD

)
^d<(

M

P

Λ

S

)^d-2

Λ_
I
≪Λ_
S

⇒Λ_
I
≪M_P^(d-2)(2d-2)Λ_
QCD
^d/(2d-2),

which yields
\Lambda_{\text{I}}\ll${10}^{5}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
. This indicates that inflation itself is not a high-energy process.
This is not unambiguously a welcomed finding, for example, the overshoot problem in string cosmology could possibly be solved by a large energy scale for inflation
Conlon and Revello 2022
.
The constraint could be circumvented if inflation is not described by the same EFTas the late-time Universe.

Is the FLBexact?

Abe et al. 2023
question the leading numerical factor in
eq.

210
: Backreactions might modify the exact value of the
\order{1}
numerical factor.

Aalsma et al. 2023
go a step further and investigate if backreactions could avoid the creation of a naked singularity entirely, and find this indeed to be the case.
215
215

215

It is argued that it is impossible to overspin or overcharge an extremal 4d Kerr–Newman BH
Sorce and Wald 2017
.

They find that there are unsuppressed decay channels that do not end in a big crunch, but in a RNdSgeometry, without creating a singularity, i.e. the observer remains outside the horizon during the entire discharge process.
216
216

216

Hassan et al. 2025
raise the concern that multi-particle states — in particular particle–anti-particle annihilation — have not been considered in the study by
Aalsma et al. 2023
, which might open up additional decay channels.

How can the FLBbe expressed for scalar fields?

Montero et al. 2021
argue that in a 4d theory with a U(1) gauge field, and a scalar field
\phi
with potential
V(\phi)>0
, such that the scalar field couples to the gauge field as in

\displaystyle S=

\displaystyle\int\!\sqrt{\absolutevalue{g}}\left(\frac{1}{2}M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}R-\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial\phi\right)^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{1}{4}\lambda(\phi)F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-V(\phi)\right)+\text{matter}

(237)

the inequality

\absolutevalue{\frac{V^\prime}{V}}<\absolutevalue{\frac{\lambda^\prime}{\lambda}}

(238)

needs to be fulfilled to assure that a Nariai branch exists.
They note that this can be in tension with the dSC, unless
\mathfrak{s}_{1}<\absolutevalue{\lambda^\prime/\lambda}
(
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
the dSC
\order{1}
constant).
We would object to the existence of the tension: The FLBmakes sure extremal Nariai BHscan decay and do not grow unbounded to scales bigger than the cosmological horizon. If there is no Nariai solution, one does not have to worry about the unbounded growth of a Nariai BHat all, as there simply is no such BH.

Can the FLBbe applied to multiple fields?

A big difference between the WGCand the FLBis that the FLBapplies to
every
particle; to satisfy the WGC, it suffices if
a
particle exists that satisfies the WGCbound
Montero et al. 2021
;
Montero et al. 2022
.
This leads to the multi-field generalisation of the FLB — the analogue of the convex hull conjecture (see text along
fig.

6
):
For a system with multiple gauge fields with Lagrangian

L=\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}{2}R-\Lambda-\frac{1}{4}f_{AB}F_{\mu\nu}^{A}F^{B\mu\nu}

(239)

with
R
the Ricci scalar,
\Lambda
the dSDEdensity,
f_{AB}
the mixing tensor, and
F_{\mu\nu}
the gauge field strength tensor,
the FLBcan be written as

m^{4}\gtrsim 2q_{A}g_{A}\left(f^{-1}\right)^{AB}q_{B}g_{B}\Lambda M_{\textnormal{P}}

(240)

which simplifies to the single-field case for
f^{-1}=1

Ban et al. 2023
;
Montero et al. 2021
;
Montero et al. 2022
.
A simple example can be a model with two mixing Abelian gauge fields, a SMU(1) gauge field and a hidden field
h
with U
h
(1) gauge symmetry
Ban et al. 2023
: Let’s say the kinetic term in the Lagrangian is of the form

L\sim-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}\mathcal{F}^{\mu\nu}-\frac{\chi}{2}\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu},

(241)

with
F_{\mu\nu}
referring to the SMU(1),

\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}
referring to the hidden gauge field,
and
\chi
the mixing parameter between the electromagnetic U(1) and the hidden U
h
(1),
such that

f=\begin{pmatrix}1&\chi\\
\chi&1\end{pmatrix}.

(242)

Then, the FLBreads as

m^{4}\gtrsim\frac{6\left(M_{\textnormal{P}}H\right)^{2}}{1-\chi^{2}}\left(g_{h}q_{h}\right)^{2}

(243)

or, if there are more than two gauge symmetries in the hidden sector, as

m^{4}\gtrsim 6\left(M_{\textnormal{P}}H\right)^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(g_{hj}q_{hj}\right)^{2}.

(244)

Using the formalism of
the charge-to-mass ratio vector
\vec{z}=\frac{1}{m}\left(q_{1},\dots,q_{N}\right)
and an approximation of
eq.

240
,
Guidetti et al. 2023
express the range of allowed values as

1<\norm{\Vec{z}}<\sqrt{\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{H}}.

(245)

They use this bound to find a maximum number of axions that are allowed in a theory by the following reasoning:
Assume you have a theory with
N
axions.
The largest individual axionic charge that is allowed by the FLBin the theory (if the axionic FLBholds in this form), is
z_{\text{max}}=M_{\textnormal{P}}/fS_{\iota}
.
217
217

217

Here,
f
is the axion decay constant, as usual, which is unrelated to the mixing tensor
f^{AB}
.

This in turn gives us an upper bound for the charge-to-mass ratio vector of
\norm{\Vec{z}}=\sqrt{N}M_{\textnormal{P}}/fS_{\iota}
.
It follows that the number of axions in the theory

N<\frac{f^{2}S_{\iota}^{2}}{M_{\textnormal{P}}H}.

(246)

Furthermore, if the axionic WGC
218
218

218

fS_{\iota}<M_{\textnormal{P}}
holds, then

N<\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{H}.

(247)

How can the FLBconstrain general
p
-forms?

In
section

II.19.2
we explain how the WGCcan be generalised for
p
-forms.
Hassan et al. 2025
generalise the FLBfor
p
-forms:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\frac{1}{p+1}+\frac{\beta-p}{4}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim gHM_{\textnormal{P}}^{\frac{d-2}{2}}

(248)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\frac{\beta-p}{4}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim gM_{\textnormal{P}}^{\frac{d-2}{2}},

(249)

with
\mathcal{T}
the tension and
\beta=4
for even
p\neq 4
, and
\beta=3
for odd
p\neq 3
.
For
p>4
, these are upper bounds on the tension, and lower bounds for
p<4
. This can be understood as follows: The charge on the brane scales as
\mathcal{T}^{p/4}
. For
p>4
, the charge on the brane increases with increasing tension, which increases the nucleation rate (in analogy to the Schwinger pair production rate). Since the FLBrequires that decays happen not too fast, it is natural to understand this as an upper bound on the tension.
Furthermore, for 2-branes without world-volume gauge fields and in the absence of axions, the bound

\mathcal{T}\gtrsim\left(gHM_{\textnormal{P}}\right)^{3/2}

(250)

is derived.

Is there a magnetic counterpart?

In analogy to the magnetic WGC(
eq.

461
) that demands that the magnetic monopole is not a BHand sets an upper bound on the cutoff scale of an EFT, one can demand that the magnetic monopole is not a Nariai BH, which yields a lower bound on the gauge coupling
Montero et al. 2021
:

g^{2}\geq\frac{3}{2}\left(\frac{H}{M_{\textnormal{P}}}\right)^{2}.

(251)

This bound assures that a Nariai solution exists, and that the magnetic monopole (which is a fundamental particle and not a BH) fits in the static dSpatch. Furthermore, this bound is equivalent to demanding that the EFTcutoff
gM_{\textnormal{P}}
from the flat-space magnetic WGCis not below the Hubble scale.

II.7.3
Evidence

The FLBwas first studied by
Montero et al. 2020b
, and later discussed in the context of
type II string theory
Guidetti et al. 2023
and
holography
Mishra 2023
.
A meticulous overview article is presented by
Montero et al. 2021
.
Hassan et al. 2025
provide a compact discussion and extend the FLBto more general
p
-forms.
Furthermore, relations to other swampland conjectures are highlighted in
sections

II.20.1
,
II.20.23
and
II.20.18
.

II.8
Finite Number of Massless Fields Conjecture

The number of massless fields that appear in a
d
-dimensional EFT, which is coupled to (Einstein) gravity, is finite
Brennan et al. 2018
.

II.8.1
Implications for Cosmology

Inflation

An aspect where the number of massless fields is of particular relevance is inflation. Some models explain the exponential inflation in the early universe by a collection of multiple fields, each described by an EoSof the form of

\ddot{\phi}+3H\dot{\phi}+V^{\prime}=0,

(252)

where each field experiences a restoring force from its potential, but also the Hubble friction
Dimopoulos et al. 2008
.
The Hubble term
3H^{2}=\sum_{\phi}V\left(\phi\right)
depends on the collection of scalar field potentials
Dimopoulos et al. 2008
. Models that require an infinite number of fields belong to the swampland.

As we will elaborate in
section

II.8.2
, a finite number of massless scalar fields is related to the finiteness of moduli space.
Cecotti 2020b
argues that a finite moduli volume should be understood as a condition for having a finite action. Requiring a finite action for the Universe has interesting consequences: If finiteness from the past to the future is demanded, the lifetime of the Universe has to be finite, the Universe needs to be spatially compact, and a cosmological constant, massless scalar fields, eternal inflation, the ekpyrotic scenario, as well as bounce and cyclic models are ruled out
Barrow 2020
. If only finiteness up to now, without making predictions about the future, is required, then inflation is allowed as a transient phase, eternal inflation is still ruled out, yet the ekpyrotic scenario (though requiring an infinite field displacement, which violates the DC) as well as cyclic models (though highly constrained and requiring a singular beginning) can be accommodated
Jonas et al. 2021
.

Dark Energy and Dark Matter

A finite number of vacua can rule out the anthropic principle
Marsh 2017
: The number of vacua simply might be too small to explain the necessary fine-tuning. This is in particular suggested for models where DMinteracts with DE. The variation in the DEdensity
\rho_{\Lambda}
is related to the variation in the DMmass as
\delta\rho_{\Lambda}\sim\delta m_{\text{DM}}m_{\text{DM}}^{3}
, which indicates that a small shift in DMmass yields a large shift in the DEdensity. For the anthropic principle to hold, the number of available vacua must be larger than the required fine-tuning.
Marsh 2017
finds fine-tuning of the order of
10^{10^{10}}
necessary for simple examples of DM–DEinteractions, which vastly exceeds even the most generous estimates of the number of string theory vacua of
{10}^{500}
for type IIB string theory or
{10}^{272\,000}
in F-theory
Taylor and Wang 2015
.
219
219

219

Going into the opposite direction,
Bousso et al. 2011
reason that the number of vacua that allow for observers to exist,
\mathfrak{N}
, is related to the time DEbegins to dominate,
t_{\Lambda}
, the time it takes for observers to exist,
t_{\textnormal{obs}}
, as well as to the timescale related to spatial curvature,
t_{\textnormal{c}}
:
\log t_{\Lambda}\approx\log t_{\textnormal{obs}}\approx\log t_{\textnormal{c}}\approx\log\sqrt{\mathfrak{N}}
— this yields
\mathfrak{N}\sim${10}^{123}$
, which is smaller than the number of flux vacua in string theory. Here, no coupling between DEand DMwas assumed. It is more to be understood as a naïve upper bound on the size of the landscape.

Observational evidence against the anthropic principle would include the observation of energy transfer between DEand DM, which constrains masses
220
220

220

If DMhas a decay channel into two gamma photons, the mass can be constrained
Marsh 2017
. If such a decay is present during a redshift range of
20<z<1100
, the CMBspectrum is damped at small scales due to the extra energy injection (which could ionise hydrogen and helium and increase the optical depth to recombination) — that such a damping is not observed puts upper bounds on the energy injection from this decay channel
Anchordoqui et al. 2024h
;
Slatyer and Wu 2017
.

, couplings, and the relative energy density of DM,
\Omega_{\text{DM}}
.
Making the anthropic principle implausible is not a problem per se, as a proper theory that predicts the observations might be preferred over the anthropic principle. Besides,
Dimopoulos et al. 2008
explain that having multiple fields could actually make fine-tuning obsolete, as the fields can dampen each other.

Tomboulis 2021
investigates if there are flat metric solutions in multigravity theory if there is a cosmological constant the size of the Planck mass
221
221

221

Multigravity needs Planck-sized cosmological constants (which arise from integrating out matter fields) in order to be consistent up to the Planck scale
Tomboulis 2021
.

and concludes that without fine-tuning there are no such solutions for a finite number of fields, but that there are flat solutions with an infinite-dimensional parameter space if there is an infinite number of fields. The fields considered are
n
massive spin-2 fields,
n+1
rank-2 tensor fields, but only one massless field. Having infinitely many fields indicates that the underlying fundamental theory is non-local
Tomboulis 2021
.

Particle physics

produced only a limited number of fundamental particles, which agrees well with the notion that there should only be a finite number of massless scalar fields, as every parameter entering the Lagrangian has to be viewed as the vacuum expectation value of a field
Brennan et al. 2018
;
Vafa 2019
.

II.8.2
General Remarks

In earlier work,
Vafa 2005
states that this conjecture was not based on a mathematical fact, rather on experience from working in string theory. A concern could be that there might be solutions that successfully describe QGbut are not realised in string theory. For 10 dimensions, it can be shown that this is not the case: every solution free of inconsistencies is realised in string theory
Adams et al. 2014
. Furthermore, there is more rigorous mathematical support for such a conjecture nowadays:

•

In all known examples, the dimension of the cohomology of the manifold is finite
Vafa 2005
. The cohomology class provides an upper bound for the number of massless modes on the manifold
Vafa 2005
.
222
222

222

Since there are no known string realisations without any extra scalar fields (there is always at least the string coupling or the volume modulus), a lower bound might also exist
Vafa 2005
.

•

For a
d
-dimensional theory, the rank of the gauge group is
r_{G}\leq 26-d

Kim et al. 2020
, ergo, the number of matter contents is finite
Montero and Vafa 2021
.

•

Compactness of moduli space.

The last point needs to be debated.
The idea that there is only a finite number of massless modes is based on the conjecture that for a specific number of dimensions (and supersymmetry), only a finite number of CYcompactifications exists
Tarazi and Vafa 2021
;
Heckman and Vafa 2019
.
223
223

223

The predominance of CYmanifolds in string theory can be explained by an observation by
Candelas et al. 1985
: To have chiral fermions and a number of supercharges that can reasonably be dealt with, a Ricci-flat manifold with SU(3) holonomy is sufficient
Tadros and Vilja 2022
— CYmanifolds are Ricci-flat Kähler manifolds, and as such are smooth manifolds with a complex structure and a Riemann metric, i.e. a scalar product exist, and the space is differentiable.

Blumenhagen et al. 2013
note that no exact CYmetric on a compact manifold has been constructed (only algorithmic
224
224

224

More recently also through machine learning
Anderson et al. 2021
.

approximations), whereas for non-compact manifolds, exact CYmetrics exist.
For a compact region of moduli space, the number of flux vacua is finite
Ashok and Douglas 2004
, respectively, a moduli space of finite volume has a finite number of different QGvacua
Vafa 2005
;
Hamada et al. 2022
;
Acharya and Douglas 2006
;
Douglas and Lu 2005
;
Kim et al. 2024
.
225
225

225

The number of massless scalars emerging from compactification is proportional to Hodge numbers of the internal manifold — it is assumed but not proven that there is an upper bound on Hodge numbers for compact CYmanifolds
Brennan et al. 2018
.

While
Douglas and Lu 2006
say that CYspaces are not compact,

Kumar and Wells 2021
mention that it is often assumed that the number of CYthreefolds is finite, even though it is not proven.
226
226

226

A finite number of CYthreefolds indicates a finite number of tuneable parameters in EFTsthat are part of the landscape
Heckman and Vafa 2019
.

Gross 1993
shows that there is only a finite number of CYmanifolds that admit an elliptic fibration
Kumar and Taylor 2009
, and the tameness conjecture (
section

II.16
) implies that there is only a finite number of topologically distinct compact CYmanifolds.
Further support comes from the DC: the moduli space for an EFTwith a finite cutoff scale must be compact, as otherwise infinite distance limits exist, where massless towers of states emerge that cause the breakdown of the EFT
Hamada et al. 2022
.
227
227

227

For example, if the moduli space of a probe brane is not compact, there is an infinite number of internal states, which violates the Bekenstein bound
Hamada et al. 2022
;
Bedroya et al. 2022
;
Hamada and Vafa 2021
.

The discussion could be resolved by having a finite landscape of theories and an infinite swampland
Kim et al. 2024
;
Taylor and Turner 2018
.
228
228

228

Having an infinite swampland and a finite landscape is to be expected: The landscape is spanned by the different moduli respectively the different compactifications that can be performed, leading to a discrete set, while the swampland contains all the possible choices of couplings or operator coefficients to build EFTs, which are continua
Hebecker 2023
.

Long et al. 2021
state that the following reasoning in favour of the conjecture that the number of massless fields is finite is incorrect (yet we will reason that their counterargument is toothless):
Given a cutoff scale for an EFT, it is reasonable that there is a finite number of string vacua that can act as a background, which also means that the number of massless or light species modes allowed in the theory is finite. Each such mode can end up in a BH, increasing its entropy. Without the cutoff, the number of such modes is infinite, which renders the BHentropy infinite as well.
According to
Long et al. 2021
, this reasoning is incorrect, as the BHentropy could be finite even in the case of an infinite number of modes:
The radius of a BHthat is fully described by the EFTis
r>1/\Lambda
.
The entropy of such a BHis given by
\mathcal{S}>\left(M_{\textnormal{P}}/\Lambda\right)^{d-2}
and should be bigger than the number of species in the theory, as each mode can be a constituent of the BH:
\mathcal{S}>N_{\textnormal{S}}
.
It follows that there is no contradiction between an infinite number of species and the finiteness of BHentropy if
N_{\textnormal{S}}<\left(M_{\textnormal{P}}/\Lambda\right)^{d-2}
respectively
\Lambda\leq\Lambda_{\textnormal{S}}=M_{\textnormal{P}}/N_{\textnormal{S}}^{1/\left(d-2\right)}
holds
Dvali and Redi 2008
;
Dvali 2010a
;
Dvali and Gomez 2010
.
However, we’d like to point out that in this case the cutoff scale of the EFTis going to zero, which makes the EFTinvalid everywhere, effectively ruling out EFTswith an infinite number of massless fields.

For AdSspace, there is an argument against an infinite number of massless fields invoking the AdSDC, presented by
Lust et al. 2019
:
String theory backgrounds of the form AdS
{}_{d}\crossproduct S^{D-d}/Z_{k}
allow for an infinite number of massless fields by sending
k\rightarrow\infty
. In the absence of separation of scales,
S^{D-d}
cannot be made arbitrarily small compared to the AdS
d
radius. The AdSDCstates that there is no separation of scales. Therefore,
S^{D-d}
is always part of the full spacetime, and the infinite number of massless modes is localised on the codimension-
{\left(D-d\right)}
defect, which is allowed in QG. This is not possible in pure AdSspace. This leaves us with two possible conclusions: either, AdSspace cannot be purely flat, i.e. there is always an internal space
Y_{p}
, such that AdS
{}_{d}\crossproduct Y_{p}\rightarrow\text{Mink}_{d}
, or, there is only a finite number of massless fields.

How could an infinite number of vacua even emerge?

Acharya and Douglas 2006
state three possibilities:

Topology

The extra-dimensions could be described by an infinite number of different topologies.

Fields

The solutions to the EoMcould be infinitely numerous, ranging from different metrics to brane configurations or gauge fields.

Fluxes

The number of fluxes could be infinite.

Regarding topologies, there is a theorem by
Cheeger 1970
, according to which a sequence of Riemannian manifolds with sectional curvatures
229
229

229

Without the bound, there might not be an EFTfor the specific compactification of the manifold
Hamada et al. 2022
.

and diameters bounded from above and volumes bounded from below,
230
230

230

Without bounds on the diameters and the volumes, infinite towers of states emerge in the infinite distance limits, causing the EFTsto break down
Hamada et al. 2022
.

has only a finite number of diffeomorphisms
Hamada et al. 2022
.
This implies that there can only be a finite number of topologies in a sequence, if the manifolds have a non-zero volume and are not too elongated, and if all components of the Riemann tensor are bounded.

Acharya and Douglas 2006
present explicit examples of infinite sequences of Einstein 7-manifolds as infinite topologies that fail because of Cheeger’s theorem:
On the one hand, all components of the Riemann curvature tensor are bounded because every closed geodesic is longer than the string scale. As long as strings have a non-zero length, the volume of the extra dimensions will always be positive, i.e. there is a lower bound on the volume. If a manifold is too elongated, it had an infinite diameter, which contradicts the DC.
On the other hand, an infinite sequence of manifolds with non-negative volume leads to infinite volumes in the curled-up dimensions, i.e. a contradiction in itself, since the extra-dimensions are defined to be small, which means that they cannot be infinitely large. Only a finite number leads to finite volumes of the extra dimensions.

Regarding the number of fields and fluxes,
Acharya and Douglas 2006
point out that distinct solutions are distinct points in moduli space, but if they lead to the same EoS, they should be close together, so close in fact that they do not physically present different vacua. We think that this is not necessarily true and that a more elaborate proof is needed. On the one hand, largely separated points in moduli space could represent theories that have aspects that cancel each other in the EoS. On the other hand, even a small shift in moduli space could mean a large difference — think of phase transitions
Acharya and Douglas 2006
.
Moreover,
Bakker et al. 2023
show that in order to exclude the pathological examples of
Acharya and Douglas 2006
, it suffices to show the definability of the period mapping in the complex structure moduli space.
Furthermore, support from a broader string-theoretical context for a finite number of fields and especially fluxes is listed in
section

II.8.3
.

What goes wrong at infinity?

An infinite number of fields within an EFTdoes not have the same implications as an infinite number of different vacua in moduli space.
On the one hand, the latter might not be too problematic, as the real world would only occupy a finite ball in the infinite-dimensional parameter space, with a radius shrinking with more precise experimental data
Kumar 2006
, i.e. the landscape would be finite, while the swampland would be infinite.
On the other hand, an infinite number of vacua would imply a non-compact configuration space, and a diverging partition function, as scalar fields probe all the vacua, even if the vacua are disconnected (which would contradict the cobordism conjecture (
section

II.2
))
Hamada et al. 2022
. This is also supported by Cheeger’s theorem
Cheeger 1970
, as well as by the work of
Cecotti 2020b
, who argues that a finite moduli volume should be understood as a condition for having a finite action.

An infinite number of fields in an EFTis more problematic: it leads to non-renormalisability, infinite entropy, and diverging partition functions.
(i) If there is an infinite number of couplings, Wilsonian renormalisability is not given
Chaudhuri 2005
.
(ii) The entropy of a system is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{N}T^{3}L^{3}

(253)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{N}^{1/4}E^{3/4}L^{3/4}

(254)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{N}^{1/4}L^{3/2},

(255)

with
\mathfrak{N}
the number of fields,
T
the temperature,
L
the size of the system,
E=\mathfrak{N}T^{4}L^{3}
the energy in the system
Hamada et al. 2022
, which implies that an infinite number of fields yields an infinite entropy (which is also true for a system of infinite extent).
(iii)
A partition function

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathfrak{N}=-\phi_{\text{max}}\sqrt{\Lambda/2\pi^{2}}}^{\phi_{\text{max}}\sqrt{\Lambda/2\pi^{2}}}e^{-2\mathfrak{N}^{2}\pi^{2}/\phi_{\text{max}}T}

(256)

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{\phi_{\text{max}}}{\sqrt{2\pi^{2}}}\int_{-\sqrt{\Lambda}}^{\sqrt{\Lambda}}\!e^{-p^{2}/T}\,\mathrm{d}p

(257)

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{\phi_{\text{max}}}{\sqrt{2\pi/T}}\erf\left(\sqrt{\Lambda/T}\right),

(258)

with
T
the temperature,

\phi_{\text{max}}
a maximum field value (the field could be periodic with
\phi\simeq\phi+\phi_{\text{max}}
),
and
\Lambda
the theory cutoff, might diverge if the number of fields,
\mathfrak{N}
, is infinite, unless the DC, the SSC, and the WGCare satisfied, such that
\phi_{\text{max}}
is finite and that
\Lambda(\phi_{\text{max}})\leq 1/\phi_{\text{max}}^{2}

Hamada et al. 2022
.

Studying charged BHs,
Hong et al. 2010
find strong cosmic censorship and BHcomplementarity
231
231

231

They show that an observer could see a quantum clone of information: once as in-falling and once in the form of Hawking radiation, if there is an exponentially large number of fields present in the theory.

violated, if the number of massless fields is exponentially large. In this case, they were able to extend semi-classical results behind the inner horizon, which indicates that the singularity becomes observable and the strong CCCis violated. A finite number of massless fields conserves cosmic censorship, as trans-Planckian curvatures occur around the horizon, keeping the singularity a physical singularity, while the breakdown of the EFTis guaranteed, making it compatible with our expectations about QG.

II.9
Gravitino Swampland Conjecture

The sound speed
234
234

234

‘In a general theory of many interacting fields, the scalar sound speed
c_{\textnormal{s}}
may be understood as the determinant of the matrix of sound speeds of all fields kinetically coupled to the gravitino and with mass below the UVcutoff’

Kolb et al. 2021a
;
Kolb et al. 2021b
.
of gravitino(s) must be positive-definite,
c^{2}_{\textnormal{s}}>0
, at all points in moduli space and for all initial conditions, in all 4d effective field theories that are low-energy limits of quantum gravity
Kolb et al. 2021b
.

II.9.1
Implications for Cosmology

Kolb et al. 2021a
present a null hypothesis and three possible outcomes, based on the GSC:
The null hypothesis is that there is
‘a constant mass gravitino, no light superpartners at the end of inflation that kinetically couple with the gravitino, and a conventional inflationary thermal history.’

The three possible outcomes are:

•

The gravitino is observed. This will make it impossible to observe the B-mode polarization of the CMB.

•

B-mode polarization of the CMBis observed. Then, it will be impossible to observe a gravitino in collider experiments.
235
235

235

Contrary
Cribiori et al. 2021a
, which state that the detection of B-modes just sets a lower bound of
100\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
for the gravitino mass.

•

Gravitinos and B-mode polarization are observed. The null hypothesis has to be rejected.

II.9.2
General Remarks

For simple models of cosmic acceleration in dS, the gravitino is well described by Rarita–Schwinger
Rarita and Schwinger 1941
models, in which the sound speed of the gravitino is given by

c_{\textnormal{s}}^{2}=\left(\frac{m_{\textnormal{3/2}}^{2}-\omega H^{2}}{m_{\textnormal{3/2}}^{2}+H^{2}}\right)^{2},

(259)

such that, in a radiation dominated universe, the sound speed vanishes when
H=\sqrt{3}m_{\textnormal{3/2}}

Kolb et al. 2021a
.
The given definition of the GSCreduces to the statement that the gravitino mass
236
236

236

The gravitino mass is sometimes interpreted as a measure of the supersymmetry breaking scale
Anchordoqui et al. 2023d

\Lambda_{\text{SUSY}}^{2}\simeq m_{\textnormal{3/2}}M_{\textnormal{P}}
— the absence of supersymmetry in particle accelerators and a high gravitino mass go hand in hand
Cribiori 2022
.

m_{\textnormal{3/2}}
has to be above the value of the Hubble constant at the end of inflation
H_{\textnormal{e}}
:
237
237

237

Generally, the following holds: if
m<H
, i.e. when the KKtower drops below the Hubble scale, the EFTwould experience decompactification, which means that it is no longer a FLRWcosmology. If there is no KKtower, but strings, then the universe would undergo a stringy phase when
m<H
, so, again, the EFTis no longer applicable, but a string theory is needed
Rudelius 2023a
.

m_{\textnormal{3/2}}>H_{\textnormal{e}}

(260)

for certain, but not necessarily all, supergravity models
Kolb et al. 2021a
;
Kolb et al. 2021b
.

What happens if the gravitino is too light?

The massive spin-3/2 state (a gravitino) carries a helicity-3/2 state and a helicity-1/2 state with decoupled EoM.
For
m_{\textnormal{3/2}}<H
the sound speed of the helicity-1/2 gravitino vanishes, which causes a divergent production of gravitationally produced particles with arbitrarily high wavenumber
k

Kolb et al. 2021b
(since with vanishing sound speed, the energy per field excitation is independent of the momentum
Kolb et al. 2021a
).

The particle number of non-relativistic particles at late times is a physical observable, and a divergent production of gravitinos is therefore pathological
Kolb et al. 2021b
.
However, there are supergravities free of this pathology
Kolb et al. 2021b
, e.g. a cubic nilpotent superfield studied by
Terada 2021
.

Dudas et al. 2021
find that neither
c_{\textnormal{s}}=0
is a general feature of supergravities, nor that
c_{\textnormal{s}}=0
always causes catastrophic gravitino production:
A nilpotent superfield eliminates a scalar from the theory and can be seen as an IRlimit of a heavy sgoldstino (the scalar field associated with supersymmetry breaking)
Ferrara et al. 2016
.
The mixing of the inflatino and the goldstino prevents the catastrophic gravitino production
Antoniadis et al. 2021
;
Hasegawa et al. 2017
.
The culprit is an additional constraint on the higher-derivative operators, which eliminates the pseudoscalar, fermionic, and auxiliary partners of the inflaton
Ferrara et al. 2016
;
Dall’Agata et al. 2016
— these constraints introduce additional pathologies such as
c_{\textnormal{s}}>1
.

This catastrophic gravitino problem differs from the conventional gravitino problem
Khlopov and Linde 1984
;
Ellis et al. 1984
, as the catastrophic particle production is driven by UVmodes and the conventional gravitino problem takes place during inflation/the period of reheating after inflation
Eberl et al. 2021
;
Nilles et al. 2001a
;
Nilles et al. 2001b
;
Dalianis and Farakos 2017
, i.e. within the IREFT
Kolb et al. 2021b
.

What supports the GSC?

The GSCdemands that the gravitino mass is above the Hubble scale.
Other swampland conjectures rule out a vanishing gravitino mass:
The FLBsays that charged gravitinos cannot be massless.
The no non-supersymmetric theories conjecture (
section

II.12
) says that the superpotential
W
in
\mathcal{N}=1
supergravities cannot vanish, but this also means that the gravitino mass cannot vanish, as
m_{\textnormal{3/2}}^{2}=e^{K}W\bar{W}
(
K
the Kähler potential)
Cribiori 2022
.

Cribiori et al. 2021a
show that the limit of vanishing gravitino mass corresponds to an infinite tower of states, a behaviour we know from the DCthat indicates the breakdown of the EFT(
section

II.5
).

Cribiori 2022
shows that for some models, the gravitino mass vanishes in the points of infinite volume — the same points where a global symmetry (
section

II.10
) is restored.

Cribiori et al. 2021b
show that at dScritical points the determinant of the gravitino mass matrix vanishes, and that the WGCputs these points in the swampland.
Also
Coudarchet et al. 2021
find inconsistencies if the gravitino mass vanishes.

Is the relation between the gravitino mass and the Hubble scale an exact inequality?

No, the inequality as presented in
eq.

260
certainly has missing
\order{1}
parameters. However, we can gain more insights using the DC(see text around
eq.

154
).

Furthermore,
for supersymmetric AdSvacua, there is a direct relation between the gravitino mass and the cosmological constant

\Lambda_{\textnormal{cc}}\geq-3m_{\textnormal{3/2}}^{2}
,
238
238

238

Castellano et al. 2021
argue that the bound is saturated and the expression is an equality, whereas
Cribiori et al. 2021a
state it as a bound, which would allow for finite gravitino mass in the
\absolutevalue{\Lambda}\rightarrow 0
limit.

and it follows that
m\sim m_{\textnormal{3/2}}^{\mathfrak{g}}\sim\absolutevalue{\Lambda}^{\mathfrak{g}/2}

Castellano et al. 2021
;
Cribiori et al. 2021a
.
239
239

239

The parameter
\mathfrak{g}
is defined in
section

II.5
around
eq.

154
.

The last expression strongly resembles the AdSDCfrom
section

II.1

Cribiori et al. 2021a
;
Lust et al. 2019
(even though it is on an entirely different background and yields different predictions
Cribiori 2022
).
240
240

240

The GSCapplies to AdS, Minkowski, and dS.
On the one hand, the AdSDCdoes not require the gravitino mass to go to zero in the limit of vanishing potential. It could be another tower, and the gravitino mass could remain finite in this limit
Cribiori et al. 2021a
.
On the other hand, the GSCis independent of the potential (when supersymmetry is broken)
Cribiori et al. 2021a
.
However, the limit
m_{\textnormal{3/2}}\rightarrow 0
always implies that the potential vanishes as well
Cribiori et al. 2021a
.

Evidence

The GSCwas introduced by
Kolb et al. 2021b
;

and studied in the context of

II.10
No Global Symmetries Conjecture

In the presence of gravity, there are no continuous global symmetries
Banks and Dixon 1988
.

In this article, the no global symmetries conjecture is an argument we often refer to, e.g. when mentioning that an infinite distance limit or the limit of vanishing coupling is problematic because a global symmetry arises in such a limit.
241
241

241

For example,
Cordova et al. 2022a
, who motivate the DCas well as the WGCfrom the no global symmetries conjecture, and

Montero et al. 2021
show that if the no global symmetries conjecture (
section

II.10
) is violated in supergravities, the FLBis violated as well.

Yet, the conjecture that there are no global symmetries in
QG
is not necessarily itself a
swampland
conjecture, as it does not really constrain EFTs: An EFTcan have global symmetries. It is only expected that those symmetries are explicitly broken or gauged at higher energies
Graña and Herráez 2021
;
Lee et al. 2018
;
Montero et al. 2017
.
242
242

242

This is not a string theory–exclusive result, e.g. unbroken chiral symmetries are discussed in loop QG
Barnett and Smolin 2015
;
Gambini and Pullin 2015
, and truncated renormalisation group flows in asymptotic safety scenarios in Euclidean space show no symmetry-violating terms
Eichhorn and Gies 2011
;
Eichhorn and Lippoldt 2017
;
Eichhorn and Held 2017
;
Eichhorn et al. 2018b
;
Eichhorn and Pauly 2021
, yet at higher order or in Lorentzian spacetime, these symmetries might well be broken.

Still, given the importance of the conjecture for the swampland programme, we would like to make a few humble comments, without attempting to deliver a complete picture or comprehensive review.

II.10.1
Implications for Cosmology

Some ideas rely on the existence of global symmetries, e.g. global or semilocal strings, global monopoles, textures
Burgess et al. 2008a
;
Kamionkowski and March-Russell 1992
, and domain walls, which can either be ruled out based on the no global symmetries conjecture alone, or in combination with observational data
Afzal et al. 2023
.

Axions

might be required in QGto break global symmetries
Draper et al. 2022
;
McNamara and Vafa 2019
;
Heidenreich et al. 2021a
.

Black holes

are expected to decay via Hawking radiation and their entropy is expected to be finite. Moreover, they are expected to have no hair. Furthermore, it is expected that there are no naked singularities.
None of those expectations would be met if there were global symmetries.

If we had a continuous global symmetry, there would be BHscarrying global charge.
If BHsevaporate via Hawking radiation, the evaporation process is unitary and consistent with the Bekenstein–Hawking entropy formula (
S=A/4G
)
Harlow and Shaghoulian 2021
.
Since Hawking radiation is insensitive to charge
243
243

243

The degree of global symmetry violation by Hawking radiation can be quantified using relative entropies
Chen and Lin 2021
.

,
either charge conservation is violated
Brennan et al. 2018
, or
we would end up with a remnant
244
244

244

We make more remarks on remnants in
section

II.19.2
.

carrying the global charge
Harlow et al. 2023
;
Rudelius and Shao 2020
;
Banks and Seiberg 2011
;
Zeldovich 1976
;
Hawking 1975
.
245
245

245

This would happen in a finite time and might already be observable after the Page time (see
Almheiri et al. 2019
;
Almheiri et al. 2020
;
Penington 2020
;
Penington et al. 2020
), i.e. it is not necessary to wait until the BHfully evaporates
Chen and Lin 2021
.

Such a remnant represents a naked singularity, which is incompatible with the CCC
246
246

246

The CCCis a classical conjecture about GRand not a string-theoretical swampland conjecture, and is therefore not treated in this review. However, a brief discussion can be found in
section

II.19.1
in the context of the WGC.

.
Each remnant corresponds to a microstate in the theory.
The BHinformation paradox tells us that a BHof finite size can only hold a finite amount of information, and therefore the number of microstates is finite
247
247

247

The Coleman–Mermin–Wagner theorem
Coleman 1973
;
Mermin and Wagner 1966
states that there is no spontaneous symmetry breaking of global symmetries in vacuum states or thermal states
Montero et al. 2017
. The theorem applies to the IR of the Euclidean solution — where a BHcan be regarded as a thermal state (despite the Hawking radiation) —, and predicts that the expectation value of any charged operator vanishes, which in turn renders the global charge completely unobservable outside the BHand the BHmicrostate infinitely degenerate
Montero et al. 2017
.

as well — with a continuous global charge we would end up with an infinite number of different remnants and therefore infinite entropy
Harlow and Shaghoulian 2021
;
Banks and Seiberg 2011
;
Bousso 1999b
;
Reece 2023
;
Harlow and Ooguri 2019a
;
Hsin et al. 2021
.
248
248

248

This argument about BHthermodynamics does not only rule out continuous global symmetries, but applies, in a similar fashion, to non-compact continuous gauge symmetries as well, which indicates that Abelian gauge groups in QGare U(1) and not
\mathbb{R}
, ergo, electric charge is quantised
Reece 2023
.
As a further aside, we note that gravitons are neutral under the global symmetry, which means that gravitational bremsstrahlung cannot radiate away the global charge either
van Beest et al. 2022
.

A too-naive way out of the dilemma is to assume that global symmetries exist but that it is Hawking radiation, which is the culprit and the wrong description of the decay process:
the global charge of a BHis a one-point function of a simple operator — being wrong about this indicates that the EFTcannot be trusted at all
Reece 2023
.

Reece 2023
advances the notion that it is more likely to discard continuous global symmetries than to abandon several decades of successful and self-consistent research about BHthermodynamics. We would like to raise the point that paradigmatic shifts keep happening in physics. Fortunately, there are stronger arguments against continuous global symmetries and this is probably not the paradigmatic shift we require.

Besides continuous symmetries, we could also consider discrete global symmetries, as in this case, the number of stable remnants might be large but finite. However, at least in the context of AdS/CFT
249
249

249

In AdS/CFT, BHevaporation is unitary
Harlow and Shaghoulian 2021
.

Harlow and Ooguri 2019b
;
Harlow and Ooguri 2019a
, the compatibility with discrete global symmetries with QGis generally questioned
Harlow and Shaghoulian 2021
.
250
250

250

QGsin less than
\left(3+1\right)
dimensions with global symmetries do not have BHsdescribed by the Bekenstein–Hawking entropy, ergo, the argument does not apply
Harlow and Shaghoulian 2021
.

Another issue that arises in the context of global charges and BHsis the no-hair theorem. To be compatible with the no-hair theorem, the global charge is only allowed to show as quantum hair
Dvali and Gomez 2013b
.
251
251

251

Quantum hair arises when a continuous gauge group is Higgsed to a discrete subgroup
Dvali and Redi 2008
;
Krauss and Wilczek 1989
.
Classically, one can build BHswith arbitrary charge
Q=\int_{S^{2}}\!B

Montero et al. 2017
:
If we have a field
B=Q\mathrm{d}\Omega
with
Q
the charge and
\mathrm{d}\Omega
the unit sphere
S^{2}
volume form, where
B
is not a local observable, no issue arises, as any contractible region can be gauged away.
Furthermore, quantum hair from massive higher-spin gauge fields can be realised
Dvali 2006a
;
Dvali 2006b
;
Dvali and Redi 2008
.
The charges related to Aharonov–Bohm phases are only detectable quantum-mechanically
Dvali 2008
;
Dvali 2006c
.

This could mean that the no-hair theorem is only semi-classically true, but not in QG, respectively it only shows at higher energy scales.
Without being able to measure the charge, the uncertainty regarding that charge is infinite, which means that the entropy is also infinite, which is in contradiction with our expectation that the entropy of a BHis finite and related to its mass squared
Palti 2019
.

Contrary to the presented points,
Dvali and Gußmann 2016
argue that semi-classical arguments based on BHremnants do not hold: global charges can be conserved by BHsand show as classical skyrmion hair.
252
252

252

A skyrmion BHis a baryonic BHwhere the BHhorizon is inside the baryon
Dvali and Gußmann 2016
.

This does not rule out the no global symmetries conjecture, but it voids arguments in favour of it based on semi-classical BHphysics.
Also
Dvali and Gomez 2013b
argue that the BHargument cannot be used to rule out global symmetries, as higher-order quantum effects always allow deducing the global charge of a BH.

To summarise, we can say that throwing global charge in a BHcan lead to three different outcomes:

•

The BHswallows the global charge and keeps it, eventually leading to stable remnants, at odds with BHthermodynamics.

•

The BHdestroys the global charge, being at odds with charge conservation.

•

The BHradiates the global charge away, being at odds with the no-hair theorem.

Inflation

Some models of inflation might show (approximate) global symmetries regarding some aspect of the model, e.g. Starobinsky inflation has a global scale invariance related to the
R^{2}
-term, which is broken by the Einstein–Hilbert term in the action
Ketov 2024
.

Wormholes

Early work by
Kallosh et al. 1995
indicates that pure Einstein-gravity in 4d is incompatible with global symmetries, but that the addition of axions or stringy effects might remove those incompatibilities, allowing for global symmetries in the context of wormholes.

If one includes Euclidean wormholes — closed connected spatial components of the universe (baby universes
253
253

253

A baby universe carries only a global charge, no gauge charge
McNamara and Vafa 2020
;
Coleman 1988
.

) — in
the gravitational path integral, global charge can end up in those regions, inaccessible to the observer in the parent universe, which violates charge conservation
Giddings and Strominger 1988
;
Abbott and Wise 1989
;
Kallosh et al. 1995
;
Harlow et al. 2023
;
Hsin et al. 2021
. This is not an issue for gauge charge, as gauge charge must be zero in the closed universe
Harlow et al. 2023
.
254
254

254

If the no global symmetries conjecture respectively the cobordism conjecture (
section

II.2
) hold, the Hilbert space of a baby universe is one-dimensional
Agmon et al. 2023
.

II.10.2
General Remarks

Between which symmetries should we distinguish?

Global symmetries describe unitary transformations of quantum states and act non-trivially on the Hilbert space
Graña and Herráez 2021
;
van Beest et al. 2022
and commute with the Hamiltonian
Prieto 2024
, while gauge symmetries describe mathematical redundancies in the description of the Hilbert space of the theory
Fichet and Saraswat 2020
.
A continuous symmetry leads to a conserved worldsheet current, which in turn serves as the vertex operator for a gauge boson
Banks and Dixon 1988
;
Fichet and Saraswat 2020
.
A symmetry can be discrete or continuous, and it can be gauged or spontaneously
255
255

255

Spontaneously broken symmetries keep their topological operators, which we assume to be forbidden in QG
Heidenreich et al. 2021a
.

or explicitly broken.
256
256

256

Montero et al. 2017
reason that Chern–Simons terms — describing the most generic breaking mechanism — must be present in a theory that can be consistently extended into the UV.

García-Valdecasas 2023
opposes this idea and states that Chern–Simons terms are only a signal of non-invertibility of symmetries, not of their breaking.

In particle physics, ((continuous) global) symmetries play an important role, as the couplings and interactions are determined by (approximate (continuous)) global symmetries
Burgess et al. 2008a
, and the very weak couplings of local/gauge symmetries can be treated as global symmetries
Cicoli et al. 2023a
;
Harlow et al. 2023
;
Daus et al. 2020
;
Fichet and Saraswat 2020
.

Where do global symmetries come from?

Models with approximate global symmetries are not continuous deformations of a theory with exact global symmetries, instead, such models emerge as EFTs, where the small parameters that describe the approximate global symmetries are of dynamical origin and not free parameters
Fichet and Saraswat 2020
.
Global symmetries often emerge in the infinite distance limit of an EFT, but are censored by the appearance of an infinite tower of light states
Lee et al. 2018
.
257
257

257

A behaviour known from the DC.

A global symmetry can arise from compactification. Compactifying a
D
-dimensional theory with a
\left(p+1\right)
-form to
d=p+3
dimensions can lead to global symmetries
Montero et al. 2017
. This makes the actual application of the no global symmetries conjecture as a swampland conjecture troublesome, as it doesn’t allow us to rule out a theory of QGfrom the EFTperspective.
Yet, it highlights why global symmetries on an EFT-level are gauge symmetries on the full QG-level
Brennan et al. 2018
:
Global symmetries usually originate from symmetries of compact extra dimensions, which are gauged since diffeomorphisms of the internal space are part of the gravitational gauge symmetry (cf.
Tadros and Vilja 2022
;
Polchinski 1998c
).

To what degree are global symmetries allowed in EFTs?

The WGCprovides us with a breaking scale, respectively a limit for the
‘globalness’
of a symmetry.
The size of the symmetry-violating effects in the IR EFTare estimated to be
\exp\left(-8\pi^{2}M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}/\Lambda\right)
with
\Lambda
the cutoff scale of the EFT
Draper et al. 2022
;
Kallosh et al. 1995
;
Fichet and Saraswat 2020
;
Daus et al. 2020
. This gives rise to the critique that this conjecture does not yield meaningful phenomenological constraints, as the gauging / breaking scale is not specified
Graña and Herráez 2021
.
Furthermore, there are examples of lower-dimensional theories with global symmetries
Borissova et al. 2024
;
Harlow and Shaghoulian 2021
.
258
258

258

But those theories are no QG-candidates to actually describe our Universe
Borissova et al. 2024
.

What are the holographic arguments against global symmetries?

If we have a CFTwith a global symmetry, it will have a massless particle that corresponds to the gauge field of that symmetry, but this means that the symmetry is local in spacetime and not global
Banks and Dixon 1988
;
Cicoli et al. 2023a
, a contradiction with the initial assumption that the symmetry was global, ergo, global symmetries on CFTsare self-contradictory.

Harlow and Ooguri 2019a
;
Harlow and Ooguri 2019b
use the AdS/CFTcorrespondence to show that there are no global symmetries in (holographic) QG. This agrees with the notion that a global symmetry on the worldsheet is always gauged away in the target space
Palti 2019
;
Banks and Dixon 1988
.
259
259

259

A global symmetry on the string world-sheet would lead to global spacetime symmetries, which do not exist
Banks and Seiberg 2011
.

This does not mean that QGcannot have gauge symmetries. However,
Palti 2019
notes that a
‘theory with a gauge symmetry, coupled to gravity, must have states of all possible charges (consistent with Dirac quantization) under the gauge symmetry.’
260
260

260

This is the CC.
