.

II.16
Tameness Conjecture

A valid EFTbelow a given cutoff scale is labelled by a definable parameter space, and has a scalar field space and coupling functions that are definable under an o-minimal structure, which is
\mathbb{R}_{\text{an,exp}}

Grimm et al. 2022a
;
Grimm 2021b
;
Douglas et al. 2023
.
306
306

306

\mathbb{R}_{\text{an,exp}}
is an o-minimal structure generated by
\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}
— the semi-algebraic set of all polynomial (in)equalities —, i.e.
\mathbb{R}_{\text{an,exp}}
is the structure of all restricted real analytic functions
\mathbb{R}_{\text{an}}
, and the real exponential function
\exp:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

Grimm 2021b
.
The set of all restricted real analytic functions and exponential polynomial equations and its projections generate this structure
Grimm 2021b
, which can be understood as the subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
described by the polynomials
P\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},f_{1},\dots,f_{m},e^{x_{1}},\dots,e^{x_{m}}\right)=0
, with
f_{i}
a restricted real analytic function
Grimm et al. 2022a
.
An analytic function is a function that at any point of its domain can be expressed as a power series that converges at that point
Douglas et al. 2024
.
A structure is o-minimal if every definable subset of
\mathbb{R}
is a finite union of points and open intervals
Grimm 2021b
;
Douglas et al. 2023
;
Douglas et al. 2024
.

II.16.1
Implications for Cosmology

Scalar field potentials have only a finite number of minima, as tame functions do not allow for an infinite number of oscillations by definition
Grimm 2021b
.
307
307

307

Tame functions do not show pathological behaviour like infinite oscillations or accumulation of singularities, for example,
V(\phi)=\sin\left(\phi^{-1}\right)
cannot appear in a string compactification
Grimm 2021b
;
Douglas et al. 2024
.

II.16.2
General Remarks

The tameness conjecture imposes finiteness constraints on the geometry of the EFT
Grimm et al. 2022a
,
308
308

308

The parameter space of an EFTis not only assumed to exist in general, but also to be restricted, as certain components, e.g. infinite discrete components like a lattice, are excluded
Grimm et al. 2022a
.

i.e. it constrains all the couplings
g

Grimm 2021b
, the field space metrics
G_{ab}
, the scalar potentials
V
, kinetic matrices
Grimm et al. 2022a
, as well as all the correlators, partitions
Lanza 2024
, amplitudes, and so forth
Douglas et al. 2023
. A reason to expect physical theories to be tame stems from the observation that observables are usually real analytic functions or (a finite number of) phase transitions
Douglas et al. 2023
.

Some authors state that assessing the tameness of a theory requires knowledge about the UVproperties of said theory
Douglas et al. 2023
;
Grimm 2021b
;
Douglas et al. 2024
. The top-down prediction is that an EFTwith a finite cutoff that is part of the landscape is tame
Douglas et al. 2023
, since, as long as the number of loop corrections is finite, tameness is preserved when integrating out heavy fields
Douglas et al. 2024
.
309
309

309

Feynman amplitudes are tame
Douglas et al. 2024
.

An EFTthat is not tame is therefore certainly in the swampland, whereas an EFTthat is tame might or might not be in the landscape.

II.16.3
Evidence

The tameness conjecture is proposed by
Grimm 2021b
,
and discussed in the context of
type IIB string theory
Grimm et al. 2022a
;
Grimm et al. 2022b
,
QFTs
Douglas et al. 2024
,
QFTsand CFTs
Douglas et al. 2023
, and using
machine learning techniques
Lanza 2024
.
Furthermore, relations to other swampland conjectures are highlighted in
sections

II.20.32
,
II.20.7
and
II.20.6
.

II.17
Trans–Planckian Censorship Conjecture

The TCCis presented by
Bedroya and Vafa 2020
:
Trans-Planckian quantum fluctuations remain quantum.
Trans-Planckian quantum fluctuations should always remain smaller than the Hubble horizon, and therefore do not freeze in an expanding universe. Otherwise, there would be classical observations of trans-Planckian quantum modes.
For a phase of accelerated expansion, this means that
a mode with physical wavelength
\lambda<l_{\textnormal{P}}=1/M_{\textnormal{P}}
at
t_{\textnormal{i}}
will always remain smaller than the Hubble scale and the following bound on length scales leaving the horizon holds:

\displaystyle\frac{a_{\textnormal{f}}}{a_{\textnormal{i}}}

\displaystyle<\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{H_{\textnormal{f}}}

(380)

\displaystyle\int_{t_{\textnormal{i}}}^{t_{\textnormal{f}}}\!H(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle<\log\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{H_{\textnormal{f}}},

(381)

with
a_{\textnormal{f}}
and
H_{\textnormal{f}}
the scale factor respectively the Hubble factor at the end of the phase, and
a_{\textnormal{i}}
the scale factor at the beginning of the phase
Shlivko 2023
;
Heisenberg et al. 2021
;
Bedroya 2022
;
Das and Ramos 2020
;
Brandenberger 2021a
;
Bedroya and Vafa 2020
;
Blumenhagen et al. 2020b
.
This is a global, non-local statement, i.e. it must be true everywhere
Blumenhagen et al. 2020b
.
As it should be with any swampland conjecture, this statement becomes trivial when gravity decouples, i.e. for
l_{\textnormal{P}}\rightarrow 0
respectively
M_{\textnormal{P}}\rightarrow\infty
.

II.17.1
Implications for Cosmology

An important quantity in the TCCis the Hubble parameter. Since
H
corresponds to the energy density in the Universe, the bound
H<M_{\textnormal{P}}
should naturally hold at all times
Bedroya and Vafa 2020
.
In models without accelerating expansion, the Hubble radius grows faster than the wavelength of any mode, i.e. the TCCis always satisfied
Brandenberger 2021a
.
310
310

310

For example, in string gas cosmology
Brandenberger and Vafa 1989
, the early phase of the universe is quasistatic, and in the ekpyrotic scenario
Khoury et al. 2001
(see
section

II.4.1
), it is contracting, ergo, the TCCconstraints are easily satisfied
Bedroya et al. 2020a
.

The same holds for emergent cosmologies or scenarios with a bounce
Brandenberger and Peter 2017
;
Finelli and Brandenberger 2002
;
Gasperini and Veneziano 1993
;
Khoury et al. 2002
;
Battarra et al. 2014
;
Bramberger et al. 2017
;
Farnsworth et al. 2017
;
Ijjas and Steinhardt 2017
;
Gielen and Turok 2016
;
Easson et al. 2011
;
Creminelli and Senatore 2007
;
Buchbinder et al. 2007
, as long as the energy scale of the bounce respectively the energy scale of the emergent phase is below the Planck scale
Brandenberger 2021a
;
Bedroya et al. 2020a
.
311
311

311

Modes leaving the Hubble radius have a length of
\lambda\sim M_{\textnormal{P}}/\Lambda^{2}
, with
\Lambda
the energy scale of the bounce respectively the emergence scale
Brandenberger 2021a
.

In the following, we study cases where the TCCis more constraining.

Lifetime

The TCClimits the lifetime of a metastable dSsolutions to be
Bedroya and Vafa 2020
;
Rasulian et al. 2021

\tau<\frac{1}{H_{\textnormal{f}}}\log\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{H_{\textnormal{f}}};

(382)

since

\dot{H}=-\frac{\left(1+w\right)\rho}{\left(d-2\right)},

(383)

H
is monotonically decreasing with time for an EoSparameter
w>-1
, such that the TCCimplies

\displaystyle H_{\textnormal{f}}\left(t_{\textnormal{f}}-t_{\textnormal{i}}\right)\leq\int^{t_{\textnormal{f}}}_{t_{\textnormal{i}}}\!H\,\mathrm{d}t<\log\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{H_{\textnormal{f}}}.

(384)

For the lifetime of our Universe, this yields an upper
312
312

312

For example,
Tsukahara 2023
studies a setting where D3-branes cause a decay of dSspace before the TCCbound is reached.

bound of
${10}^{\order{\num{e12}}}$
years
Bedroya and Vafa 2020
.

Agmon et al. 2023
notes that our Universe passes only thanks to the log term, and

Vafa 2025
notes that the accelerated expansion we see today could indicate that we are the start of
‘the end of our universe’
.
If the universe lives longer than the lifetime predicted by the TCC, the EFTbreaks down, the cosmological constant dramatically changes in value, or we tunnel to a different vacuum solution
Bedroya 2020
.

It is insightful to compare this lifetime bound with other bounds:

Aalsma and Shiu 2020
find that the scrambling time
313
313

313

‘The scrambling time of a system is the time that it takes for the information of a generic pure state to disperse among all microscopic degrees of freedom’

Bedroya 2020
. For example, if a state falls into a BHand perturbs the equilibrium of the horizon, the dispersion time is the time it takes for that external perturbation to thermalise and to be radiated away
Bedroya 2020
. For dSspace, it is the time it takes something trans-Planckian to stretch beyond the Hubble horizon
Bedroya 2020
.

allows for
N_{e}<\log\mathcal{S}_{\textnormal{dS}}

e
-foldings, with
\mathcal{S}_{\textnormal{dS}}
the entropy of dSspace. Since
\mathcal{S}_{\textnormal{dS}}\sim M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}/H^{2}
, this allows for twice as many
e
-foldings as the TCC.
Aalsma and Shiu 2020
stress that inflation doesn’t have to stop after that, but backreaction effects become important. This limit assures that the entanglement entropy between sub- and super-Hubble modes remains below the thermal entropy after inflation, respectively the Gibbons–Hawking entropy of dSspace
Brandenberger 2021b
;
Brahma et al. 2020a
;
Aalsma et al. 2021a
;
Brahma 2022
.
Up to logarithmic corrections
Blumenhagen et al. 2021
, the scrambling time of dSspace is therfore equal to (twice
Andriot et al. 2020a
;
Aalsma and Shiu 2020
) the TCCbound
Bedroya 2020
.
314
314

314

A scrambling time that corresponds to the lifetime predicted by the TCCis for example found in a holographic model where the vacuum is dominated by
quantum hair

Addazi 2020
: trans-Planckian modes get entangled with the quantum hair (and decohere) within
\tau\sim\sqrt{N}l_{\textnormal{P}}\log N
, with
N\sim\mathcal{S}
the number of quantum hairs that correspond to the entropy of the vacuum
\mathcal{S}
, which saturates the TCCbound.

Alternatively, treating particles as wave packets leads to a UVcutoff scale that imposes a lifetime for the EFTdescription, after which new physics is to be expected
Blamart et al. 2023
:
\tau_{\textnormal{th}}\sim 2H^{-1}\log\left(H^{-1}\right)
for an eternal dSphase, respectively
\tau_{\textnormal{th}}\sim H^{-1}\log\left(H^{-1}\right)
for a dSphase preceded by a matter- or radiation-dominated phase.
Similarly,
Danielsson and Olsson 2004
derive an upper bound for the lifetime of dSspace, based on the idea that if non-gravitational interactions are frozen, a local observer will see that everything thermalises within
\tau\sim R^{3}/l_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}
. The bound relies on weaker assumptions than the TCCand allows for a longer lifetime of dSspace.
Stronger assumptions yield a thermalisation time of dSspace of
\tau_{\textnormal{th}}\sim H^{-1}\log\left(H^{-1}\right)
, which is equal to the TCCprediction, ergo, by the time a dSspace thermalises, it ceases to exist: it is too short-lived to be considered a thermal background
Bedroya 2020
.

Andriot et al. 2023b
look into the future of a possible crunch of the universe, and investigate the implications of the TCCin this context:
Their guiding idea is that even during a crunch, no relativistic mode that corresponds to the typical energy scale of the universe at a time
t_{\textnormal{i}}
can be blue-shifted to become trans-Planckian.
To investigate the implications of this idea, they study the quantity
\sqrt{V}/M_{\textnormal{P}}
instead of
H
. In dSspace,
H
and
\sqrt{V}/M_{\textnormal{P}}
represent the same quantity, but in other spacetimes,
H
does not provide a meaningful length scale. This change to energy scales allows studying such systems more generally. For AdSspaces, conditions for the scalar field potential very similar to the dSCare obtained:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\left(d-1\right)\left(d-2\right)}}

(385)

\displaystyle 0>V(\phi)

\displaystyle\geq-e^{-\mathfrak{c}\absolutevalue{\phi-\phi_i}}

(386)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{-\frac{V^{\prime}}{V}}_{\phi\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\geq\mathfrak{c}

(387)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\frac{V^{\prime\prime}}{V}}_{\phi\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\geq\mathfrak{c}^{2}.

(388)

While the TCCyields a maximum lifetime of the expansion phase, this more general treatment yields a lifetime for a contracting phase:

t_{\textnormal{f}}-t_{\textnormal{i}}<\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{H_\textnormal{i}}}\log\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}{\sqrt{\absolutevalue{V_\textnormal{i}}}}.

(389)

The closer to the crunch we start, the larger is
\absolutevalue{H_\textnormal{i}}
, which indicates the consistency of the statement.

Scalar field potentials

are constrained in the asymptotic limit of moduli space
Bedroya and Vafa 2020
;
Heisenberg et al. 2021
;
Bedroya and Vafa 2020
;
van Beest et al. 2022
;
Basile and Lanza 2020
:

\left.\frac{\absolutevalue{\nabla V}}{V}\right|_{\text{asympt}}\geq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\left(d-1\right)\left(d-2\right)}}.

(390)

The bound becomes trivial for many large dimensions
Bonnefoy et al. 2021
.
In 4d, the bound is also derived using Hodge theory
Bastian et al. 2021
.
Since the bound holds only in the asymptotic limit, it is weaker than for example the dSC
Basile and Lanza 2020
, but it is analytically more powerful, as it does not contain any unknown
\order{1}
-constants
Bedroya and Vafa 2020
.
We can derive this bound as follows
Bedroya and Vafa 2020
:
The TCCinforms us that

\int_{\phi_{\textnormal{i}}}^{\phi_{\textnormal{f}}}\!\frac{H}{\dot{\phi}}\,\mathrm{d}\phi=\int_{t_{\textnormal{i}}}^{t_{\textnormal{f}}}\!H(t)\,\mathrm{d}t<\log\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{H_{\textnormal{f}}}.

(391)

The Friedmann equation

\frac{\left(d-1\right)\left(d-2\right)}{2}H^{2}=\frac{\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2}+V(\phi)

(392)

yields, for positive
V(\phi)
,

To see why it only holds in the asymptotic field limit, we can come from the other direction, assuming
V^{\prime}<0
for definiteness:

\displaystyle\left.\expectationvalue{\frac{-V^\prime}{V}}\right\rvert_{\phi_{\textnormal{i}}}^{\phi_{\textnormal{f}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Delta\phi}\int_{\phi_{\textnormal{i}}}^{\phi_{\textnormal{f}}}\!\frac{-V^{\prime}}{V}\,\mathrm{d}\phi=\frac{\log\left(V_{\textnormal{i}}\right)\log\left(V_{\textnormal{f}}\right)}{\Delta\phi}

(393)

\displaystyle>\frac{\log\left(V_{\textnormal{i}}\right)-\log\left(\left(d-1\right)\left(d-2\right)/2\right)}{\Delta\phi}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\left(d-1\right)\left(d-2\right)}},

which yields

in the infinite field limit.

A bound on the scalar field excursion using
eq.

390
is derived by
van de Heisteeg et al. 2023a
:

V(\phi)\leq\mathfrak{c}\exp\left(\frac{-2\Delta\phi}{\sqrt{\left(d-1\right)\left(d-2\right)}}\right),

(395)

which implies that a scalar field potential decays exponentially for sufficiently large scalar field displacements, i.e. a scalar field potential can only be flat for a finite scalar field range.

Axions

with decay constant
f
and potential
V\propto\cos(\phi/f)
satisfy the TCCif
f\sqrt{\mathfrak{N}}\sim 0.6
,
\mathfrak{N}
an arbitrary number of identical axion fields
\phi
, as numerical simulations by
Shlivko 2023
show. This presents a dilemma for inflation, as observational constraints on the spectral tilt of the CMBpowerspectrum,
n_{s}=0.9649\pm 0.0042\approx 1-\frac{2}{f\sqrt{\mathfrak{N}}}

Planck Collaboration et al. 2020
require
f\sqrt{\mathfrak{N}}>1
.

Axions as quintessential DEwith an instanton-generated potential
V(\phi)=m_{\text{SSB}}^{2}\cdot e^{-S_{\iota}\cos(\phi/f)}
that is of the order of today’s critical energy density,
m_{\text{SSB}}\gtrsim\order{\textnormal{TeV}}
the mass scale of supersymmetry breaking and
S_{\iota}
the instanton action,
faces its own challenges, as
Shlivko 2023
numerically shows:

\displaystyle m_{\text{SSB}}^{2}\cdot e^{-S_{\iota}}

\displaystyle\lesssim H_{0}^{2}

(396)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow S_{\iota}

\displaystyle\gtrsim 2\ln\left(\frac{m_{\text{SSB}}}{H_{0}}\right)

(397)

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\sim 1/S_{\iota}\sim{10^{-2}}

(398)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathfrak{N}

\displaystyle\sim 10^{4}.

(399)

While
\mathfrak{N}\sim 10^{4}
is technically possible in string theory,
Shlivko 2023
finds that it requires fine-tuning of the initial conditions for the fields to satisfy the TCC.

Primordial Black Holes

Cai and Wang 2020
work out mass constraints for PBHsunder the assumption that PBHsare formed from large curvature perturbations at small scales after entering a matter- or radiation-dominated phase. If a PBHforms during the radiation dominated era, the mass can be estimated from the horizon mass as

m_{\textnormal{PBH}}=\varsigma\frac{4}{3}\pi H_{\textnormal{c}}^{-3}\left(3M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}H_{\textnormal{c}}^{2}\right),

(400)

with
\varsigma
the collapse efficiency.
Then, assuming that the Hubble parameter during the formation of the PBH
H_{\textnormal{c}}
is smaller than the Hubble parameter at the end of inflation
H_{\textnormal{f}}
, a lower bound on the mass can be obtained:

\displaystyle m_{\textnormal{PBH}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi\varsigma M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}{H_{\textnormal{c}}}

(401)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{4\pi\varsigma M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}{H_{\textnormal{f}}}

(402)

\displaystyle\geq 4\pi\varsigma M_{\textnormal{P}}e^{N_{e}}

(403)

\displaystyle m_{\textnormal{PBH,r}}

\displaystyle>\varsigma\left(\frac{H_{\textnormal{f}}}{${10}^{9}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$}\right)^{2}M_{\odot}

(404)

\displaystyle m_{\textnormal{PBH,m}}

\displaystyle>\varsigma\left(\frac{${10}^{9}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$}{H_{\textnormal{f}}}\right)^{4}M_{\odot}.

(405)

The bound
m_{\textnormal{PBH,r}}
assumes a radiation-dominated phase after inflation. The bound
m_{\textnormal{PBH,m}}
assumes a short matter-dominated phase between inflation and reheating.
If the Hubble scale during inflation is
H>$10\text{\,}\mathrm{TeV}$
, then PBHscannot account for the entirety of DM
Cai and Wang 2020
.

Dark Energy

The TCCforbids any trans-Planckian mode to ever cross the horizon, which constrains the number of
e
-folds during the DEdominated epoch
Mizuno et al. 2020
.
315
315

315

Given the fact the the DE-dominated phase is not even ongoing for 1
e
-fold so far, this is admittedly a rather theoretical constraint.

Li et al. 2020b
argue that the DEdominated phase should be shorter than naively assumed:

\displaystyle N_{\textnormal{DE}}

\displaystyle<\log\left(\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{H_{\textnormal{I,ini}}}\right)

(406)

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{H_{0}}\log\left(\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{H_{\textnormal{I,end}}}\right),

(407)

i.e. they claim that today’s Hubble parameter
H_{0}
as well as the Hubble parameter at the beginning of inflation
H_{\textnormal{I,ini}}
are of relevance, instead of just the Hubble parameter at the end of inflation
H_{\textnormal{I,end}}
.
They make three strong assumptions to derive this bound:

•

H_{\textnormal{DE}}=H_{0}
is constant during the DEera.

•

The present horizon is
k_{0}=a_{0}H_{0}=a_{\textnormal{I,ini}}H_{\textnormal{I,ini}}
.

•

The dSChas to be applied to their chosen AdSpotential.

We leave it to the reader to assess how well motivated those assumptions are, but we see especially the last assumption critically, as the dSCshould not be necessary for AdSpotentials (see
section

II.4
for further details).

Quintessence

models show a large difference between the Planck scale and today’s Hubble scale, which means that the TCCis easily satisfied
Heisenberg et al. 2021
.
316
316

316

For scalar fields, we derive the bound
\frac{\absolutevalue{\phi_\textnormal{f}-\phi_\textnormal{i}}}{\sqrt{\left(d-1\right)\left(d-2\right)}}<-\log\frac{H_{\textnormal{f}}}{M_{\textnormal{P}}}
in
section

II.17.1
.

The lifetime bound of the TCCimplies that quintessence has to discharge until the time
\tau_{\textnormal{TCC}}

Bedroya 2020
, which requires that the quintessence potential has a minimal gradient of
\absolutevalue{V^\prime}/V\gtrsim\order{\log^{-2}V}
in the bulk respectively
\absolutevalue{V^\prime}/V\gtrsim 2/\left[\left(d-1\right)\left(d-2\right)\right]
asymptotically
Bedroya 2020
.
If we want to extend a DEdominated phase into the infinite future, the constraint from
section

II.17.1
strengthens, and a tension with the DCarises, as a large field displacement develops
Heisenberg et al. 2021
. Since the TCCalready predicts a finite lifetime of the universe, we wouldn’t give too much weight to this concern.

Koga and Ookouchi 2021
study a model of bubble nucleation in 5 dimensions.
A bubble has an inside and an outside, which can have dS, Minkowski, or AdSgeometry.
In their study,
Koga and Ookouchi 2021
found that if both sides are AdS, positive cosmological constants yield eternal 4d dSspaces on the bubble that violate the TCC. They reason that the bubble tension, which they treated as a free parameter, is actually constrained, and taking into account this constraint on the bubble tension rules out solutions where a decay process leads to a bubble with a positive cosmological constant. Moreover, 4d AdSspaces cannot be created on the bubble, as there exists no solution that leads to such a decay, i.e. the cosmological constant cannot be negative. They conclude that the cosmological constant must vanish,
and propose a thawing and a freezing quintessence field instead, one acting as inflation, the other as DE.

A multi-field DEmodel in curved field space is studied by
Payeur et al. 2024
. They find that either the TCCis violated, as the epoch of accelerated expansion is eternal, or the DC, as the field eventually passes a Planckian field range. We present their model around
eq.

149
. They claim that a small patch in the parameter space is compatible with the TCC, the DC, the dSC, as well as observational constraints.

Generalised Proca vector fields

are appealing because they can successfully reduce the Hubble tension
Heisenberg et al. 2021
.
Heisenberg et al. 2021
study a model with three fixed points: a radiation dominated phase, a matter dominated phase, and a DEdominated phase. The DEdominated phase shows phantom-like behaviour, with an EoSapproaching
\omega=-1
from below. They show that this fixed point is inconsistent with the TCC. An issue with Proca fields could be that they are ruled out by the TCCif they approach a dSsolution asymptotically: since the TCCrestricts the lifetime of dSspace, a cosmological model should not asymptote a dSattractor solution
Brahma and Hossain 2021
.

Dark matter

produced by the amplification of quantum fluctuations during inflation could suffer from the low energy scale of inflation predicted by the TCC, as a small Hubble scale during inflation keeps quantum fluctuations small
Tenkanen 2020
.

Pre-Inflationary Phase

Mizuno et al. 2020
derive a bound for the epoch before inflation:

\frac{H_{\textnormal{I}}}{M_{\textnormal{P}}}\lesssim\left(\frac{T_{0}}{T_{\textnormal{rh}}}\right)^{1+\frac{2}{1+3w}},

(408)

with
w
the EoSbefore inflation. For
w<1/3
, this bound becomes even more stringent than the TCCbound.
A radiation-dominated phase before inflation leads to an energy scale of inflation of
{10}^{4}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
and a bound on the tensor-to-scalar ratio of
r_{\textnormal{ts}}<${10}^{-47}$

Brandenberger and Wilson-Ewing 2020
.
A contracting phase before inflation relaxes the constraints
Brandenberger and Wilson-Ewing 2020
.
A radiation-dominated epoch before brane inflation leads to inconsistencies: inflation would start at
t_{\textnormal{i}}=${10}^{23}\text{\,}\mathrm{s}$
, and
r_{\textnormal{ts}}<${10}^{-54}$

Mohammadi et al. 2021
. If the Hubble parameter is allowed to vary as
H(N_{e})=H_{\textnormal{i}}e^{\mathfrak{c}N_{e}}
, the pre-inflation phase lasts for
{10}^{-8}\text{\,}\mathrm{s}
and
r_{\textnormal{ts}}<${10}^{-2}$
is allowed
Mohammadi et al. 2021
.

Inflation

might be in the swampland, according to

Bedroya et al. 2024c
, who motivate the TCCfrom a bottom-up perspective.
However, while they show that a certain class of models is likely part of the swampland, inflation itself is not conceptually ruled out: if the cosmology at hand either will never transition into a decelerating phase or will have phases that are dominated by quantum effects, such as bubble nucleation, tunnelling events, or large quantum fluctuations, the model can evade their crushing conclusion.
The TCCmakes sure that no trans-Planckian modes that describe microscopic UVmodes are redshifted into macroscopic IRobservables described by an EFT.
However,
the relevance of such constraints on time-dependent EFTshas been questioned
Kaloper and Scargill 2019
;
Dvali et al. 2020
;
Burgess et al. 2021
;
Komissarov et al. 2023
;
Lacombe and Mukohyama 2023

Cicoli et al. 2023a
. In the following, we assume their relevance to
derive some quantitative bounds, assess constraints, and study different classes of models in more detail.

The inflaton potential has an upper bound, which we derive by following the evolution of a mode
Bedroya et al. 2020a
:

•

Initially, we start with the Hubble radius
1/H_{\textnormal{i}}
.

•

This scale grows by the factor of
\exp N_{e}
during inflation.

•

Between the end of inflation and the beginning of the radiation dominated epoch, i.e. during reheating, the scale grows by a factor of
a_{\textnormal{r}}/a_{\textnormal{f}}
.

•

Since the end of reheating, the scale has grown by a factor of
\frac{T_{\textnormal{r}}g_{*}(T_{\textnormal{r}})^{1/3}}{T_{\textnormal{0}}g_{*}(T_{\textnormal{0}})^{1/3}}
, where we account for the change in the effective number of relativistic degrees of freedom.
317
317

317

Conservation of entropy informs us that the temperature
T
is related to the effective number of relativistic degrees of freedom
g_{*}(T)
as
T\propto g_{*}^{-1/3}a^{-1}

Baumann 2015
.

•

This approximates the evolution of this length scale until today, ergo, it should roughly correspond to today’s Hubble scale.

This motivates the following relation:

\frac{1}{H_{\textnormal{i}}}e^{N_{e}}\frac{a_{\textnormal{r}}}{a_{\textnormal{f}}}\frac{T_{\textnormal{r}}g_{*}(T_{\textnormal{r}})^{1/3}}{T_{\textnormal{0}}g_{*}(T_{\textnormal{0}})^{1/3}}\simeq\frac{1}{H_{0}}.

(409)

•

For the inflaton potential, we know that
H=\sqrt{V}/\sqrt{3}M_{\textnormal{P}}
.

•

If we assume that reheating takes place rapidly enough,
a_{\textnormal{r}}/a_{\textnormal{f}}\sim 1
holds.

•

Assuming that reheating lasts less than a Hubble time,
T_{\textnormal{r}}\approx V^{1/4}
holds
Bedroya et al. 2020a
.
318
318

318

A more accurate description would not significantly change the result, as the temperature during reheating can be expressed in terms of
H
as
T_{\textnormal{r}}=\left(45/4\pi^{3}g_{*}(T_{\textnormal{r}})\right)^{1/4}\sqrt{H_{\textnormal{r}}M_{\textnormal{P}}}

Brandenberger et al. 2024
.

•

To get an order of magnitude estimate, we can also assume that
g_{*}(T_{\textnormal{r}})^{1/3}\sim g_{*}(T_{\textnormal{0}})^{1/3}
.
319
319

319

Since for the SM
g_{*}(T_{\textnormal{r}})
varies between 10.56 (with a reheating temperature of
1\text{\,}\mathrm{MeV}
) and 106.75 (with a reheating temperature of
170\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
)
Mizuno et al. 2020
;
Husdal 2016
, this assumption is justified.

T_{\textnormal{r}}\approx$1\text{\,}\mathrm{MeV}$
is the lower bound for the reheating temperature from BBN
Mizuno et al. 2020
;
Hasegawa et al. 2019
;
Hannestad 2004
;
Kawasaki et al. 2000
;
Kawasaki et al. 1999
.

•

The Friedmann equation informs us that the Hubble scale is given by the current energy density:
1/H_{0}=\sqrt{3}M_{\textnormal{P}}/\sqrt{\rho_{0}}
.

•

The energy density today corresponds roughly to the following expression:
\rho_{0}\approx T_{0}^{4}\frac{T_{\textnormal{eq}}}{T_{0}}\frac{1}{\Omega_{\textnormal{m}}}
, with
T_{\textnormal{eq}}
the temperature during radiation–matter equality, and
\Omega_{\textnormal{m}}
today’s relative matter density.

This leaves us with:

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{3}M_{\textnormal{P}}}{\sqrt{V}}e^{N_{e}}\frac{V^{1/4}}{T_{\textnormal{0}}}

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{\sqrt{3}M_{\textnormal{P}}}{\sqrt{T_{0}^{4}\frac{T_{\textnormal{eq}}}{T_{0}}\frac{1}{\Omega_{\textnormal{m}}}}}

(410)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow e^{N_{e}}

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{V^{1/4}}{\sqrt{T_{0}T_{\textnormal{eq}}}}\sqrt{\Omega_{\textnormal{m}}}.

(411)

Then, we use
H\simeq\sqrt{V/3M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}
again, apply the TCCbound
e^{N_{e}}<M_{\textnormal{P}}/H
, and solve for
V
to find

\displaystyle V^{3/4}

\displaystyle<\sqrt{3}M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}\sqrt{T_{0}T_{\textnormal{eq}}}

(412)

\displaystyle V^{1/4}

\displaystyle<${10}^{9}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$\sim${10}^{-10}$M_{\textnormal{P}}.

(413)

If we express this bound again in terms of
H
to find
H\lesssim${10}^{\order{\num{e-20}}}$M_{\textnormal{P}}
, and assume a scale invariant scalar powerspectrum
P_{\zeta}=H^{2}/8\pi^{2}M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}\epsilon_{V}c_{\textnormal{s}}\sim${10}^{-9}$
we find the bound on the tensor-to-scalar ratio
r_{\textnormal{ts}}=16\epsilon_{V}c_{\textnormal{s}}\lesssim${10}^{-31}$

Shi and Qiu 2021
;
Brahma 2020a
;
Bedroya et al. 2020a
;
Kamali and Brandenberger 2020a
;
Dhuria and Goswami 2019
;
Brandenberger 2021a
;
Brandenberger et al. 2024
;
van de Heisteeg et al. 2023a
;
Santos et al. 2022
;
Kamali et al. 2023
.

Allowing the reheating process to last for
N_{\textnormal{rh}}

e
-foldings leads to a weaker bound
Shi and Qiu 2021
;
Mizuno et al. 2020
:

\displaystyle e^{N_{\textnormal{rh}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{\textnormal{rh}}}{a_{\textnormal{f}}}

(414)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\rho_{\textnormal{f}}}{\rho_{\textnormal{rh}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3\left(1+w\right)}}

(415)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}M_{\textnormal{P}}H_{\textnormal{f}}}{T_{\textnormal{rh}}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{2}{3\left(1+w\right)}}

(416)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{H}{M_{\textnormal{P}}}

\displaystyle<\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}H_{0}}{T_{\textnormal{rh}}T_{0}}\left(\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}H}{T_{\textnormal{rh}}}\right)^{\frac{2}{3\left(1+w\right)}-1}

(417)

\displaystyle r_{\textnormal{ts}}

\displaystyle<${10}^{-8}$\left(\frac{$1\text{\,}\mathrm{MeV}$}{T_{\textnormal{rh}}}\right)^{2},

(418)

where BBNputs a constraint of
T_{\textnormal{rh}}>$1\text{\,}\mathrm{MeV}$

Hasegawa et al. 2019
;
Hannestad 2004
;
Kawasaki et al. 2000
;
Kawasaki et al. 1999
.

These constraints can be mitigated, e.g. by

•

assuming
H
to be dynamic during inflation, which leads to the relaxed constraints
V^{1/4}<${10}^{-5}$M_{\textnormal{P}}
and
r_{\textnormal{ts}}<${10}^{-10}$

Kamali and Brandenberger 2020a
;

•

a non-standard cosmology after the end of inflation
Kamali and Brandenberger 2020a
;
Dhuria and Goswami 2019
;
Torabian 2020a
, for example, the phase between the end of inflation and BBNcould be a phase with an EoSof
w=-1/3
, which yields
Dhuria and Goswami 2019
:

–

r_{\textnormal{ts}}\lesssim${10}^{-8}$

–

H_{\textnormal{I}}\approx${10}^{10}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$

–

V^{1/4}\approx${10}^{14}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
;

•

non-standard initial conditions for fluctuations
Kamali and Brandenberger 2020a
: Tensor modes could also be produced outside of inflation by non-BDterms, e.g. by modified gravity, thermal fluctuations, or excited initial states
Brahma 2020a
;
Agullo 2015
.
Non-BDstates for tensor modes can increase the upper bound for
r_{\textnormal{ts}}
by about a factor of
{10}^{6}
— further remedy comes from allowing scalar modes to also be sourced from non-BDstates
Brahma 2020a
.
Even though the enhancement from non-BDstates on scalar modes allows
\epsilon_{H}<${10}^{-9}$
, it cancels in a calculation of
r_{\textnormal{ts}}
— but it allows the tensor modes to be enhanced even further, which then eventually allows for
r_{\textnormal{ts}}<${10}^{-3}$
.

Single-field inflation

in a GRsetting seems to be only consistent with the TCCif fine-tuning takes place
Trivedi 2022a
;
Bedroya et al. 2020a
;
Jin et al. 2021
.
Also, non-BDscalar or tensor modes lead to viable solutions that satisfy the TCCas well as observational constraints
Naskar and Pal 2022
:
The powerspectra for scalar and tensor modes are given by

\displaystyle P_{\textnormal{s}}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{H^{2}}{8\epsilon M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}\absolutevalue{u_k^\textnormal{s}+w_k^\textnormal{s}}^{2}

(419)

\displaystyle P_{\textnormal{t}}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{2H^{2}}{M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}\absolutevalue{u_k^\textnormal{t}+w_k^\textnormal{t}}^{2}

(420)

\displaystyle r_{\textnormal{ts}}

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{\textnormal{t}}(k)}{P_{\textnormal{s}}(k)}=16\epsilon\frac{\absolutevalue{u_k^\textnormal{t}+w_k^\textnormal{t}}^{2}}{\absolutevalue{u_k^\textnormal{s}+w_k^\textnormal{s}}^{2}}

(421)

with
\epsilon=\epsilon_{V}=\epsilon_{H}
(since it is the single-field case) the slow-roll parameter,
and
u
and
w
the Bogolyubov coefficients that characterise the non-BDstates, such that
u_{k}=1
and
w_{k}=0
corresponds to the BDstates.
320
320

320

The Bogolyubov coefficients appear in the definition of the bispectra / 3-point correlation functions.
Studying three-point correlation functions has several benefits
Naskar and Pal 2022
:
(i) they are sensitive to the initial state;
(ii) autocorrelation of tensor modes respectively non-Gausssianities show in the spectrum of primordial GWs;
(iii) tensor–scalar–scalar correlators probe the diffeomorphism breaking during inflation
Bartolo et al. 2016
;
(iv) mixed correlators show as the quadrupole moment in the CMBspectrum, and might be observable in future CMBmissions
Abazajian et al. 2016
.

The Bogolyubov coefficients satisfy the Wronskian condition
u_{k}^{2}-w_{k}^{2}=1
.
The TCCputs the following constraint on the backreactions
Greene et al. 2005
;
Holman and Tolley 2008
;
Naskar and Pal 2022
:

w_{0}\leq\sqrt{\epsilon\eta}\frac{HM_{\textnormal{P}}}{\Lambda},

(422)

with
\Lambda>H
a cutoff scale.
It is now no longer
\epsilon<${10}^{-31}$
that has to hold, but
\epsilon/\absolutevalue{u_k^\textnormal{s}+w_k^\textnormal{s}}^{2}<${10}^{-31}$
. This relaxes the bound on
r_{\textnormal{ts}}
.

Among others, the TCCwas explicitly used to by
Okada et al. 2021
to constrain an inflection point scenario by demanding
H_{\textnormal{I}}<$1\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
,
as well as by
Mohammadi et al. 2022
, who derived
\rho<3M_{\textnormal{P;4}}M_{\textnormal{P;5}}^{3}/4\pi e^{N_{e}}\approx${10}^{35}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
as an upper bound for the energy density at the end of brane inflation,
where they explicitly set the 5-dimensional Planck mass to
M_{\textnormal{P;5}}\sim$2\text{\times}{10}^{14}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
, which rules out the typical GUTscale (
\left(${10}^{16}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$\right)^{4}\sim${10}^{64}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
) models.

Slow-roll large-field inflation

is in tension with the TCC.
While
Sanna and Sebastiani 2021
found constant roll inflation and some models in
f(R)
gravity that suppress the amplitude of primordial GWsto be compatible with the TCC,

Ossoulian et al. 2023
found that even models that are compatible with the DCand the dSCcan violate the TCCconstraints, such as a power-law and an exponential model in
f(R,T)
gravity with a non-canonical scalar field.
Let us highlight why large field excursions and slow-rolling are problematic.
In terms of field excursion, we can make the following observation
Bedroya et al. 2020a
:
Let us first write the field range as

\absolutevalue{\Delta\phi}=\absolutevalue{\dot{\phi}\Delta t}.

(423)

Then, we use the slow-roll condition
3H\dot{\phi}=-V^{\prime}
and the TCCcondition
\Delta t\leq H^{-1}\log H^{-1}
to find

\absolutevalue{\Delta\phi}\leq\absolutevalue{\frac{V^\prime}{3H^2}\log H^{-1}}.

(424)

With the slow-roll parameter
\epsilon_{V}=\left(V^{\prime}/V\right)^{2}/2
and the TCCenergy bound on the potential
V
we find then
\absolutevalue{\Delta\phi}\leq${10}^{-13}$M_{\textnormal{P}}
, which conflicts with large-field inflation.

That slow-rolling is problematic can also be seen by focussing on the amplitude of the primordial powerspectrum respectively the tensor-to-scalar ratio
Sanna and Sebastiani 2021
:
We start with a vanilla slow-roll model of inflation:

\displaystyle\frac{3H^{2}M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}{8\pi}

\displaystyle=\frac{\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2}+V(\phi)

(425)

\displaystyle\ddot{\phi}+3H\dot{\phi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{d}V}{\mathrm{d}\phi}

(426)

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=\frac{\dot{\phi}^{2}-2V}{\dot{\phi}^{2}+2V}

(427)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{H}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\dot{H}}{H}

(428)

\displaystyle\eta_{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{\dot{\epsilon}_{H}}{H\epsilon_{H}}

(429)

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}\epsilon_{H}}\frac{H^{2}}{M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\sim

{10}^{-9}

(430)

\displaystyle n_{\textnormal{s}}

\displaystyle=1-2\epsilon_{H}-\eta_{H}

\displaystyle=

0.9649(0.0042)

(431)

\displaystyle r_{\textnormal{ts}}

\displaystyle=16\epsilon_{H}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle 0.06.

(432)

The slow-roll approximation
\epsilon_{H},\absolutevalue{\eta_H}\ll 1
yield then

\displaystyle\epsilon_{H}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{3\left(1+w(N_{e})\right)}{2}

(433)

\displaystyle\eta_{H}

\displaystyle\approx-\frac{\mathrm{d}\log\left(1+w(N_{e})\right)}{\mathrm{d}N_{e}}

(434)

\displaystyle 1-n_{\textnormal{s}}

\displaystyle=3\left(1+w(N_{e})\right)-\frac{\mathrm{d}\log\left(1+w(N_{e})\right)}{\mathrm{d}N_{e}}

(435)

\displaystyle r_{\textnormal{ts}}

\displaystyle=24\left(1+w(N_{e})\right).

(436)

Solving
eq.

430
for
\epsilon_{H}
, and using the TCCbound on
H
yields

\epsilon_{H}<\frac{e^{-2N_{e}}}{8\pi^{2}P}.

(437)

Applying this bound to the slow-roll approximation for
\epsilon_{H}
yields
1+w<${10}^{-45}$
which in turn yields
r_{\textnormal{ts}}<${10}^{-44}$
, basically eradicating any gravitational B-modes.

Small-Field Models

: the minimally required number of
e
-folds (to be compatible with the TCC) is typically twice or more than what is required to explain CMBfluctuations.

D-term hybrid inflation

Halyo 1996
;
Binétruy and Dvali 1996
;
Domcke and Schmitz 2018
;
Domcke and Schmitz 2017
;
Schmitz and Yanagida 2018
is compatible with the TCC
Schmitz 2020
:
it has a scalar power spectrum with an amplitude of the order of the GUTscale
\sim${10}^{16}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
, and sets an upper bound on the gravitino mass scale of
m_{\textnormal{3/2}}\lesssim$10\text{\,}\mathrm{MeV}$
that would allow DMto consist of thermally produced gravitinos.
The model is particularly interesting, as it can reduce the Hubble tension: unstable gravitinos (DM) decay into an axion quintessence field (radiation) and an axino (warm DM). The Hubble parameter shows a slower decrease in this model than in
\Lambda
CDMand shows a higher value of the Hubble constant today, which reduces the Hubble tension.
325
325

325

Hybrid inflation ends with a rapid second-order phase transition, which directly points to a potential tension with the refined dSC(
eq.

13
), which was indeed found
Schmitz 2020
:
\epsilon\ll\eta\simeq-0.02
. The DC, however, is satisfied, as well as the WGC
Schmitz 2020
.

k
-flation

with a more general Lagrangian and a time-dependent EoSdiffers from inflation described by a canonical scalar field: modes do not freeze out at the Hubble radius, but at the freeze-out radius

r_{\textnormal{f}}=\sqrt{\frac{\mathcal{F}}{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}},

(438)

with

\mathcal{F}=\frac{a\sqrt{2\epsilon_{H}}}{\sqrt{c_{\textnormal{s}}}},

(439)

with
a
the scale factor,
\epsilon_{H}=-\dot{H}/H^{2}
the Hubble slow-roll parameter (see
section

II.4.1
), and
c_{\textnormal{s}}
the speed of sound
Lin and Kinney 2020
;
Khoury and Piazza 2009
;
Geshnizjani et al. 2011
.
For canonical single-field inflation (
c_{\textnormal{s}}=1
,
\epsilon_{V}\ll 1
), the freeze-out radius corresponds to the comoving Hubble length
\left(aH\right)^{-1}
,
326
326

326

\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}/\mathcal{F}\sim a^{\prime\prime}/a\sim 2a^{2}H^{2}
while for
k
-flation, it corresponds to the acoustic length
r_{\textnormal{f}}\sim c_{\textnormal{s}}/aH

Lin and Kinney 2020
. This finding motivated
Lin and Kinney 2020
to formulate a TCCthat depends on the speed of sound:

\log\frac{c_{\textnormal{s}}(a)M_{\textnormal{P}}}{H(a)}>\log\frac{a}{a_{\textnormal{i}}},

(440)

with
a_{\textnormal{i}}
the initial scale factor at the beginning of inflation. This bound has to hold to avoid the classicalisation of trans-Planckian modes. This brings more free parameters, which means that additional assumptions have to be made to constrain models, respectively, more observational constraints have to be taken into account to assess the validity of a model.

Multi-phase inflation

happening in several stages, each with a relatively low energy scale, can help to accommodate the TCCrestrictions
Li et al. 2020b
;
Torabian 2020b
. Inflation at high energy scales cannot last long enough (without spoiling the TCC) to explain the horizon problem, but a succession of several phases of inflations can
Torabian 2020b
. To solve the horizon problem,

\displaystyle\frac{a_{\textnormal{I}}}{a_{0}}\frac{H_{\textnormal{I}}}{H_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{\textnormal{I}}}{a_{\textnormal{f,1}}}\frac{a_{\textnormal{f,1}}}{a_{\textnormal{i,2}}}\cdots\frac{a_{\textnormal{i,}m-1}}{a_{\textnormal{f,}m}}\frac{a_{\textnormal{f,}m}}{a_{\textnormal{r}}}\frac{a_{\textnormal{r}}}{a_{0}}\frac{H_{\textnormal{I}}}{H_{0}}

(441)

\displaystyle=\exp\left({-\sum_{i=1}^{m}N_{i}}\right)\frac{T_{0}}{T_{\textnormal{r}}}\frac{H_{I}}{H_{0}}\frac{a_{\textnormal{f,}m}}{a_{\textnormal{r}}}

(442)

\displaystyle=\exp\left({-\sum_{i=1}^{m}N_{i}}\right)\frac{T_{0}}{T_{\textnormal{r}}}\frac{H_{I}}{H_{0}}\left(\frac{H_{\textnormal{r}}}{H_{m}}\right)^{\frac{2}{3\left(1+w_{m}\right)}}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\left(\frac{H_{i+1}}{H_{i}}\right)^{\frac{2}{3\left(1+w_{i}\right)}}

(443)

\displaystyle\leq 1

(444)

has to hold, with
H_{\textnormal{I}}
the initial Hubble parameter of the first phase of inflation
Torabian 2020b
.
Using

\frac{a_{i}}{a_{i+1}}=\left(\frac{H_{i+1}}{H_{i}}\right)^{2/3\left(1+w\right)},

(445)

the TCCcan be expressed as

H_{\textnormal{I}}^{2-\frac{2}{3\left(1+w\right)}}<M_{\textnormal{P}}H_{\Lambda}T_{0}^{-1}T_{\textnormal{r}}H_{1}^{\frac{-2}{3\left(1+w\right)}}\frac{a_{2}}{a_{1}}\cdots\frac{a_{n}}{a_{n-1}}\frac{a_{\textnormal{r}}}{a_{n}},

(446)

which allows us to examine different scenarios
Torabian 2020a
:

H_{\textnormal{I}}<${10}^{14}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$

If the phase after inflation and before BBNis dominated by kinetic terms with
w=1
like
k
-essence, then
H_{\textnormal{I}}^{5/2}<M_{\textnormal{P}}H_{\Lambda}T_{0}^{-1}T_{\textnormal{r}}H_{\textnormal{r}}^{-1/3}
holds, which yields
H_{\textnormal{I}}\lesssim\left($10\text{\,}\mathrm{MeV}$/T_{\textnormal{r}}\right)^{1/5}\,\textnormal{MeV}
.

H_{\textnormal{I}}<$0.1\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$

If the phase after inflation and before matter–radiation equality is radiation dominated, then
H_{\textnormal{I}}^{3/2}<M_{\textnormal{P}}H_{\Lambda}T_{0}^{-1}T_{\textnormal{r}}H_{\textnormal{r}}^{-1/2}
holds.

H_{\textnormal{I}}<$100\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$

If the phase after inflation and before BBNis dominated by matter with
w=0
, then
H_{\textnormal{I}}^{4/3}<M_{\textnormal{P}}H_{\Lambda}T_{0}^{-1}T_{\textnormal{r}}H_{\textnormal{r}}^{-2/3}
holds, which yields
H_{\textnormal{I}}\lesssim 100\left($10\text{\,}\mathrm{MeV}$/T_{\textnormal{r}}\right)^{1/4}\,\textnormal{GeV}
.

H_{\textnormal{I}}<${10}^{15}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$

If the phase after inflation and before BBNis dominated by exotic matter with
w=-1/3
like cosmic strings, then
H_{\textnormal{I}}<M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}H_{\Lambda}T_{0}^{-1}T_{\textnormal{r}}^{-1}
holds, which yields
H_{\textnormal{I}}\lesssim${10}^{14}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$\left($10\text{\,}\mathrm{MeV}$/T_{\textnormal{r}}\right)
.

H_{\textnormal{I}}<$100\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$

If the phase after inflation and before BBNis dominated by moduli
327
327

327

See
section

II.17.1
why such a phase is incompatible with the TCC.
with the modulus decay rate
H\sim\Gamma_{\phi}\sim m_{\phi}^{3}/M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}
and a lower bound on the modulus mass of
10\text{\,}\mathrm{TeV}
, then
H_{\textnormal{I}}^{3/2}<M_{\textnormal{P}}H_{\Lambda}T_{0}^{-1}T_{\textnormal{r}}m_{\phi}^{-1/2}\left(m_{\phi}/\Gamma_{\phi}\right)^{2/3}
holds, which yields
H_{\textnormal{I}}^{3/2}<M_{\textnormal{P}}^{11/6}H_{\Lambda}T_{0}^{-1}m_{\phi}^{-1/3}
.

TCC-compatible multi-stage models include
warm multiphase inflation, where inflation is interrupted by a radiation-dominated phase
Berera and Calderón 2019
;
Berera et al. 2021
,
and chain inflation, where inflation undergoes a rapid succession of tunnelling events from the minimum of one vacuum to the next
Winkler and Freese 2021
. In chain inflation, bubbles of the new vacuum percolate in each step, releasing radiation
Winkler and Freese 2021
.
328
328

328

If an axion potential is used, the CMBpowerspectrum puts an upper limit on the axion decay constant of chain inflation, which agrees with the notion of several swampland conjectures that do the same, i.e. the dSC, the SSC, the TCC, the WGC, and possibly the FLB.

String gas cosmology

Battefeld and Watson 2006
produces a scale-invariant powerspectrum
Nayeri et al. 2006
and a nearly scale-invariant spectrum of GWs, with a slight blue tilt
Brandenberger et al. 2007
. While the horizon problem is absent in string gas cosmology, the flatness problem needs to be addressed
Kamali and Brandenberger 2020b
.
Kamali and Brandenberger 2020b
combine string gas cosmology with power-law inflation to do so, and find that it is compatible with the TCC.

Tachyacoustic cosmology

solves the horizon problem by allowing a superluminal speed of sound
Lin and Kinney 2019
;
Magueijo 2008
;
Bessada et al. 2009
;
Babichev et al. 2007
;
Lin and Kinney 2021
.
329
329

329

See the work by
Ellis et al. 2007
for arguments against superluminal speeds of sound.

Modes freeze out when they cross the acoustic horizon
c_{\textnormal{s}}k=aH
, and solve the horizon problem if

\frac{c_{\textnormal{s}}(a_{\textnormal{i}})}{a_{\textnormal{i}}H_{\textnormal{i}}}\geq\frac{1}{a_{0}H_{0}},

(447)

which yields a period of trans-Planckian energy densities and violates the TCC
Lin and Kinney 2021
.

Moduli-Dominated Phase

Observational data allows for a phase between the end of inflation and BBNthat is not radiation-dominated, i.e. the EoSfor that phase could differ from
w=1/3
, such as in the case of a moduli-dominated phase
Dhuria and Goswami 2019
:
The moduli are of mass
m_{\textnormal{mod}}\ll H_{\textnormal{I}}
, which start to oscillate when
H\approx m_{\textnormal{mod}}
, and decay into radiation when the Hubble parameter corresponds to their decay width
\Gamma_{\textnormal{mod}}\approx H
. Such a moduli-dominated epoch is incompatible with the TCC: The moduli need to be lighter than
H_{\textnormal{I}}
to get displaced from their minima and to start oscillating, and the reheating temperature must be above the BBNtemperature. The upper bound on the energy scale of inflation from the TCCdoes not allow for such a window.

II.17.2
General Remarks

The TCCshould be viewed as a necessary but not a sufficient condition to forbid classicalisation of quantum modes:
330
330

330

See
section

II.17.2
for an explanation that we do not mean that all quantum effects are forbidden by the TCC.

in principle, non-linear modes could develop below the Hubble scale and become classical
Brandenberger 2021a
;
Berera et al. 2020
.
Large quantum fluctuations are always a possibility that would influence the background trajectory stochastically
Saito et al. 2020
.

Burgess et al. 2021
argue that it is not even a necessary condition, as trans-Planckian modes would not necessarily spoil an EFTdescription: if the vacuum evolves continuously and adiabatically into the ground state of the EFT, the description remains valid.

Berera et al. 2020
add that crossing the horizon does not necessarily lead to classicalisation (or decoherence) either: the wave needs to interact with the environment to decohere.
Cai and Piao 2020
argue that sub-Planckian modes could have interacted with trans-Planckian modes before inflation, which would alter their state (to a non-BDstate), which would result in the observability of trans-Planckian quantum effects on classical scales. They argue that this can only be avoided if the pre-inflationary phase is
‘past-complete’
and Minkowski, i.e. there are no singularities, not even in the infinite past. This is for example the case in certain bounce cosmologies, as they argue.
However, we think that this would not lead to the observability of trans-Planckian physics: if the modes start in a non-BDstate early on, but decohere during inflation, we will not be able to fully trace back the evolution of the mode to a time before the interaction with the trans-Planckian mode. The TCCis then sufficient to shield us from these trans-Planckian effects, and our EFT-description remains unaffected by the earlier trans-Planckian physics. Decoherence is likely to complete already during inflation
Burgess et al. 2023
;
Lombardo and Nacir 2005
;
Barvinsky et al. 1999
;
Martineau 2007
. However,
Burgess et al. 2008b
argue that modes much shorter than the Hubble scale do not decohere longer modes (that are still shorter than the Hubble scale) as easily, as those, being pure, can be approximated as BDstates (as BDstates are attractor solutions of the squeezing equations
Albrecht et al. 1994
).

Brahma 2020a
argues that assuming a BDstate at the onset of inflation is not justified: BDstates might be the initial states of each mode, but they might lay in the infinite past. An EFTdescription has a cutoff scale and the EFTdescription might break down before a classical mode is blue-shifted to a sub-Planckian length (when going back in time), as the mode might reach an energy scale that lies above the EFTcutoff, before it reaches the Planck scale
Brahma et al. 2021a
.
This cutoff can even be described by another swampland conjecture. This reminds us that the swampland programme is a web of interconnected conjectures.
The TCCgives conceptual guidance and helps to strengthen other conjectures such as the dSCby specifying the
\order{1}
constant.
331
331

331

If non-perturbative correction are present in the potential, the TCCcould obtain log-corrections, as for example the AdSDCdoes. This could lift the bound to
\absolutevalue{\nabla V}\geq V\left(\mathfrak{c}_{1}\log(\abs{V})+\mathfrak{c}_{2}\right)
, with
\mathfrak{c}_{1,2}
constants, which would allow for stable dSvacua, e.g. for
V=e^{-\mathfrak{c}_{2}/\mathfrak{c}_{1}}

Blumenhagen et al. 2020b
.

However, the TCCis also more forgiving than other conjectures
Deffayet et al. 2024b
: For example, it allows the dSCto be violated for short periods or individual points. To violate the TCC, the strong dSCmust be violated for some time, namely

\frac{\absolutevalue{\nabla V}}{V}\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\left(d-1\right)\left(d-2\right)}}

(448)

has to hold over a field range

\Delta\phi\geq\frac{\sqrt{\left(d-1\right)\left(d-2\right)}}{2}\log\left(\frac{\left(d-1\right)\left(d-2\right)}{2V_{\textnormal{max}}}\right),

(449)

with
V_{\textnormal{max}}
the maximum value of the potential within the field range.

What motivates the TCC?

On purely mathematical grounds, Grönwall’s inequality
332
332

332

Or
Gronwall’s
inequality, if you follow his spelling, as he dropped the umlauts after immigrating to the USA.

implies that
a_{\textnormal{f}}/a_{\textnormal{i}}<M_{\textnormal{P}}/H_{\textnormal{f}}
holds if
\int_{t_{\textnormal{i}}}^{t_{\textnormal{f}}}\!H(s)\,\mathrm{d}s<\log\left(M_{\textnormal{P}}/H_{\textnormal{f}}\right)
holds, assuming
\dot{a}(t)/a(t)\leq H(t)

Cotsakis and Miritzis 2023
.

An EFTdescribing our Universe should have a valid vacuum solution. This is at least indicated by holography and perturbative string theory.
Bedroya et al. 2024c
show that EFTsthat violate the TCCdo not have asymptotic vacuum solutions, respectively they show that a certain class of cosmologies do not have an EFTdescription of the vacuum in the asymptotic future boundary spacetime, namely cosmologies that
(i) violate the TCC,
(ii) have a transition to a deceleration phase,
(iii) are flat or negatively curved,
(iv) have a classical background evolution, i.e. are not dominated by quantum effects such as bubble nucleation or large quantum fluctuations.
The strong implication of their work is that the TCCholds not only on the infinite boundary of moduli space, but even deep inside the bulk, i.e. cosmology has to satisfy the TCC.

The TCCassures independence of our EFTdescription from QGeffects
Schneider 2021
.
We do not have to speculate about initial conditions on trans-Planckian scales to understand LSSformation
Wolf and Thébault 2023
.
If trans-Planckian physics were to affect LSSgrowth, low-energy physics would provide us with a possibility to examine high-energy physics in a way that is different from IR/UVmixing as we usually understand it. The TCCnot only secludes trans-Planckian physics from our examination, it even renders it impossible for us to figure out if there is trans-Planckian physics in the early Universe at all
Schneider 2021
.

If the TCCwere violated,
information got lost in the asymptotic limit of field space, e.g. in the infinite future
Bedroya 2022
,
and all dynamical quantum fluctuations
H<k<l_{\textnormal{P}}^{-1}
became classical
Bedroya 2020
.
Even though the observation of QGphenomena in the CMBor in LSSswould be a
‘unique opportunity’
to learn more about QG
Komissarov et al. 2023
, the
‘classical observation of a sub-Planckian quantum mode, […] is a bit strange!’

Bedroya and Vafa 2020
.

Besides these mathematical, quantum-gravitational, and string-theoretical considerations, there is also observational support that the TCCholds:
The TCCbound (
eq.

380
) constrains the number of
e
-foldings that are allowed to happen during inflation:

N_{e}<\log\left(\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{H_{\textnormal{f}}}\right).

(450)

Another bound on the number of
e
-foldings comes from structure formation and the horizon problem: the current comoving Hubble radius must have been inside the Hubble horizon at the beginning of inflation, i.e.

\displaystyle a_{\textnormal{i}}H_{\textnormal{i}}

\displaystyle<a_{\textnormal{0}}H_{\textnormal{0}}

(451)

\displaystyle N_{e}

\displaystyle=\log\left(a_{\textnormal{f}}/a_{\textnormal{i}}\right)

(452)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow e^{N_{e}}

\displaystyle>\frac{H_{\textnormal{i}}}{H_{0}}\frac{a_{\textnormal{i}}}{a_{0}}.

(453)

Furthermore, a similar bound can be derived based on entropy considerations
Brahma et al. 2020a
:
the entropy from quantum fluctuations in the early universe can be understood as momentum space entanglement entropy between sub- and super-Hubble modes. Since (globally) entropy is increasing, one can assume that the entropy at the beginning of inflation is smaller than the entropy at the beginning of a radiation-dominated phase. This yields the bound
N_{e}<\log\left(M_{\textnormal{P}}/H\right)^{1/4}+\log\epsilon_{H}^{-1/2}
, which is, at least for the observed values from the powerspectrum, numerically close to the TCCbound.

What is special about the Hubble radius?

The Hubble radius acts as a length scale threshold, where the dynamical behaviour of modes differs fundamentally:

Sub-Hubble

Modes act like independent harmonic oscillators and stretch with the expansion of space
Schneider 2021
;
Kiefer et al. 1998
;
Kamali and Brandenberger 2020a
;
Brandenberger and Martin 2013
.

Super-Hubble

Modes act like overdamped oscillators
Schneider 2021
,
fluctuations with a canonical kinetic term become squeezed and classicalise
Kiefer et al. 1998
;
Kamali and Brandenberger 2020a
;
Brandenberger and Martin 2013
respectively
modes freeze and become non-dynamical
Bedroya 2020
;
Brandenberger and Martin 2013
;
Brandenberger 2021a
.
333
333

333

Scalar and tensor fluctuations might stop oscillating, when exiting the Hubble radius, but they can still grow in amplitude
Brandenberger 2021a
.

Super-Hubble modes experience decoherence, which makes them equivalent to classical, stochastic perturbations that remain classical when re-entering the Hubble horizon
Polarski and Starobinsky 1996
;
Bedroya 2020
.

Why is there not just a simple energy cutoff to exclude trans-Planckian modes from an EFT?

The TCCcan be understood as an expression of the non-renormalisability of GR
Bedroya 2020
: In flat space, a theory can be renormalised if classical high-momentum perturbations with
k>\Lambda
(
\Lambda
the momentum cutoff) do not influence the
k<\Lambda
modes. In GR, this naive cutoff ceases to exist, as expanding space can stretch
k>\Lambda
modes into
k<\Lambda
modes.
To relax this sharp cutoff, two different energy scales need to be defined, a UVcutoff
\Lambda_{\textnormal{UV}}
and an IRcutoff
\Lambda_{\textnormal{IR}}
, such that UVmodes cannot stretch to become IRmodes. The TCCsummarises this as the condition that
k>l_{\textnormal{P}}^{-1}
modes cannot become
k<H
modes.
This preserves unitarity in the EFTdescription: no new Fourier modes have to be added to the Hilbert space, since all trans-Planckian modes remain super-Hubble
Brandenberger 2021a
;
Brandenberger et al. 2024
;
Brandenberger 2021b
;
Brandenberger and Kamali 2022
;
Brahma et al. 2021a
. A simple UVcutoff would have to be constantly adjusted with expanding space, respectively the associated Hilbert space would be time-dependent, with the degrees of freedom increasing over time, which leads to a non-unitary evolution
Berera et al. 2020
.

The finding that in an expanding background trans-Planckian modes should be hidden, as there cannot be a UVcutoff in an expanding background, leads us to a CCCanalogy
Brahma 2020b
:
While in the CCCthe naked singularity behind the BHhorizon is shielded, the TCCassures that trans-Planckian modes are and remain behind the Hubble horizon
Brandenberger 2021a
;
Brandenberger 2019
;
Brandenberger 2021b
.

How are quantum effects to be interpreted in the light of the TCC?

Quantum effects are essential for various models of inflation and large structure formation, as well as particle physics and the description of our Earthly environment — they can even be observed in a lab —; to exclude them would miss the point in trying to find an EFTthat describes our Universe
Saito et al. 2020
.
The TCCexplicitly rules out
trans-Planckian
physics. But how is this rather vague statement to be interpreted? There are different venues to explore
Saito et al. 2020
:

•

The TCCexcludes trans-Planckian effects for a certain time, such that trans-Planckian quantum fluctuations would only classicalise after the TCC-safe phase, where the EFTis not assumed to hold any longer.
334
334

334

The TCCassumes that a problematic mode actually existed in the far past. But if the corresponding fluctuation simply did not exist in the past, it cannot exit the Hubble horizon and the trans-Planckian problem does not arise in practise
Dvali et al. 2020
.

•

The TCCexcludes the observability of trans-Planckian effects, i.e. TCC-violating processes are allowed, but are not (yet) observable.

•

The TCCgives trans-Planckian effects a negligible probability, i.e. TCC-violations are sufficiently suppressed.

If not stated otherwise, our interpretation of the TCCthroughout this paper is that trans-Planckian effects are allowed, but do not classicalise within what we call the lifetime of the universe, i.e. trans-Planckian effects might become observable in the future, at a time the EFTdescription breaks down.

Saito et al. 2020
argue that a probabilistic treatment, where TCC-violations just have to be sufficiently suppressed, is not promising: even though it works as a swampland condition, as it constrains EFTs, it does not work as a reliable censorship of trans-Planckian effects from a quantum mechanical standpoint. In particular, such a probabilistic view does not noticeably change predictions for expectation values of observables, as the suppressed outliers do not contribute enough to averages.

Dvali et al. 2020
state that dSquantum fluctuations are typically produced with wavelength
\sim 1/H
, much shorter modes are only produced at a rate
\sim\exp\left(-1/H\lambda\right)
. This makes trans-Planckian modes extremely rare: for
H\ll M_{\textnormal{P}}
there is one such mode per Hubble patch per time interval
\tau\sim 1/\left(H\exp\left(-1/H\lambda\right)\right)

Dvali et al. 2020
.
Nevertheless,
Mondal 2022
uses such considerations to make an informed modification of the wave function of the universe:
The Einstein–Hilbert action is complemented by a complex boundary term. This extra term yields a lower Hubble parameter after inflation, which changes the primordial powerspectrum in a non–scale invariant fashion. Since current observations indicate a scale-invariant powerspectrum, this modification has to be weak, ergo, the energy scale of inflation has to be low, which is in line with TCCpredictions. The real part of the extra term yields a phase factor to the wave function, while the complex part yields an exponentially suppressed addition. This can be interpreted as an exponentially suppressed probability to end up in a TCC-violating state.

How is the TCCto be understood in other frames?

The TCCcan be formulated in the Jordan frame, instead of in the Einstein frame
Guleryuz 2021
:
In the Jordan frame, the Planck mass can be regarded as dynamical, respectively, the cutoff scale is not necessarily the Planck mass but, for example, the string scale, such that the TCCreads as
a_{\textnormal{f}}H_{\textnormal{f}}<a_{\textnormal{i}}M_{\textnormal{P}}(\phi)
respectively as
a_{\textnormal{f}}H_{\textnormal{f}}<a_{\textnormal{i}}M_{\textnormal{S}}
, which leads to the modified constraints

II.18
Unique Geodesic Conjecture

A
marked
335
335

335

A marked moduli space is a moduli space of a physical theory with a given basis choice for the observables — such a space is also known as Teichmüller space
Raman and Vafa 2024
.

moduli space
\hat{\mathcal{M}}
has at most one geodesic between any two points in
\hat{\mathcal{M}}
and exactly one shortest path, which is piece-wise geodesic, between any pair of points in
\hat{\mathcal{M}}
that are separated by a finite distance
Raman and Vafa 2024
.
This implies that
\hat{\mathcal{M}}
is contractible,
336
336

336

This is also an implication of the cobordism conjecture (
section

II.2
)
Raman and Vafa 2024
.

and that there is at most one geodesic between each pair of points in
\hat{\mathcal{M}}
at asymptotically infinite distance, i.e. if there is a geodesic between two points, this geodesic is unique
Raman and Vafa 2024
.

II.18.1
Implications for Cosmology

No direct cosmological implications of this conjecture have been worked out as of today. We can only hint at some very speculative ideas of our own.

Dark Energy

If moduli spaces are contractible, this limits the number of possible vacua. This could put strong constraints on the possible values for a cosmological constant. On the one hand, this could explain the specific value we measure today for the cosmological constant. On the other hand, it could render the number of available vacua so small that the anthropic principle cannot be invoked to explain its smallness (see also our discussion in
section

II.8.1
).

Inflation

If moduli space is contractible, eternal inflation cannot take place, as this would require non-trivial cycles through moduli space, which might be incompatible with contractability. Contractability and unique geodesics make it more likely that cosmology evolves towards unique and clearly defined attractor solutions. This agrees with the cobordism conjecture (
eq.

11
).

II.18.2
General Remarks

Ruehle and Sung 2024
found that
un
marked spaces allow for an infinite number of geodesics between two points via flop
337
337

337

See
footnote

175
.
transitions. Furthermore, they show a correspondence between geodesics — which are second-order Partial Differential Equations (PDEs) — and attractor flows — which are first-order PDEsgiven by the gradient flow along a potential. The flow lines are geodesics
Ruehle and Sung 2024
. Showing uniqueness of a first-order PDEis simpler
Ruehle and Sung 2024
: attractor points are dense in moduli space
Lam and Tripathy 2024
and the flows are unique; therefore, the corresponding geodesics are unique on a dense set of points. Smoothness of moduli space metrics implies then that the geodesics are unique on the entire (marked) moduli space, except for the boundaries of the moduli space. However, flow lines could intersect multiple times between two points in a marked moduli space. This would spoil the uniqueness.

Ooguri and Vafa 2007
conjecture that moduli spaces have negative scalar curvature in the asymptotic limits. This was found not to be true
Trenner and Wilson 2010
;
Marchesano et al. 2024a
;
Wilson 2006
;
Marchesano et al. 2024b
: the curvature in the asymptotic limit can be positive, and even diverge to
+\infty
.
Marchesano et al. 2024a
conjecture that in the case of a divergence, the EFTdecouples from gravity, indicating that the moduli space is compatible with QG.
Marchesano et al. 2024b
found that in the case of curvature divergence, a
rigid
4d EFT
338
338

338

Consider an EFTwith cutoff scale
\Lambda_{\text{EFT}}
. When approaching this energy scale, this EFTlooses validity. However, it is possible that a sub-sector of this EFTdecouples from gravity, keeping some non-gravitational gauge interactions and kinetic terms finite. This sub-sector does not include the infinite distance limit along which the
full
EFTbrakes down.
Marchesano et al. 2024b
;
Marchesano et al. 2024a
refer to a theory that corresponds to such a sub-sector as a
rigid field theory
.

decouples from gravity and the moduli field curvature is
R\sim\left(\Lambda_{\text{WGC}}/\Lambda_{\text{S}}\right)^{2n}
, connecting the SSCcutoff
\Lambda_{\text{S}}
with the WGCcutoff
\Lambda_{\text{WGC}}=g_{\text{rigid}}M_{\textnormal{P};d}^{\left(d-2\right)/2}
.
339
339

339

The integer
n\in\{1,\,2,\,3\}
indicates what kind of UVcompletion the rigid theory might have (
n=1
a 6d little string theory,
n=2
a 6d superCFT,
n=3
a 5d superCFT). Having a simple quantity like scalar curvature encoding the UVbehaviour of an EFTis a remarkable finding that raises hopes about what might be possible by considering more complicated quantities like the curvature tensor
Marchesano et al. 2024b
.

The scalar moduli field curvature can therefore be regarded as a measure of the relative strength of the gauge interactions in terms of gravitational interactions, i.e. a gauge interaction that is parametrically stronger than gravity leads to a positive divergence in moduli field space
Marchesano et al. 2024b
.

Cecotti 2021
note that the divergence disappears when choosing a different metric (the Hodge metric instead of the Weil–Petersson metric)
Marchesano et al. 2024a
.

II.18.3
Evidence

This conjecture was recently proposed by
Raman and Vafa 2024
.

II.19
Weak Gravity Conjecture

Gravity is the weakest force. No observation has been made so far to contradict this statement. The WGCstates that this is true for all string theoretical compactifications, and that there is always a
340
340

340

Gravity is not necessarily the weakest force for
all
particles in the theory, e.g. the strong force acts more weakly on the electron than gravity
Palti 2020a
.

stable particle, which repulsive gauge force exceeds the attractive gravitational force
Cota et al. 2022
.
The general requirement of gravity being the weakest force can be concretised in various ways, with different
\order{1}
constants as a threshold for the charge-to-mass ratio

\displaystyle 1

(458a)

\displaystyle\sqrt{G}

(458b)

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\absolutevalue{q}}{m}\right|_{\text{ext BH}}.

(458c)

Equation

458a
is presented in the original paper
Arkani-Hamed et al. 2007b
.

Equation

458b
attempts to take more care of the
\order{1}
constants involved.

Equation

458c
states that there is a (superextremal) particle with a charge-to-mass ratio greater than (or equal to) the charge-to-mass ratio of an extremal BH.
341
341

341

For an extremal BHthe charge (or spin) equals the mass in appropriate units (
Q=M
), and the inner and outer horizons overlap (
r_{\heartsuit}=M\pm\sqrt{M^{2}-Q^{2}}
).

A more formal definition of the WGCreads as follows:
In a theory with a
U(1)
gauge symmetry; gauge coupling
g
; and action

S=\int\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\sqrt{-g}\left[\left(M_{\textnormal{P};d}\right)^{d-2}\frac{R_{d}}{2}-\frac{F^{2}}{4g^{2}}+\dots\right],

(459)

with
d
the number of dimensions of the EFT,
R_{d}
the
d
-dimensional Ricci scalar,
F^{2}=\frac{1}{q!}F_{\mu_{1}...\mu_{q}}F^{\mu_{1}...\mu_{q}}
the strength of the gauge field
A_{\nu...}

Heidenreich et al. 2016a
, e.g. with the relation
F_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{[\mu}A_{\nu]}
, and
M_{\textnormal{P};d}
the
d
-dimensional Planck mass; a particle with mass

m\leq\sqrt{\frac{d-2}{d-3}}gq\left(M_{\textnormal{P};d}\right)^{\frac{d-2}{2}},

(460)

q
the charge of the particle; exists
Palti 2019
;
Heidenreich et al. 2016a
;
Lee et al. 2018
.
342
342

342

It is conjectured that the equality can only be reached by BPSstates under supersymmetry
Ooguri and Spodyneiko 2017
;
Ooguri and Vafa 2017
;
Cota et al. 2022
;
Arkani-Hamed et al. 2007b
;
Banks et al. 2006
.

Freivogel and Kleban 2016
state that

‘[t]he weak gravity conjecture is a kind of anti-BPS bound: it says the mass of a certain charged particle must be smaller or equal to the minimum value it could have in a supersymmetric theory.’

The reason it can only be saturated under supersymmetry comes from the link between the mass and the gauge coupling that the WGCestablishes: it connects Poincaré and internal symmetries, which is, according to the Coleman–Mandula theorem
Coleman and Mandula 1967
, only allowed in supersymmetry
Prieto 2024
.

Equation

460
is called the
electric weak gravity conjecture
. Applying it to the magnetic dual field

(461)

which states the magnetic Coulomb force acts more strongly on the monopole of the smallest magnetic charge than the Newtonian gravitational force

This has the interesting consequence that an EFTin the limit of vanishing coupling is valid nowhere.
