We see that there is a whole family of conjectures, each with the goal of establishing the general rule of
‘gravity is the weakest force’
with a twist, some are debunked with counterexamples, others have more evidence in their favour, and all make slightly different predictions for possible observables. In the following, we discuss the implications for cosmology.

II.19.1
Implications for Cosmology

The WGCis of particular interest, as it might be a conjecture with testable implications for low-energy physics
Harlow et al. 2023
. For the SM, the implications are feeble
Harlow et al. 2023
: The electron satisfies the WGC-bound by more than 20 orders of magnitude, and since the electron charge is almost of order one, the tower of charged particles predicted by the TWGC/sLWGCcould start around the Planck mass.
Some implications are reassuring: the neutrino is electrically neutral, photons are massless
358
358

358

See
section

II.19.1
for further details.

Reece 2019
, and electric charge is quantised
Abu-Ajamieh et al. 2024a
.
More exciting predictions are the upper bound on the proton lifetime of
{10}^{42}
years
Abu-Ajamieh et al. 2024a
, and the existence of magnetic monopoles. Because the mass of the magnetic monopole could be around the Planck scale, its existence is currently not falsifiable.
Furthermore, an additional aspect that might be of interest is the finding that the Bell inequalities
Bell 1964
cannot be violated if the WGCdoes not hold, indicating that there were hidden variables in quantum theory if gravity was not the weakest force
Sinha and Zahed 2023
.
359
359

359

Violating the Bell inequalities might give rise to cosmological observations
Choudhury et al. 2017
;
Maldacena 2016
.

Axion models,

be it for inflation, hyperinflation, EDE, or quintessence, are constrained by the axionic WGC

S_{\iota}\leq M_{\textnormal{P}}/f

(462)

with

S_{\iota}
the instanton
360
360

360

Instantons are objects charged under axions, lack time-evolution, and their action is the equivalent to the tension
Heidenreich et al. 2016a
. Instantons are not physical objects like particles or branes, but events that are introduced as a tool for computation
Stout 2020
.

action
361
361

361

The action term
S_{\iota}
plays the role of the mass
Junghans 2016
.

, and

f
the axion
362
362

362

By an axion we mean any shift-symmetric periodic scalar. The axion period is called the axion decay constant
f
. The inverse axion decay constant plays the role of the gauge coupling
Junghans 2016
.

decay constant
Blumenhagen 2018
;
Gonzalo and Ibáñez 2019
;
Harlow et al. 2023
;
Hebecker et al. 2018
;
Grimm and van de Heisteeg 2020
;
Montero et al. 2015
;
Heidenreich et al. 2020
;
Hebecker et al. 2017b
;
Kooner et al. 2016
;
Choi et al. 2021
.
This bound is motivated from multiple angles:
focussing on
S_{\iota}
, focussing on
f
, and focussing on the combined bound
fS_{\iota}
.

S_{\iota}

needs to be large enough to guarantee perturbative control over the EFT, as in a string compactification,
S_{\iota}
is set by the volume in string units of the internal cycle wrapped by the instanton
Cicoli et al. 2023b
.
The instanton expansion breaks down when
S_{\iota}<1
, and new, light states appear
Harlow et al. 2023
;
Stout 2020
.
363
363

363

Stout 2020
notes that it poses an issue that the interesting limit of small instanton action is ill-defined if one wants to study how QGprevents global symmetries. He finds that it might be better to opt for a different formalism based on the Yang–Lee theory of phase transitions, which allows studying the different energy levels of the instantons: There will be an axion field value
\phi_{*}
for which the energy gap
\Delta E
between the energy of a state and the vacuum is minimal. The WGCcan then be expressed as
\sqrt{\Delta E(\phi_{*})/\Delta E^{\prime\prime}(\phi_{*})}\lesssim M_{\textnormal{P}}/f
, which remains well-defined in the limit of vanishing instanton action.

S_{\iota}
plays a key role in what could be considered a magnetic form of the axionic WGC:
Studying global U(1) symmetries Higgsed under axions,
Daus et al. 2020
find the condition

e^{-S_{\iota}}\gtrsim e^{-M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}/\Lambda^{2}},

(463)

which they call the
Swampland Global Symmetries Conjecture
. The bound relates the cutoff scale
\Lambda
to the action of the instanton
S_{\iota}
.

f\leq M_{\text{P}}

is another condition to be in a regime of perturbative control
Brown et al. 2015
;
Dolan et al. 2017
;
Hebecker et al. 2018
;
Goswami 2019
.
This rules out trans-Planckian field ranges
Draper 2018
:
Axions fragment into inhomogeneous modes on a timescale
\log(t)\sim m/fqg
. A slowly fragmenting scalar moves a distance
\Delta\phi\lesssim M_{\textnormal{P}}m/fqg
. Applying the WGCyields
\Delta\phi\lesssim M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}/f
. Requiring
f<M_{\textnormal{P}}
rules out trans-Planckian field ranges.

At first, it seems that models with multiple fields can circumvent the constraint by having the individual decay constants on a sub-Planckian scale, but the effective decay constant trans-Planckian.
The alignment of multiple fields
Kaplan and Rattazzi 2016
;
Choi and Im 2016
;
Choi et al. 2014
;
Higaki and Takahashi 2014
;
Hebecker et al. 2019b
;
Kim et al. 2005
;
Reig 2021
, where the parent QGtheory satisfies the WGC
Saraswat 2017
, would be such a mechanism.
However, while
Montero et al. 2015
show that kinetic alignment yields field ranges
\sim\sqrt{\mathfrak{N}}f_{\text{max}}
(with
f_{\text{max}}
the maximal axion periodicity and
\mathfrak{N}
the number of axions), which corresponds to travelling diagonally through the charge lattice, and

Bachlechner et al. 2016
find
S_{\text{min}}\gtrsim\sqrt{\mathfrak{N}}M_{\textnormal{P}}/f_{\text{max}}
, such that gravitational instanton contributions with
f_{\text{max}}=M_{\textnormal{P}}
are still under control,

Rudelius 2015a
;
Rudelius 2015b
shows that in a theory with
\mathfrak{N}
axions and
\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{N}}\left(f_{i}S_{i}\right)^{2}\leq 1
, such that the convex hull conjecture is satisfied,
the effective axion decay constant
f_{\text{eff}}\sim f_{i}S_{i}
scales like
1/\sqrt{\mathfrak{N}}
,
because one also aligns the charge vectors
\vec{z}_{i}=\sum_{j}\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{f_{ij}S_{i}}\vec{e}_{j}
(with
\left\{\vec{e}_{j}\right\}
an orthonormal basis of the vector space), such that the charge vectors have to increase in length to still span the unit ball at smaller angles separating them.
This cancels the effect of alignment and a trans-Planckian field range is required (but forbidden), unless there are additional potential contributions or multiple windings of the instanton, such that the moduli space can be traversed multiple times. The observation can be summarised in the statement that the moduli space for axions has a radius
r\lesssim\pi M_{\textnormal{P}}

Rudelius 2015b
;
Bachlechner et al. 2015a
.
Even when travelling diagonally through axion space, the axion moduli space is bounded by the WGC, and large decay constants are simply in the swampland.
Moreover,
Conlon 2012
finds that the CEBis violated if
\sum_{i}f_{i}^{2}>\order{1}M_{\textnormal{P}}
.
Furthermore, having a large number of axions
\mathfrak{N}
seems problematic in itself: either, the number of instantons grows larger than
\mathfrak{N}^{2}
, probably even exponentially, or there has to be a loophole that allows the axions to traverse a trans-Planckian field range
Junghans 2016
.
For a U(1) gauge theory with a 2-form gauge potential, the bound
\Lambda\lesssim\sqrt{fM_{\textnormal{P}}}
is motivated in analogy to the magnetic WGC
Reece 2019
;
Craig and Garcia 2018
.

fS_{\iota}\sim 1

is a bound that should hold in string theory, since the axion decay constant and the instanton action are related to the saxion
\varphi
, such that

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{\varphi}

(464)

\displaystyle S_{\iota}

\displaystyle\sim\varphi

(465)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow fS_{\iota}

\displaystyle\sim 1,

(466)

which shows that the large-field regime
f>1
is inaccessible in the instanton expansion with
S_{\iota}>1

Blumenhagen 2018
.
The same relation can also be understood as an analogy to the (non-axionic) WGC:

\left.\frac{q_{\iota}}{S_{\iota}}\right|_{\text{WGC}}\geq\left.\frac{q_{\iota}}{S_{\iota}}\right|_{\text{ext}}\sim\frac{f}{M_{\textnormal{P}}},

(467)

with
q_{\iota}
the instanton charge; to strengthen the analogy, one can require that this relation is satisfied by the instanton with the smallest action
364
364

364

Hebecker et al. 2019b
suggest that axion potentials with large monotonic regions — which they construct in a type IIB setting — might parametrically violate this strong form of the axionic WGC.

Kooner et al. 2016
or the one of lowest charge
365
365

365

This immediately opens up the loophole that the WGC-instanton is not the one of lowest charge
Brown et al. 2016
;
Hebecker et al. 2015a
;
Brown et al. 2015
;
Rudelius 2015b
.

Hebecker et al. 2018
;
Grimm and van de Heisteeg 2020
;
Montero et al. 2015
;
Heidenreich et al. 2020
;
Harlow et al. 2023
.

Heidenreich et al. 2015
mention that in a 4d theory with a U(1) plus a KKU(1), the axion decay constant
f
grows like
1/\sqrt{S_{\iota}}
, which leaves a small loophole for trans-Planckian decay constants compatible with the WGC(and in particular with the convex hull conjecture).

Counterexamples

to the axionic WGCare presented in the form of Einstein–Maxwell–dilaton theory
Loges et al. 2020a
and Einstein–axion–dilaton theory
Andriolo et al. 2020
, which violate the axionic WGC, unless positivity bounds on higher-derivative operators
Adams et al. 2006
, and in the latter case additionally symmetries on the higher-order derivatives (which are inherited from the UVtheory), are taken into account
Loges et al. 2020b
(see
Andriolo et al. 2020
for more comments on the necessity but insufficiency of positivity bounds).

Furthermore, warped geometries with open strings might provide a counterexample to the axionic WGC
Kooner et al. 2016
: in the limits of supergravity, where instanton effects are exponentially suppressed, large axionic decay constants are generated with
M_{\textnormal{P}}/f_{\iota}<S_{\iota}
. Either, the instantons couple to the open string axions with a suppressed effective axion coupling
f^{\prime}<f
or new stringy effects appear in that limit, such that the effective coupling is smaller — or the axionic WGCis incorrect
Kooner et al. 2016
.

Black Holes

The magnetic WGCdemands that the minimally charged magnetic object is not a black hole/brane
Hebecker et al. 2017c
, i.e. the magnetic monopole is not a BH
Harlow et al. 2023
;
Heidenreich et al. 2017
.
The cutoff scale of the theory is related to the classical radius of the magnetic monopole
Heidenreich et al. 2017
.
366
366

366

The technicalities are laid out by
Saraswat 2017
:
Since a BHis a classical concept, all charges carried by BHsare macroscopic and continuous
Rojo 2019
.
Continuous gauge symmetries are compact in QG
Banks and Seiberg 2011
.
Therefore, the magnetic charge in BHsolutions obeys the Dirac quantisation
q_{\textnormal{m}}=2\pi n/g
with
n\in\mathbb{Z}
(to avoid exact global symmetries
de la Fuente et al. 2015
), and the extremal magnetically charged BHat zero temperature has a mass of
m_{\text{BH}}=q_{\textnormal{m}}
and a large entropy
S\sim g^{-2}
, i.e. it has a large number of degenerate states. There is no apparent reason to believe that magnetic charge can only appear in such a high-entropy setting. Therefore, it is expected that there is a magnetic monopole with mass
m_{\textnormal{m}}\sim 1/g^{2}l
that is not a BH, i.e. it is an extended object — meaning that it is bigger than the Schwarzschild radius, ergo
l\gtrsim 1/g^{2}lM_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}
. It follows that
1/l\lesssim gM_{\textnormal{P}}
, which shows the connection to the magnetic WGC:
\Lambda_{\text{EFT}}\sim 1/l\lesssim gM_{\textnormal{P}}
.

The electric WGCrequires BHsto decay
Fisher and Mogni 2017
;
Cota et al. 2022
;
Arkani-Hamed et al. 2007b
;
Banks et al. 2006
:
367
367

367

Studying the full non-linear effects of charged extremal BHsin Einstein–Born–Infeld Gravity,
Wu and Lu 2024
find that
‘large extremal particle-like black holes may break up and decay even when gravity is not the weaker force’
.

Assume you start with a large BH. The BHemits particles through Hawking radiation. Since Hawking radiation is a charge-neutral process, the BHsheds its mass, yet not its charge, i.e. at one point it will be so strongly charged that the electromagnetic repulsion overcomes the gravitational attraction — if gravity remains the weakest force.
368
368

368

Forming a superextremal BHby overcharging an extremal BH, i.e. by throwing in an additional charged particle, does not work
Anand et al. 2024
: the Coulomb repulsion is too strong, and the particle will never reach the horizon
Izumi et al. 2024
.

If the WGCis violated, BHsdo not decay and form stable remnants:
The number of particles of mass
m
and charge
q
a BHcan emit is
n=Q/q
, with
Q
the BHcharge, resulting in a total mass of
m\cdot n=mQ/q<M
, where the BHmass
M
cannot be reached due to conservation of (binding) energy. When the extremality
Q/M=1
is reached, the BHtemperature is zero
369
369

369

In theories with a dilaton, this is not necessarily the case, but the Bekenstein–Hawking entropy approaches zero in the extremal limit
Heidenreich 2020
.

,
Hawking radiation stops, and the BHis semiclassically stable
Montero et al. 2016
.
Assuming a very weak gauge coupling of
g\sim${10}^{-100}$
and a BHof several Planck masses, e.g.
M\sim 10M_{\textnormal{P}}
, the BHcan have any charge in between 0 and
Q\sim${10}^{100}$
and still satisfy
M>gQM_{\textnormal{P}}

Arkani-Hamed et al. 2007b
. The quantum-mechanical totalitarian principle
370
370

370

The quantum-mechanical totalitarian principle
‘everything that is not forbidden is compulsory’
(which is actually not the totalitarian principle, but the principle of plenitude) is often attributed to Gell-Mann, even though he wrote
‘[a]nything that is not compulsory is forbidden’

Gell-Mann 1956
(which is the actual totalitarian principle)
Kragh 2019
.

would assure that each possible state gets realised, and we end up with
\sim${10}^{100}$
remnants
Arkani-Hamed et al. 2007b
.
Remnants represent naked singularities — which violate the CCC — and as such global symmetries — which violate the no global symmetries conjecture (
section

II.10
).
371
371

371

The compatibility of the existence of remnants with QGis still under debate
Giddings 1992
;
Palti 2019
;
van Beest et al. 2022
;
Heidenreich et al. 2017
, e.g. disfavoured by
Susskind 1995
;
Banks et al. 2006
but found to be unproblematic by
Casini 2008
.
We further discuss the question of stable remnants in
sections

II.19.2
,
II.2.1
and
II.10.1
.
Moreover, new research on the possible decay paths of BHsmight indicate that a BHis never at risk of becoming superextremal and remnants might be avoided entirely
Brown et al. 2024
;
Davies et al. 2024
.

The WGCassures that this situation is avoided: The WGCpredicts the existence of a particle with
q/m>1
, which the BHcan emit through Schwinger pair production.
372
372

372

Hawking radiation is the dominant process for BHdischarge if the temperature is high compared to the BHmass; if the temperature is low compared to the BHmass, Schwinger pair production is dominating
Furuuchi 2018
.
Both processes can be interpreted as a form of quantum tunnelling
Parikh and Wilczek 2000
;
Furuuchi 2018
. This means that even an exponentially suppressed decay rate represents an instability of the BH, which satisfies the WGC.
The decay does not need to be efficient
Furuuchi 2018
: if arbitrarily large RNBHswere requested to decay efficiently, charged particles with arbitrarily small mass were required to be present in the theory, which is not even true for the SM, as there is no massless charged particle.

Lin and Shiu 2024
find that the Schwinger rate declines and vanishes when a particle’s charge-to-mass ratio approaches the BHextremality bound, satisfying the WGC.

The BHcan shed its charge and ceases to be extremal.

For a BHin an Einstein–Maxwell universe, with an action
Kats et al. 2007
given by

{AmSalign}

The positivity bound can be studied on the level of the Lagrangian.
That contributions of the form
\left(F_{\mu\nu}\right)^{2}

Cano et al. 2020

Cano et al. 2019
;
Cano et al. 2020
find that in Einstein–Maxwell–Dilaton theory or more generally heterotic string theories, the corrections are either positive or null (meaning that the next higher-order term is absent).
As an interesting aside, in their setup, the Wald entropy ceases to be correlated to the charge-to-mass ratio. This implies that in a setting where the perturbation of the entropy is positive, the WGCis not automatically implied.
The same observation is made by
Aalsma 2022
, when studying corrections to extremal BHsusing the Iyer-Wald formalism.
Furthermore, for black branes, it can be shown that corrections to entropy are not necessarily correlated with corrections to extremality
Sadeghi et al. 2022d
, but if four-derivative couplings obey scattering positivity bounds, higher-order derivative corrections decrease the tension-to-charge ratio of extremal black branes
Noumi and Satake 2022
.
Taking into account higher-order corrections to the static, spherically symmetric, extremal solution of non-vanishing horizon area,
Etheredge and Heidenreich 2022
show that mass, entropy, and self-force corrections are of independent and arbitrary sign. Furthermore, they note — referring to
McPeak 2022
—  that the entropy corrections at fixed mass and charge near extremality can diverge and are an independent quantity from extremal entropy corrections, which are finite.

Henriksson et al. 2022
claim that a small violation of the bound might be allowed, referring to work that shows a reduced EFTcutoff scale
Bellazzini et al. 2019
;
Alberte et al. 2020
, but also stating that a small violation of the bound itself would indicate a weakening of causality, i.e. superluminality
380
380

380

‘Roughly, the resolution seems to be that gravitational interactions universally cause a time delay, so EFT operators that cause a time advance are allowed in principle as long as the advance is smaller than the gravitational time delay’

Furthermore,
Aalsma 2022
worries that small BHsmight experience different corrections than large BHs: On the one hand, large BHscould be above the EFTcutoff scale. On the other hand, the ratio between the local and the non-local corrections to the gravitational EoMis
\sim\left(r_{\heartsuit}/l_{\textnormal{P}}\right)^{2}
, with
r_{\heartsuit}
the radius of the BHhorizon and
l_{\textnormal{P}}
the Planck length scale, i.e. for large BHsthe classical / local corrections dominate
Arfaei et al. 2023
.
If higher-derivative terms make small BHssuperextremal, then they also have to make them self-repulsive
Gendler and Valenzuela 2021
: If not, two such superextremal BHscould form a gravitationally bound state with twice the charge and less than twice the mass, i.e. with a higher charge-to-mass ratio. This is an important observation for the Repulsive Force Conjecture (
section

II.19.4
) (RFC).
At least for small BHsin AdSspace, the extremal mass decreases in a canonical ensemble if unitarity and the NECare given
Aalsma 2022
.

Large BHscontaining multiple charges are able to decay when the convex hull of the charge-to-mass ratio of small BHscontains the unit ball — regarding four-derivative corrections, the space of allowed charge-to-mass ratios is convex, as the corrections are smaller than the two-derivative terms; this simplifies the convex hull requirement to requiring a positive shift to extremality
Jones and McPeak 2020
.

381

See

BHsdo not exist in isolation. The implications of the presence of additional fields is studied by various authors:

•

Studying RNBHsolutions in dSspacetime with a varying cosmological constant,
Lüben et al. 2021
find that
m^{2}>qgM_{\textnormal{P}}H
is necessary to avoid super-extremality of the BH: such a BHdischarges with the Schwinger rate
\Gamma\sim\exp\left(-m^{2}/qgE\right)

Fröb et al. 2014
;
Ai and Drewes 2020
;
Brown 2019
;
Gould et al. 2019
;
Qiu and Sorbo 2020
, i.e. it discharges rapidly for
m^{2}\ll qE
and evolves towards a superextremal neutral Nariai solution, which lies outside of physical phase space (as they show); therefore it violates the CCC. This observation was motivated by a dSversion of the WGC — the FLB.

•

Chen et al. 2020
study RNand Bardeen BHsin AdSspacetimes with quintessence, and find that larger AdSradii make it easier to satisfy the WGC. Furthermore, they note that negative corrections to the quintessence density decreases the charge-to-mass ratio of the BH.

•

For extremal Kerr–Newman AdSBHs,
Sadeghi et al. 2022c
find that the presence of quintessence decreases the charge-to-mass ratio, as the added correction is inversely proportional to the entropy.

There are a few additional aspects where insights about BHsfrom the WGCcan be gained:

•

In 4-dimensional asymptotically flat RNor GBspacetimes, an innermost stable circular orbit exists, unless the WGCis violated and the charge-to-mass ratio is greater than unity
Paul and Bhamidipati 2024
.

•

Alipour et al. 2024
study RNBHsin Kiselev spacetime and find that smaller photon spheres are associated with larger charge-to-mass ratios
Q/M
of the BHs, which yields a potential testing ground for the WGC, given enough observational precision of BHphoton spheres, respectively the absence of a photon sphere violates the WGCin their setting.

•

The deformability of BHscan be studied using tidal Love numbers, as work in an early stage by
De Luca et al. 2023
indicates that there is a relation between positivity of tidal Love numbers and higher-order corrections through the WGC.

•

Another open question is how rotation affects the WGCbounds:
On the one hand, rotation seems to have an impact on the corrections to positivity bounds
Aalsma 2022
;
Reall and Santos 2019
;
Cano and Ruipérez 2019
.
On the other hand, BHscan lose angular momentum through superradiance
Aalsma and Shiu 2022
. Furthermore, in pure gravity in six or more dimensions, the angular momentum of BHsis unconstrained
Myers and Perry 1986
.

Aalsma and Shiu 2022
examine a class of BHsthat allow a mapping of rotating Kerr–Newman solutions to charged but non-rotating KKBHs, without finding consistent corrections to WGCbounds.

Ma et al. 2023
discuss rotating black strings, where the higher-order corrections induce a
negative
entropy shift, spoiling the mutually re-enforcing bond between the WGCand the CCC.

Cosmic Censorship

The CCCrequests that naked singularities must be behind a horizon
Harlow et al. 2023
;
Wald 1997
;
Cui and Wang 2024
;
Penrose 2002
.
No naked singularity has been observed, and numerical simulations suggest that even in higher-dimensional spaces, quantum corrections would restore the censorship for proposed counterexamples
Harlow et al. 2023
;
Gregory and Laflamme 1993
;
Lehner and Pretorius 2011
;
Emparan 2020
;
Crisford and Santos 2017
;
Horowitz et al. 2016
;
Crisford et al. 2018
;
Horowitz and Santos 2019
;
Figueras et al. 2016
;
Figueras et al. 2017
;
Lehner and Pretorius 2010
.
To satisfy the CCCrespecetively the WGC, a particle of a certain minimum charge has to be present in the theory
Cui and Wang 2024
;
Horowitz and Santos 2019
. However, the charge bounds are not necessarily equal, as examples exist where lower bounds to the CCCexist but do not agree to the WGCbound
Horowitz and Santos 2019
;
Song et al. 2021
;
Song et al. 2024
;
Hu et al. 2020
.
Some weaker forms of the WGCallow the superextremal particles to be BHsthemselves, yet superextremal BHswould represent naked singularities, and are therefore incompatible with the CCC, which rules out those weaker forms of the WGC
Sadeghi et al. 2024
;
Sadeghi and Gashti 2024
.

Sadeghi et al. 2023b
come to the conclusion that for charged BHsin dSspace, the CCCis (not) satisfied whenever the WGCis (not) obeyed.
For AdSspaces,
Anand et al. 2024
find that the WGCprevents a violation of the CCC: The CCCcould be violated if one was able to overcharge a RNBHby throwing in an additional particle. As they show, the WGCassures that all available particles that could be thrown into the BHincrease the entropy and enlarge the horizon, such that the CCCis always satisfied. Exploring the other direction, they also show that if the central charge of a radiating extremal BHsurpasses the electric charge, superradiant WGC-particles are emitted that move the BHaway from the extremal state, protecting the CCC.
Also
Crisford and Santos 2017
adumbrate that the mechanism they propose to violate cosmic censorship fails if the WGCholds.
For quintessence, the compatibility of the CCCand the WGCis shown
Alipour et al. 2023a
;
Alipour et al. 2023b
.
We conclude that gravity’s weakness seems to prevent naked singularities
Crisford et al. 2018
, in dSas well as in AdSspaces.
382
382

382

Implicitly, we were referring to the weak CCCin this section. There is a strong form that states that all processes described by GRremain predictable throughout the entire spacetime.
Tu et al. 2024
find that processes that violate the strong CCCrequire WGC-violating particles and BHswith short hair (deviations from the RNmetric near a BHhorizon, i.e. there are localised deviations for interactions near the horizon).

It is perhaps ironic that for many years people hoped that cosmic censorship would fail so that we had the possibility of observing effects of quantum gravity. Now we find that a conjecture about quantum gravity is preserving cosmic censorship. It appears that quantum gravity wants to remain hidden.
Horowitz and Santos 2019

Dark energy

faces model-dependent constraints. Especially, models that couple DEto DMhave to make sure that the coupling is in such a way that gravity remains the weakest force.
In the following, we start with general statements and transition into more concrete findings about the implications of the WGCfor DE.

From
eq.

537
we can see that the mass scale of a magnetic monopole
383
383

383

A ’t Hooft–Polyakov monopole
Agmon et al. 2023
.

is
m\sim\Lambda/g^{2}
; its size is
\sim 1/\Lambda

Huang et al. 2006
;
Huang 2008a
. If one assumes that the magnetic monopole is smaller than the universe,
L\geq 1/\Lambda
, and that the minimally charged monopole is lighter than a Nariai BH,
m\lesssim L/G
, we can set bounds on the DEdensity
\rho_{V}
, by using the size of pure dSspace with a positive cosmological constant (
L=\sqrt{3/8\pi G\rho_{V}}
)
Huang et al. 2006
:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\sqrt{3/8\pi G\rho_{V}}

(470)

\displaystyle\geq 1/\Lambda\sim 1/mg^{2}

(471)

\displaystyle\gtrsim G/Lg^{2}

(472)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow 3/8\pi G\rho_{V}

\displaystyle\gtrsim G/g^{2}

(473)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\rho_{V}

\displaystyle\lesssim g^{2}/G^{2}\sim g^{2}M_{\textnormal{P}}^{4}.

(474)

A small coupling would therefore explain a small cosmological constant. This makes the smallness of the cosmological constant more natural, but one has to explain the smallness of the coupling.

One possibility to realise a near-Minkowski universe is by starting with an unstable dSuniverse, which decays through brane nucleation into a stable Minkowski solution. This idea is presented by
Bousso and Polchinski 2000
, and makes a flat Universe probable, which circumvents anthropic arguments. However, their realisation is in tension with the WGC
Liu et al. 2023
. Another realisation is presented by
Kaloper and Westphal 2022
. In this realisation, the tension with the WGCcan be avoided
Liu et al. 2023
.

DEin the form of a dynamical scalar field is constraint by the scalar WGC.
Table

3
gives an overview of various potentials and the constraints they face from the scalar WGC.

Axions as EDE
Poulin et al. 2018
were promising candidates for lowering the Hubble tension
Poulin et al. 2019
and have been studied in a string-theoretical context
Alexander and McDonough 2019
before, yet it appears that the WGClimits the axion decay constant
in this context
to
f<0.008M_{\textnormal{P}}
, which makes this form of EDEless likely from a theoretical standpoint
Rudelius 2023b
. In a general axion context, we mentioned the possible loophole of having a very small instanton action, which would allow the axion decay constant to be large. This loophole is not available in the EDEcontext, as the axion field needs to be light
Rudelius 2023b
.

Cicoli et al. 2023b
do not share these conclusions: they find that axionic EDEwith
f\simeq 0.2
can present a viable model which can be embedded in type IIB string theory. However, the WGCmakes fine-tuning of the model necessary, which, from our standpoint, speaks rather against such a model, even though it does not rule it out.

Axionic hilltop quintessence that is in agreement with the dSC, violates the WGCand is therefore in the swampland, unless there are heavy particles and supersymmetry that enable the axion to be slow-rolling by reducing the instanton action (
reducing
meaning to be of
\order{1}
instead of
S_{\iota}\sim\order{100}
, which is the value in the absence of heavy particles)
Ibe et al. 2019
. However,
Cicoli et al. 2022a
find that a sub-Planckian axion decay constant is easier to realise near the maximum of the axion potential, where the supersymmetry breaking scale is extremely low, i.e. axionic quintessence is rather viable in a non-supersymmetric Minkowski vacuum.
Tada and Terada 2024
derive axion-like quintessence by a reconstructing DESI-data for
\omega_{0}\omega_{a}
CDM. The potential satisfies the WGC(as well as the dSCand the DC).

Sadeghi et al. 2023c
put forward the idea that BHssurrounded by quintessence or a cloud of strings might experience a repulsive force, indicated by the WGC, which would contribute to the expansion of the Universe. BHsas drivers of cosmic expansion have been studied previously
Farrah et al. 2023
;
Parnovsky 2023
;
Avelino 2023
;
Mistele 2023
;
Lei et al. 2024
. We’d like to remark that to explain the expansion of space, it is not enough to have repulsive objects that travel through space.

Dark Matter

If DMis charged under a massless Abelian gauge field, i.e. there is a dark photon
384
384

384

See

II.19.1
for further details.

Long and Wang 2019
;

Observations indicate that DMis approximately collisionless, but collective dark plasma effects, which can lead to density fluctuations, are possible nevertheless
Heikinheimo et al. 2015

Ackerman et al. 2009

The frequency of the fluctuations in the plasma is

\omega_{\text{plasma}}=\sqrt{\frac{g^{2}\rho_{\text{DM}}}{m_{\text{DM}}^{2}}}\gtrsim\frac{\sqrt{\rho_{\text{DM}}}}{M_{\textnormal{P}}},

(475)

with
\rho
the energy density, and the inequality arises from applying the WGCbound.
The timescale for those density fluctuations (
\sim 1/\omega_{\text{plasma}}
) corresponds roughly to the timescale of galaxy cluster mergers. This means that galaxy mergers might provide evidence for the effects of the weak gauge forces between DMparticles that approximately saturate the WGCbounds. The DMdistribution in galaxy cluster collisions acts as an indicator of the gauge coupling.

Interacting dark radiation in the form of dark gluons as part of a non-Abelian gauge group in the dark sector
Buen-Abad et al. 2015

Branon DM
violates the sLWGC
Nam 2023b
:
Branons are fluctuations of our Universe’s brane, which is embedded into a higher-dimensional bulk. The model is specified by the following set of equations:

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=S_{\text{bulk}}+S_{\text{brane}}

(476)

\displaystyle S_{\text{bulk}}

\displaystyle=\frac{M_{\textnormal{P};5}^{3}}{2}\int\!\sqrt{-G}\left(R_{5}-2\Lambda_{\textnormal{cc};5}\right)\,\mathrm{d}^{5}X

(477)

\displaystyle S_{\text{brane}}

\displaystyle=\int\!\sqrt{-\tilde{g}}\left(\mathcal{T}^{4}+\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{SM}}+\dots\right)\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x

(478)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}s_{\textnormal{bulk}}^{2}

\displaystyle=G_{MN}\mathrm{d}X^{M}\mathrm{d}X^{N}

(479)

\displaystyle=g_{\mu\nu}\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}-r^{2}\left(\mathrm{d}\theta+X_{\mu}\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\right)^{2}

(480)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}s_{\text{brane}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}

(481)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=G_{MN}\partial_{\mu}Y^{M}\partial_{\nu}Y^{N}

(482)

\displaystyle=g_{\mu\nu}-r_{0}^{2}\left(X_{\mu}+\partial_{\mu}Y^{\theta}\right)\left(X_{\nu}+\partial_{\nu}Y^{\theta}\right)

(483)

\displaystyle Y^{M}

\displaystyle=\left(x^{\mu}\,,\,Y^{\theta}\left(x\right)\right),

(484)

with

Dimensional reduction of the bulk action yields an effective action in 4 dimensions:

S_{4}=\int\sqrt{g}\left(\frac{M_{\textnormal{P};4}^{2}}{2}R_{4}-\frac{X_{\mu\nu}X^{\mu\nu}}{4\kappa^{2}}\right),

(485)

with all 4d quantities, and

\kappa=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{M_{\textnormal{P};4}r_{0}}

(486)

the KKgauge coupling.

\Lambda\lesssim\kappa^{1/3}M_{\textnormal{P};4}
, i.e. the EFTbreaks down when the KKfield decouples, which puts this model of DMin the swampland.

Hierarchy Problem

Another interesting feature of the WGCis the predicted upper bound on particle masses, which can explain why the EWscale is so much lower than the Planck scale
Harlow et al. 2023
;
Cheung and Remmen 2014a
;
Lust and Palti 2018
;
Craig et al. 2019
:
The difference between the baryon number and the lepton number might be a gauge symmetry
B-L
.
385
385

385

Fermi explained the neutron decay with a four-fermion interaction Lagrangian of the form
L_{\beta}\sim G_{F}\bar{n}pe^{-}\bar{\nu}+\text{h.c.}

Reece 2023
.
This beta decay Lagrangian is compatible with two global symmetries, the baryon number, and the lepton number
Reece 2023
.
Discovering that the neutron is unstable, opened the question if the proton is unstable as well, which lead to the decay Lagrangian
L_{\text{dec}}=y_{p}pe^{-}\pi^{0}+\text{h.c.}
, which explicitly breaks the two global symmetries, but allows for the
B-L
symmetry
Reece 2023
.

If neutrinos are the WGCparticles and acquire their mass through EWsymmetry breaking, the Dirac neutrino mass is then predicted to be
m_{\nu}=y_{\nu}h/\sqrt{2}<\sqrt{2}g_{B-L}M_{\textnormal{P}}
, with
y_{\nu}\sim${10}^{-12}$
the SMvalue and
h
the Higgs scale.
386
386

386

Invoking the more stringent TWGCand sLWGC, additional predictions arise
Harlow et al. 2023
: The expected towers of
B-L
-charged particles should start at
m\sim g_{B-L}M_{\textnormal{P}}\lesssim\order{\text{keV}}
, which would indicate the existence of billions of yet undetected particles that interact with ordinary matter below the TeV-scale (if this gauge symmetry is unbroken). This is not ruled out by data, yet surprising. In the case of an unbroken
B-L
-symmetry, the cutoff scale for the EFTis around
{10}^{10}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
.

Experimental bounds on the coupling
g_{B-L}\sim${10}^{-28}$
predict then
h\lesssim${10}^{-16}$M_{\textnormal{P}}
, which is in agreement with the observed EWhierarchy.
387
387

387

Abu-Ajamieh et al. 2024a
disagree: they use experimental constraints from the LEPand the WGCto derive a lower bound of
g\gtrsim$8.7\text{\times}{10}^{-17}$
; this means that an extra U(1) cannot solve the hierarchy problem.

This is a very nice, experimentally confirmed prediction based on a swampland conjecture, unfortunately one with a big shortcoming: We just replaced the unexplained smallness of the EWhierarchy with another unexplained small number, namely the smallness of the
B-L
coupling
g_{B-L}\sim${10}^{-28}$
. This shift might be even more problematic, as it might hint at inconsistencies within the theory itself: A small coupling restores a global symmetry (see
section

II.10
)!
The issue might arise because of the attempt to relate the neutrino mass to the Higgs mass — which seems to be the obvious thing to do, but not a necessity: to relate the WGCbound to the EWscale,
a
new Abelian gauge group needs to be introduced with charged matter that obtains some of its mass from the Higgs field
Craig et al. 2019
, but this has not necessarily to be done in the form of a neutrino.

Inflation

The WGCpredicts a suppression of the scalar powerspectrum at scales
k\gtrsim$100\text{\,}{\mathrm{Mpc}}^{-1}$

Winkler et al. 2020
.

Large-field inflation
388
388

388

An interesting aspect of large-field inflation, i.e. inflationary models where the inflaton field traverses a trans-Planckian range, is that the potential is subject to all Planck suppressed corrections (because of the trans-Planckian field range), which means that large-field inflation is always sensitive to UVphysics
Brown et al. 2016
.
See the work by
Banks et al. 2003
on the viability of trans-Planckian field ranges for inflation.

seems to be in tension with the WGC
Heidenreich et al. 2015
, as the following examples will highlight. A reoccurring theme will be that the necessary trans-Planckian field ranges are in tension with the magnetic WGC. The constraints can sometimes be circumvented by adding complexity to the model, e.g. in the form of additional fields
389
389

389

Models invoking additional fields are for example pole
\mathfrak{N}
-flation (where the inflaton field are
\mathfrak{N}
open string moduli such as D3-branes)
Dias et al. 2019b
or multi axion natural inflation (see
section

II.19.1
).

The following example of axion monodromy is a large-field inflation model compatible with the WGC
Rompineve Sorbello 2017
:
The Lagrangian and potential of the model are given by

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\left(\partial\phi\right)^{2}-V\left(\phi/f\right)

(487)

\displaystyle V\left(\phi\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}\phi^{2}}{2}+\beta(\phi)\cos\left(\frac{\phi}{f}\right),

(488)

with
\phi
an axion that couples to instantons,

f
the axion decay constant,

\beta
a parameter.
Slow-roll inflation starts in a regime of large
\phi
, where the quadratic term dominates.
The field rolls into the regime where the wiggles from the cosine-terms dominate: there, domain walls separate local minima, which the field tunnels through with a non-vanishing probability.
396
396

396

The wiggling of the field in the late phase of inflation causes GWsthat are a fingerprint of this model that might become observable with the upcoming space-borne GWdetectors
Rompineve Sorbello 2017
.

This gives a discrete set of
n\in\mathbb{Z}
vacua of energy

E\simeq\frac{m^{2}\phi^{2}_{\textnormal{min}}}{2}\simeq\frac{m^{2}n^{2}\left(2\pi f\right)^{2}}{2}\simeq\frac{n^{2}q^{2}g^{2}}{2}.

(489)

For
p=3
domain walls in 4 dimensions, the WGCis
\mathcal{T}\leq gqM_{\textnormal{P}}
.
The tension can also be expressed as
\mathcal{T}=\sqrt{\beta}f
.
To satisfy the electric WGC, the tension has to be small. A small tension indicates that the parameter
\beta
is small, i.e. the wiggles from the cosine term are small, which makes inflation last longer. This means a long-lasting slow-roll phase of inflation is preferred by the electric WGC.
Since there are no
\left(-2\right)
-branes, we can to obtain the magnetic WGCfor domain walls

\Lambda\lesssim\left(qg\right)^{1/3}M_{\textnormal{P}}^{1/3}\simeq\left(2\pi mf\right)^{1/3}M_{\textnormal{P}}^{1/3}

(490)

by analytic continuation of
eq.

536
.
During inflation, the Hubble scale is below the cutoff scale. This helps us to constrain the allowed field range during inflation:

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{V}{3M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}}}

(491)

\displaystyle=\frac{m\phi}{\sqrt{6}M_{\textnormal{P}}}

(492)

\displaystyle\lesssim\Lambda

(493)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{\phi}{M_{\textnormal{P}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{m}\right)^{2/3}\left(\frac{2\pi f}{M_{\textnormal{P}}}\right)^{1/3},

(494)

which rules out parametrically large field ranges, but leaves a comfortable cushion of
\phi/M_{\textnormal{P}}\lesssim${10}^{3}$
, i.e. this form of axion monodromy inflation is an example of large-field inflation that is compatible with the WGC.
397
397

397

An unresolved issue with this model is that instantons will couple to the axions, which does not leave a flat direction in the potential to inflate
Rompineve Sorbello 2017
. See the work by
Hebecker et al. 2015a
for a possible solution to this.

Extranatural Inflation
398
398

398

Natural inflation needs trans-Planckian field excursion, in order to explain the 50 to 60
e
-folds together with the observed bounds on the spectral tilt and the tensor-to-scalar ratio
Rompineve Sorbello 2017
;
van Beest et al. 2022
. Extranatural inflation sacrifices the gauge coupling, which can be very tiny (
\sim${10}^{\order{\num{e-3}}}$
), or the locality of Wilson lines
Furuuchi 2018
;
Furuuchi and Wu 2014
.

​
Arkani-Hamed et al. 2003
is ruled out by the WGC
Saraswat 2017
: The Wilson line
399
399

399

Since a Wilson line is a non-local gauge operator, this non-local interaction is a manifestation of UV/IRmixing in the theory
Lust and Palti 2018
.

from a U(1) gauge field around a circular extra-dimension
S^{1}
of radius
r
gives rise to the inflaton field
\phi
. Since the U(1) is gauge invariant and compact, the potential of the inflaton is of the form
\cos\left(n\phi/f\right)
with
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
f=1/2\pi rg
. In such a setting, inflation lasts for
N_{e}\sim f/M_{\textnormal{P}}

e
-foldings, which requires the gauge coupling
g
to be small enough to explain enough
e
-foldings. However, the magnetic WGCpresents a cutoff
\Lambda<gM_{\textnormal{P}}
, and the size of the compactification manifold has to be larger than
1/\Lambda
. This leads to the following constraint:

1\lesssim 2\pi r\Lambda\lesssim 2\pi rgM_{\textnormal{P}}=M_{\textnormal{P}}/f,

(495)

which rules out single field extranatural inflation, as it requires a super-Planckian decay constant
de la Fuente et al. 2015
;
Heidenreich et al. 2015
;
Huang 2007a
. We see that — while natural inflation is ruled out by the electric WGC — extranatural inflation is constrained by the magnetic WGC
Rompineve Sorbello 2017
.

Chromonatural inflation

with

L=-\mu^{4}\left[1+\cos(\chi/f)\right]-\frac{\lambda}{8f}\chi F^{a}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{F}^{a\mu\nu},

(496)

\mu\lesssim\Lambda_{\text{QG}}
an energy scale,

\chi
an axion,

f
the axion decay constant,

\lambda
the coupling, which could be quantised (
\lambda=n\frac{g^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
),
and
F^{a}_{\mu\nu}
the field strength tensor,
is in tension with the sLWGCcombined with observational constraints: the number of
e
-folds needs to be high enough to explain observations and the bounds on the scalar power spectrum amplitude and spectral tilt need to be respected, which requires the coupling to be small (
g\sim${10}^{-6}$
), but
\mu
to be large, which spoils the sLWGC-requirement
\mu\lesssim\Lambda_{\text{QG}}

Heidenreich et al. 2018b
;
Dimastrogiovanni et al. 2013
;
Adshead and Wyman 2012
;
Adshead et al. 2013a
;
Adshead et al. 2013b
;
Agrawal et al. 2018b
.
Claimed to be compatible with the WGCis
spectator chromonatural inflation
, where the axion field takes a spectator role besides an inflaton field
Holland et al. 2020
;
Bagherian et al. 2023
: Both fields have a sub-Planckian decay constant, where it is important to note that for the axionic gauge field, this is not a decay constant in the usual understanding, as the field is not normalised and therefore not coupled in the usual sense. The coupling is instantaneous, as the coupling is instantly activated when the inflaton field reaches a certain threshold of stabilisation.

Chaotic inflation

Linde 1983

requires trans-Planckian field excursion to inflate enough
Kaloper and Lawrence 2014
;
Madsen and Coles 1988
, which can be realised e.g. by identifying a complex inflaton field with a Wilson line
Ibanez et al. 2015
or membrane emission
Kaloper and Sorbo 2009
, but to be compatible with the scalar WGC, the potentials have to be asymptotically linear
Gonzalo and Ibáñez 2019
.
(Single-field and assisted
Liddle et al. 1998
) eternal chaotic inflation appears to be incompatible with the WGC
Huang et al. 2007
;
Huang 2007b
;
Huang 2008b
.
400
400

400

Huang et al. 2007
present a concrete example with the potential
V(\phi_{i})=\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\phi_{i}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\lambda\phi_{i}^{4}
:
If the field is dominated by the quartic contribution,
H\sim\sqrt{\mathfrak{N}\lambda}\phi^{2}/M_{\textnormal{P}}
, and the WGC — acting as an IRcutoff of the theory — puts a bound of
\mathfrak{N}\lambda\phi^{4}/M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}\leq H^{2}\leq\Lambda^{2}\sim\lambda M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}
, which yields
\phi<M_{\textnormal{P}}/\mathfrak{N}^{1/4}
. As the number of fields
\mathfrak{N}\geq 1
, this is in direct contradiction with our initial finding that for chaotic inflation, trans-Planckian field excursion is necessary to yield enough
e
-foldings while maintaining slow-rolling.
If the field is dominated by the mass term,
H=\sqrt{\mathfrak{N}}m\phi/M_{\textnormal{P}}
; analogue reasoning yields
\phi<M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}/\mathfrak{N}m=\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{\mathfrak{N}}\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{m}
. For eternal inflation to happen,
\mathfrak{N}<M_{\textnormal{P}}/m
.
Huang et al. 2007
claim that this leads to a contradiction with the initial assumption that the field traverses a trans-Planckian range, as
m<M_{\textnormal{P}}
. We don’t see this contradiction, as this just yields
\phi<\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{\mathfrak{N}}\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{m}>\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{M_{\textnormal{P}}/m}\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{m}=M_{\textnormal{P}}
.
The same potential is studied by
Huang 2008b
, but their example might be incompatible with the dSCand would not hold for large
\mathfrak{N}
.

Early work by
Huang 2007b
proposes a lower cutoff bound for the inflaton field of
\Lambda\leq H
, with
H
the Hubble scale, which would not only rule out trans-Planckian field excursion, but even trans-Hubbleian. The bound is derived for polynomial potentials, based on the idea that gravitational contributions to the running of scalar coupling should be less than the contribution of scalar fields themselves, to comply with the WGC(
Huang 2007c
elaborates further on this idea, and
Huang 2007a
makes some notes).

Higgs inflation and Starobinsky inflation

Starobinsky 1980
of the form
V(\phi)=\left(1-\exp(-\sqrt{2/3}\phi/M_\textnormal{P})\right)^{2}
are inconsistent with the scalar WGC(see
section

II.19.2
) in their most simple form — perturbations need to be included
Gonzalo and Ibáñez 2019
. The inconsistency of Starobinsky inflation without the inclusion of perturbations comes from the observation that the gravitational scattering amplitudes are larger than the self-scattering amplitudes of the inflaton at large background field values
Dudas et al. 2023
;
Benakli et al. 2020
;
Gonzalo and Ibáñez 2019
. To realise Starobinsky inflation, two chiral fields are necessary
Ellis et al. 2013
, of which one needs to be stabilised
Dudas et al. 2023
. This, however, does not guarantee that the WGC(or other swampland conjectures) are satisfied. Depending on the concrete realisation of the potential, there might be a tension between the model and the WGC
Dudas et al. 2023
.

Liu 2022
presents the constraints coming from the (strong) scalar WGC(
section

II.19.2
) for Higgs inflation, Higgs–Dilaton inflation, and Palatini Higgs inflation.
401
401

401

The equations are lengthy, and are not used by
Liu 2022
to compare with observational data. Therefore, we won’t reproduce the results here.

Inverse monomial inflation

is a single field model on a brane with a potential of the form
V(\phi)=\mathfrak{c}^{4+\mathfrak{p}}/\phi^{\mathfrak{p}}
that satisfies the (strong) scalar WGC(as well as the combined dSC)
Gashti et al. 2024a
. However, it violates the refined dSCand potentially the DC
Gashti et al. 2024a
.

Running Vacuum Model (Vacuumon)

Mavromatos et al. 2020
;
Basilakos et al. 2020a
;
Basilakos et al. 2020b
;
Basilakos et al. 2019
;
Basilakos et al. 2016
present this model, which depicts the vacuum as a fluid with a scalar field description:
402
402

402

The vacuumon depicts a running vacuum solution and uses a scalar field to represent this. However, the scalar field does not undergo quantum fluctuations and does not generate cosmological perturbations in the standard way of an inflaton field.

The scalar field itself is not slow-rolling during inflation, yet the model allows for an axion field that is slow-rolling. There is no singularity at the beginning of the Universe. The field exits inflation gracefully and enters a radiation-dominated epoch without reheating. The model satisfies the strong scalar WGC(
eq.

523
) as well as the DCand the dSC.
The following equations define the model:

{AmSalign}

ρ
=

˙

ϕ

2

2

+V(
ϕ
)∼
c
_0+
c
_1H^2+
c
_2H^4

p=

˙

ϕ

2

2

-V(
ϕ
)

˙

^2=-2
˙

H

M_P^2

V=3H^2M_P^2(1+

a

6H

2

dH

2

da

)

where we indicated in the first line that the density contains terms proportional to
H^{2}
as well as proportional to
H^{4}
with undefined constants
\mathfrak{c}_{i}
. Due to the
H^{4}
-term, the model shows Starobinsky inflation–like behaviour at early times. At late times, the vacuumon takes the role of dynamic DE.
To test the model for compatibility with the swampland conjectures, the potential can be expressed in terms of the vacuumon
\phi
.
Mavromatos et al. 2020
compare two cases: (a) a generic running vacuum model with relativistic matter, and (b) a string-inspired running vacuum model with stiff gravitational axions.

\displaystyle(a)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}V(\phi)&=V_{0}\frac{2+\cosh^{2}\phi}{\cosh^{4}\phi}\\
V_{0}&=\frac{H_{\textnormal{I}}^{2}}{\lambda}\\
\phi(a)&=\sinh^{-1}\left(\sqrt{\beta}a^{2}\right)\end{cases}

(497)

\displaystyle(b)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}V(\phi)&=V_{0}\frac{\frac{2}{3}+\cosh^{2}\phi}{\cosh^{4}\phi}\\
V_{0}&=\frac{9H_{\textnormal{I}}^{2}}{\lambda}\\
\phi(a)&=\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\sinh^{-1}\left(\sqrt{\beta}a^{3}\right)\end{cases}

(498)

with
\lambda
a parameter that defines the scale of inflation and
\beta
a parameter that defines how quickly the vacuumon transitions between the different phases. These can then be tested for consistency with the swampland conjectures, which
Mavromatos et al. 2020
confirm.

Neutrinos:

The scalar WGCrequires normal mass hierarchy for neutrinos
Gonzalo and Ibáñez 2019
.
Furthermore, the lightest neutrino is Dirac with
m_{\nu_{1}}\lesssim\Lambda_{4}^{1/4}

Gonzalo and Ibáñez 2019
;
Ibanez et al. 2017
.
403
403

403

See
sections

II.12.1
and
II.1.1
for other conjectures that have a preference for Dirac neutrinos.
In the context of the WGC, the mass bound is explained by
Lust and Palti 2018
using their findings about scalar fields: The tower of bound states of the lightest particle is protected from decaying. In the SMthese are neutrinos. Over long distances, neutrinos feel only gravity and can form a bound gravitational state. However, if there is a cosmological constant, which acts as a repulsive force, the force acting on two neutrinos is modified:

F=m_{\nu}\left(-m_{\nu}/r^{2}+\Lambda r/3\right).

To avoid bound states,
\Lambda>m_{\nu}^{4}
is required.

It’s either a lower bound on the cosmological constant
\Lambda_{\textnormal{cc}}
or an upper bound on the neutrino mass (of the lightest neutrino)
m_{\nu_{1}}
. The latter indicates the scale of EWsymmetry breaking and gives therefore the mass of the Higgs boson
Lust and Palti 2018
.

Adopting a ruled-out form of the WGC, namely that the WGCparticle has to be the lightest particle in the theory,
Das et al. 2020b
derive a lower bound on the electric charge of neutrinos of
q_{\nu}\gtrsim${10}^{-28}$\frac{m_{\nu}q_{e}}{$0.1\text{\,}\mathrm{eV}$}
.
Since the lightest particle does not need to be the WGC-particle, nor does the WGConly apply to the electric charge, this bound does not hold. Neutrinos are likely electrically neutral
Abu-Ajamieh et al. 2024a
.

Photons

Observations put stringent bounds on the mass of (SM) photons
Goldhaber and Nieto 2010
;
Wu et al. 2016
;
Bonetti et al. 2016
;
Bonetti et al. 2017
.
The WGCindicates that SMphotons are massless
Reece 2019
.
If the photon gained a mass through a mechanism (other than Higgsing), the string scale would be
M_{\textnormal{s}}\lesssim 2\pi\sqrt{m_{\gamma}M_{\textnormal{P}}/e}
, which would mean that stringy effects should be visible at the LHC, unless the elementary charge is actually not the electron charge
e
, but a small quantity, which would change the denominator to
e/N

Draper et al. 2022
;
Craig and Garcia 2018
.

Craig and Garcia 2018
propose that the arguments used by
Reece 2019
in favour of massless photons could be parametrically violated in the IR, allowing massive photons to be part of EFTs.

Beside a massless SMphoton, there could be an additional massive dark photon.
A dark photon is a (ultra-light) DMcandidate that gains mass through Higgsing or by eating an axion (Stückelberg photon)
Goodsell et al. 2009
;
Cline 2024
, and is studied for a range of phenomenological implications:
dark massive photons could alter the CMB
Pospelov et al. 2018
,
heat the early Universe
Kovetz et al. 2019
,
and act as an extra radio background
Montero et al. 2022
;
Garcia et al. 2020
;
Caputo et al. 2020
.
Dark photons could be produced by quantum fluctuations during inflation in the form of massive vector bosons with a mass of
m_{\gamma}\approx${10}^{-5}\text{\,}\mathrm{eV}$\left(${10}^{14}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$/H_{\textnormal{I}}\right)^{4}
, with
H_{\textnormal{I}}
the Hubble parameter during inflation
Graham et al. 2016
;
Craig and Garcia 2018
. The proposed mechanism forbids the presence of scalar fields with
m_{\phi}<H_{\textnormal{I}}
, as otherwise isocurvature perturbations would occur that are incompatible with observations
Craig and Garcia 2018
;
Graham et al. 2016
;
Fox et al. 2004
.
This constraint together with the TWGCtranslates into a mass bound for dark photons,

\displaystyle H_{\textnormal{I}}\lesssim\Lambda\lesssim g^{1/3}M_{\textnormal{P}}

\displaystyle\sim\left(\frac{m_{\gamma}}{H_{\textnormal{I}}}\right)^{1/3}M_{\textnormal{P}}

(499)

\displaystyle m_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\gtrsim$10\text{\,}\mathrm{eV}$

(500)

that can be lowered in a broken clockwork construction,

\displaystyle H_{\textnormal{I}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{g^{n}m_{\gamma}}{H_{\textnormal{I}}}\right)^{1/3}M_{\textnormal{P}}

(501)

\displaystyle m_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{$10\text{\,}\mathrm{eV}$}{q^{n/2}},

(502)

which would allow for smaller dark photons masses, depending on the parameters
q
and
n
(
g\sim q^{n}m_{\gamma}/H_{\textnormal{I}}
)
Craig and Garcia 2018
.
Another lower mass bound on dark photons is derived from positivity bounds on the scattering amplitude: a dark photon must have a mass larger than its U(1) gauge coupling, as otherwise the EFTbreaks down in already observable energy regimes
Noumi et al. 2023
. Furthermore, there is a lower bound on the cross-section for non-gravitational interactions between dark sector particles
X
and SMphotons
\gamma
of
\sigma_{\gamma-X}^{\textnormal{ng}}\gtrsim\order{\left(qg\Lambda/M_\textnormal{P}m_e\right)^2}

Noumi et al. 2023
.

Quarks

Under the caveat that holography is applied in a regime of strong gravity,
McInnes 2022
presents a peculiar finding, which might indicate that quark matter
cannot
exist, not even in the core of neutron stars, as he derives a lower bound on density:

\rho_{\text{enth}}(T=0)>\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{4\left(32\pi N_{\text{c}}^{4}\right)^{1/6}k_{5}l_{5}^{5}}\left(\frac{m}{q}\right)_{\text{min}},

(503)

with
\rho_{\text{enth}}
the enthalpy density,

N_{\text{c}}
the number of colors characterising the boundary field,

k_{5}
controlling the relation between the enthalpy density and the quark chemical potential of cold quark matter, and

l_{5}
the AdS
5
length scale.
We won’t investigate this idea here any further, but leave it here as a source of inspiration for future work.

II.19.2
General Remarks

The WGCdoes not only demand that gravity is the
weakest
force
Brennan et al. 2018
;
Palti 2017
, it is also a
weak
conjecture itself, as there might be a cutoff scale below
\Lambda
, after which the theory changes in character such that the WGCdoes not apply
Palti 2019
. Nevertheless, the WGCis a
‘prime example of a swampland conjecture […] [with] a broad consensus that it is actually a correct statement’

Cicoli et al. 2023a
. In the following, we would like to gain some intuition by addressing various questions.

Why should we not believe the WGC?

There are no proofs.
404
404

404

This statement has been repeated in the literature over and over again for the past few years. However, more recently
Heidenreich and Lotito 2024
proofed the sLWGC(and therefore also the TWGCor any milder form, such as the general WGC) for bosonic strings in
d\geq 6
dimensions. This is still no fully general proof, but one step towards it.

The initial motivation for the WGCwas the assumption that ((near-)extremal) BHsshould be able to decay. There is no observation yet that proofs that this is actually the case. Furthermore, in AdS/CFT
405
405

405

Even though the WGCis studied in CFTcontext, it is unclear if a counterexample from CFTruled out the WGC
Montero et al. 2016
: On the one hand, weakly coupled gauge fields and BHsare present in three spacetime dimensions, on the other hand, gravity works differently in three spacetime dimensions than in higher-dimensional spacetimes, such that the WGCmight not be applicable to CFTs, or maybe only to a certain subclass of CFTs.

this is a statement about BHswith a radius smaller than the AdSradius, which are not well understood
Harlow et al. 2023
. There are entropic arguments that also large near-extremal BHsneed to be unstable
Montero 2019
, but this could be satisfied by un-charged BHdecays and is therefore no proof of the WGCeither. However, for large BHs, a charged decay channel is possible, which would support the WGC.

Another argument against the WGCis the absence of magnetic monopoles: The minimally charged magnetic monopole in the theory should have a mass
m_{\text{m}}\sim\Lambda/g^{2}
and a size of
r_{\text{m}}\sim 1/\Lambda

Arkani-Hamed et al. 2007b
. BHsare classical concepts, and therefore the charges should be macroscopic and the minimally charged magnetic monopole should not be a BH, i.e.
1\gtrsim m_{\text{m}}/M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}r_{\text{m}}
, which yields the magnetic WGC
Arkani-Hamed et al. 2007b
. But, we haven’t observed magnetic monopoles yet, maybe because they actually are BHsand the WGCdoes not hold?

Despite the strong evidence from string theory, the WGCmight simply suffer from a lamppost effect: most known examples from string theory involve either weak coupling or BPSstates — no non-supersymmetric example of strong coupling is known
Harlow et al. 2023
.

Which observations would rule out the WGC?

The detection of a super-light
B-L
gauge boson in neutron–antineutron scattering experiments
Addazi 2018
.

Furthermore, if we observed features that are predicted by inflationary models that were deemed incompatible with the WGC, the WGCwould be challenged.

The discovery of a very light magnetic monopole would mean that either the cutoff of the EFTis very low or that the coupling is strong. Both would be surprising and might lead to incompatibilities with the SM(but precise bounds would have to be checked).

If the strong correspondence between the WGCand the CCCholds, the observation of naked singularities would pose an issue for both conjectures.

What speaks against stable remnants?

Stable BHremnants correspond to global charges, which contradicts the no global symmetries conjecture (
section

II.10
) as well as the cobordism conjecture (
section

II.2
).
One of the issues with a large number of stable remnants is that they contribute to the mean energy density with
\rho(m,g)\exp(-m/T)
, with
T
the temperature of the thermal bath, such that the integrated contribution diverges, which renders the EFTpathological
Montero et al. 2016
.
406
406

406

In other words, the energy density a uniformly accelerated observer observes would diverge due to the Unruh effect caused by the infinitely many BHstates we can build at arbitrarily low energy
Montero et al. 2017
.

The early Universe had a radiation dominated epoch between inflation and BBN, with an EoSof
\omega\approx 1/3
.
If PBHswere formed during this time, their EoSwas
\omega=0
.
In order not to spoil BBN-predictions, most PBHsmust evaporate into massless degrees of freedom
Solomon and Stojkovic 2020
.
If they form stable remnants, i.e. evaporation stops at the extremal limit, the EoSof the universe does not reach
\omega=1/3

Solomon and Stojkovic 2020
or PBHsoverclose the Universe
Dai et al. 2009
.

Another argument disfavouring stable remnants comes from the CEB:

If
there is no charge quantisation nor mass quantisation, there is an infinite spectrum of degrees of freedom, which violates the CEB
Banks and Seiberg 2011
, i.e. the entropy is infinite in a finite volume
Dai et al. 2009
.
We italicised the
‘if’
because charge quantisation limits the number of degrees of freedom to a large but finite value. Furthermore, the number of stable remnants within a given finite volume would still be finite.

A large but finite number of remnants per se seems not to be a problem, so it is not undisputed that the WGCeven is a meaningful constraint, as only the vanishing coupling limit
g\rightarrow 0
with an infinite number of remnants poses an actual problem
Montero 2019
. Since in explicit examples the number of BHremnants might be large but finite, the tower of states that emerges is finite and the states are labelled by distinct charges and are therefore in principle distinguishable
Cottrell et al. 2017
;
Cheung et al. 2018
.
In particular, when charge quantisation is considered, the density of states remains finite
Saraswat 2017
.
Once more, we’d like to stress that the swampland programme makes statements about the boundary of theory space, cosmology happens in the bulk and might circumvent certain conjectures. Nevertheless, the remnants argument can provide us with some bottom-up intuition about what can go wrong when an EFTviolates the WGC
van Beest et al. 2022
.

Is there no proof of the WGC?

There is an actual string-theoretical proof presented by
Heidenreich and Lotito 2024
. However, the proof only holds for bosonic string theories in six or more dimensions. In less than six dimensions, other objects such as D-branes or solitons might be part of the charge spectrum. Furthermore, string loop corrections might spoil the bound.
Besides this proof, considerations from BHthermodynamics are used to derive the WGCbound for specific systems:

For the case of a Kerr–Newman BH, described by a RNmetric, and a massive scalar field,

Hod 2017
starts from Bekenstein’s generalised second law of thermodynamics (
S_{\text{BH}}=k_{B}c^{3}A/4G\hbar
,
A
the BHsurface area)
Hawking 1975
;
Bekenstein 1973
;
Bekenstein 1974

to derive a lower bound on the relaxation time of perturbed physical systems, the universal relaxation bound
Tt_{\text{relax}}\geq\hbar/\pi
,
T
the temperature
Gruzinov 2007
;
Hod 2007a
;
Hod 2008
;
Hod 2007b
;
Mezerji and Sadeghi 2022
, which, as he shows, implies the WGC.

A similar approach is taken by
Fisher and Mogni 2017
, who also start from the generalised second law of thermodynamics: They show that in theories where the WGCis violated, BHscan grow unbounded. Furthermore, they show that larger BHscontain fewer microscopic states, as decreasing quantum corrections outcompete an increasing Bekenstein–Hawking term. As there is no bound in size, very large BHswill eventually reach negative entropy states, i.e. the BHsitself contains less than 1 microscopic state. It is expected that unitarity forbids this from happening. However, unitarity of the S-matrix alone is insufficient to assure that the WGCholds
Loges et al. 2020a
.
407
407

407

Large RNBHsare also violating Lloyd’s
Lloyd 2000
quantum information theory bound on computational speed
Cottrell and Montero 2018
.

A result in agreement with the WGC, but stronger in nature, is derived by
Yu and Wen 2018
when studying the second law of BHthermodynamics: to protect cosmic censorship, a lower bound on the mass-to-charge ratio exists for
all
massive charged particles.

Assuming that BHdo not leave behind stable remnants, and applying the Pauli exclusion principle,
Baddis et al. 2024
derive a statistical bound on the BHcharge-to-mass ratio that strongly resembles the WGC:

\frac{Q^{2}}{M^{2}}>\frac{gmT}{T_{\textnormal{P}}M_{\textnormal{P}}^{3}},

(504)

with
T_{\textnormal{P}}
the Planck-temperature. This bound assures that the formation of stable remnants is suppressed.

Not a proof, but observational evidence from astrophysics is presented by
Gasperini 2006
:
Ranging from planets to superclusters, spanning 25 orders of magnitude, a relation between the mass
m
of an object and its angular momentum
J
appears to hold quite well:

J=\lambda m^{\mathfrak{p}},

(505)

where
\mathfrak{p}=2
can be set as a scale free choice if

1/\lambda=\alpha c/G_{\textnormal{N}},

(506)

with
\alpha
the fine-structure constant,
c
the speed of light, and
G_{\textnormal{N}}
Newton’s gravitational constant
Brosche 1963
;
Wesson 1979
;
Wesson 1981
;
Carrasco et al. 1982
.
A similar relation is found regarding the charge
Sirag 1979
;
Surdin 1977
;
Arge et al. 1995
;
Baliunas et al. 1996
;
Vasiliev 2000
:

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=qm

(507)

\displaystyle 1/q^{2}

\displaystyle=\alpha G_{\textnormal{N}}/c^{2}.

(508)

By using
\alpha=e^{2}/\hbar c
,
\hbar=c=1
in natural units, where
1/\sqrt{G_{\textnormal{N}}}=M_{\textnormal{P}}
, we find that
\lambda
respectively
q
give rise to energy scales that are familiar from the WGC:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\sim\Lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\alpha}M_{\textnormal{P}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle eM_{\textnormal{P}}

(509)

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\sim\Lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{\sqrt{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{\textnormal{m}}M_{\textnormal{P}}.

(510)

The WGCfollows as an empirical fit to astrophysical data.

What are the implications of the WGCfor EFTs?

The magnetic WGC(
eq.

461
) limits the range of validity of an EFT: If the coupling is weak, the cutoff scale of the WGCis low. At higher energy scales, new physics appears
Heidenreich et al. 2018b
;
Harlow et al. 2023
.
Craig et al. 2019
give an example:
Assuming the U(1) described by the EFTis an IRremnant from a Higgsed SU(2); the scale
\Lambda
will then correspond to the mass scale of a massive W boson, which appears for energies above
\Lambda
(and below the Planck scale).

Abu-Ajamieh et al. 2024b
apply the scalar WGC(
eq.

516
) to the only known scalar field in our Universe, the Higgs field, and derive bounds on the scale where new physics is expected to appear:

•

If the lightest neutrino is Majorana, the lightest fermion is the electron, and
\Lambda\lesssim$1.2\text{\times}{10}^{13}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
.

•

If the lightest neutrino is Dirac with
m_{\nu_{e}}=$1.1\text{\,}\mathrm{eV}$
,
\Lambda\lesssim$2.6\text{\times}{10}^{7}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
.

•

If the WGCparticle is the Higgs itself, the scalar WGCpredicts that new physics arise before the Higgs field suffers the instability from the quartic coupling
Degrassi et al. 2012
;
Hook et al. 2015
;
Elias-Miro et al. 2012
around
\sim${10}^{10}\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
.

It is currently debated whether the WGCis even applicable to EFTsor only to QG. On the one hand,

Furuuchi 2018
shows that if one starts with an EFTthat satisfies the WGC, spontaneous gauge symmetry breaking cannot lead to a violation of the WGC.
On the other hand,

Saraswat 2017
shows that Higgsing a WGC-compatible theory of QGcan lead to a WGC-violating EFT.
408
408

408

For example, magnetic monopoles are evaded when the magnetic confinement in the Higgs phase is taken into account
Saraswat 2017
. In the Higgs phase, states of different charges can mix, which destroys the commutation of charge and mass, i.e. the WGCis ill-defined
Cheung and Remmen 2014b
. The reason for the low-energy violation of the WGCmight lie in non-minimal field content in the high-energy regime
Saraswat 2017
.

However, this EFTsatisfies the much weaker bound
\Lambda_{\text{EFT}}\lesssim M_{\textnormal{P}}/\sqrt{-\log g}
.
This bound is motivated by entropy bounds
Saraswat 2017
and ambiguous in Abelian theories, as the gauge coupling can be normalised — proposing that the coupling of the particle with the weakest charge shall be taken still leaves some wiggle room: the particle of smallest charge could be above the EFTcutoff
Furuuchi 2018
;
Saraswat 2017
.
409
409

409

This loophole is avoided if one requires that the charge remains sub-Planckian
Lust and Palti 2018
;
Harlow 2016
or by demanding that the slightly stronger
effective WGC
holds: the particle that satisfies the WGChas to be part of the EFT
Heidenreich et al. 2015
.

In non-Abelian theories, the gauge field itself is charged and can be used to normalise the gauge coupling constant
Furuuchi 2018
.
This weaker bound, however, is also questioned, as a complete breakdown of the EFTdescription is expected when an infinite tower of particles appears, which happens at
\Lambda\sim g^{1/3}M_{\textnormal{P}}

Craig et al. 2019
;
Heidenreich et al. 2017
;
Heidenreich et al. 2018b
.

Can the WGCbe understood in terms of classical physics?

Equation

460
can be easily understood in a usual Einstein–Maxwell theory with charged particles as the condition
F_{\textnormal{EM}}\geq F_{\textnormal{G}}
for the particle species with the largest charge-to-mass ratio, with

\displaystyle F_{\textnormal{G}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}}{8\pi M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}r^{2}}

(511)

\displaystyle F_{\textnormal{EM}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(gq)^{2}}{4\pi r^{2}},

(512)

r
the distance between two such particles. If
F_{\textnormal{EM}}<F_{\textnormal{G}}
, two such particles would attract each other and form a bound state with
Q=2q
but
M<2m
, such that
Q/M>q/m
, a violation of the assumption that the initial particle was the one with the highest charge-to-mass ratio
Palti 2019
. As charge and energy are both conserved, this is a stable state. Adding up more of those particles, since they are mutually attractive (
F_{\textnormal{EM}}<F_{\textnormal{G}}
), leads to stable bound states with arbitrarily large charge.

What are the implications for our understanding of spacetime?

In strongly curved (higher dimensional) backgrounds, the WGCcan be regarded as a constraint on the local scalar curvature, expressed through the Ricci scalar
R
:

\sqrt{R}<gM_{\textnormal{P}},

(513)

as the local curvature has to be smaller than the coupling in order to have an effectively four-dimensional theory
Klaewer and Palti 2017
.

In
section

II.12
, we discuss the stability of AdS, Minkowski, and dSvacua.
Liu et al. 2024
show that dSvacua are unstable if a charged membrane is part of the EFT, and that Minkowski vacua are unstable if a WGC-satisfying membrane exists. This is in analogy to the stability of BHs, which decay when a WGC-satisfying particle exists.

The generalisation of the WGCto AdSis not straightforward
Nakayama and Nomura 2015
: First, in AdS, there is no no-hair theorem for BHs, which would allow them to shed charges in processes that do not exist in Minkowski spacetime. Second, the Gedankenexperiment of the infinitely growing BHby adding up more and more particles (when the WGCis violated), doesn’t apply to AdSbecause the BHwill at one point be larger than the AdSscale. And third, the idea of Hawking radiation is based on the finite temperature of a BHfrom the Unruh effect as seen by an observer at infinity. AdSis not infinite, ergo, it’s not a given that the critical WGCbound can be reached. Since refinements to the WGCin AdStend to approach the conditions from Minkowski space for large AdSscales, and our Universe is observed to be relatively flat, i.e. the AdSscale is very large, we refer the interested reader to the literature on holography and CFTslisted in
section

II.19.3
for further studies.

How are the magnetic and the electric WGCrelated to each other?

Palti 2019
, referring to
Arkani-Hamed et al. 2007b
, shows how
eq.

460
and
eq.

461
are related:
Equation

460
, applied to the magnetic dual field, gives a constraint on the mass of a magnetic monopole:

m_{\text{mag}}\leq g_{\text{mag}}M_{\textnormal{P}}\sim\frac{M_{\textnormal{P}}}{g_{\text{el}}},

(514)

which can serve as a probe of the gauge theory
Arkani-Hamed et al. 2007b
.

The monopole field has a cutoff at the scale
\Lambda
, as its field is linearly divergent. Therefore, the mass of the monopole is of the order of the field energy, i.e.

m_{\text{mag}}\sim\frac{\Lambda}{g_{\text{el}}^{2}}.

(515)

Equation

461
follows right away.

Does the WGCapply to scalar fields?

The inclusion of massless scalar fields modifies the WGCregarding two independent aspects
Lee et al. 2019b
: It changes the extremality condition for charged BHs
410
410

410

The charge-to-mass ratio of an extremal BHlooks different, if the gauge coupling has a moduli dependence
Lee et al. 2019b
.

, and Yukawa interactions between the WGC-satisfying particles have to be included.
411
411

411

The BHmust be able to decay into particles that do not form gravitationally bound states, ergo, the gauge force must be strong enough to not only overcome the gravitational attraction, but also a possibly attractive Yukawa potential
Lee et al. 2019b
.

Interestingly, for a tower of asymptotically massless charged particles, as predicted by the DC, the two modifications are actually equivalent
Lee et al. 2019b
.
412
412

412

For BPSstates, this is assured by supersymmetry; for non-supersymmetric theories, it represents the finding that highly excited string states can approach extremal BHsasymptotically
Lee et al. 2019b
;
Sen 1995
.

There are various proposals for the exact quantitative changes of the WGCbound. The most widely discussed ones are the
repulsive force conjecture
(which we discuss in
section

II.19.4
) and the
strong scalar WGC
(which is presented by
Gonzalo and Ibáñez 2019
and generalised by
Kusenko et al. 2020
;
Gonzalo and Ibáñez 2020
).
To cover some preliminaries, we start with a weaker form, the
scalar WGC
.

Scalar WGC

If the scalar field has a potential
413
413

413

The potential cannot have quartic terms
Gonzalo and Ibáñez 2020
;
Gonzalo and Ibáñez 2019
.

but is massless, there is a particle that satisfies the strict inequality

\absolutevalue{m^\prime}^{2}=g^{ij}\left(\partial_{i}m\right)\left(\partial_{j}m\right)>m^{2}/M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2},

(516)

which is related to the DC
414
414

414

If the field is canonically normalised, i.e.
g^{ij}=\delta^{ij}
, then
m_{\phi}\sim e^{\phi}

Grimm et al. 2018
.
See
section

II.5
for further details.

Palti 2019
;
Palti 2017
, but does not consider repulsive interactions
Benakli et al. 2020
.

Equation

516
says that, regarding massless scalar fields,
2\rightarrow 2
–scattering interactions are stronger than gravity
Shirai and Yamazaki 2019
.
415
415

415

The scalar force
F_{\text{scalar}}=\absolutevalue{m^\prime}^{2}/4\pi r^{2}
is always stronger than the gravitational force
F_{\text{gravity}}=m^{2}/8\pi M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}r^{2}

Shirai and Yamazaki 2019
.

This form of the scalar WGCseems to predict a fifth force, which is already excluded on observational grounds to
\absolutevalue{m^\prime}^{2}<${10}^{\order{\num{e-5}}}$\left(m^{2}/M_{\textnormal{P}}^{2}\right)

Shirai and Yamazaki 2019
;
Esposito-Farese 2004
;
Will 2006
;
Bertotti et al. 2003
.
416
416

416

Experimental data suggests that a fifth force is no more than
{10}^{-6}{10}^{-3}
times as strong as gravity over a range of
1\text{\,}\mathrm{m}{10}^{4}\text{\,}\mathrm{m}

Will 2006
.
However, there could be screening mechanisms at work like the chameleon mechanism
Khoury and Weltman 2004
;
Hinterbichler et al. 2011
or a disformal coupling via the Lagrangian, which leads to a geodesic equation that contains a four-force that depends on the four-velocity, i.e. the force could be screened in the solar system, where the velocity of matter is non-relativistic
Boehmer et al. 2015
.
Furthermore, a screening of conformal effects by disformal coupling contributions is discussed by
van de Bruck and Morrice 2015
.

Etheredge et al. 2022
voice some doubts about this form of the scalar WGC, as it fails to deliver a compelling argument
why
the gravitational force should be weaker than the attractive force mediated by massless scalar fields. They claim that only the following bound holds for massless scalar fields:

\frac{\left(\partial_{\phi}m\right)^{2}}{m^{2}}\geq\frac{g_{\phi\phi}}{d-2},

(517)

where the
\phi\phi
component of the moduli space metric
g_{\phi\phi}
appears, for instance, in the kinetic term
\frac{1}{2}g_{\phi\phi}\mathrm{d}\phi\wedge\star\mathrm{d}\phi
for
\phi
in the action.

If a small positive cosmological constant is part of the theory,
Antoniadis and Benakli 2020
propose some corrections to the WGC:

\displaystyle qg

\displaystyle>\sqrt{4\pi G}\left(1+\left(\frac{Gm}{2l}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle l\rightarrow\infty

(518)

\displaystyle qg

\displaystyle>\left(\frac{32\pi^{2}}{3}\right)^{1/4}\sqrt{lm}

\displaystyle l\rightarrow 0

(519)

with
l\rightarrow\infty
being the low curvature limit and
l\rightarrow 0
the strong curvature limit.
Note that
M_{\textnormal{P}}=1/\sqrt{8\pi G}
.

A generalisation to massive scalar fields (that is valid around the minimum of the potential of a scalar field) is presented by

Benakli et al. 2020
for several popular potentials:

4m_{0}^{2}\absolutevalue{\frac{\partial^4V}{\partial^2\phi\partial^2\phi^*}}_{\phi=0}\geq\frac{\tilde{c}}{M_{\textnormal{P}}}\absolutevalue{\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial\phi\partial\phi^*}}^{2}_{\phi=0},

(520)

with
\tilde{c}\approx 1
.
The constraints are summarised in
table

Benakli et al. 2020
